Investigation: what's actually stalling past the loading screen

Goal: find why the game never visibly progresses past the loading screen, now that
the file-I/O + APC + spinlock + sprintf chain (Phase 3H-3J) is fixed and the game
runs a real, continuous per-frame loop.

--- Finding 1: the "healthy 2 calls/sec" measurement was wrong ---

Initial call-rate instrumentation on sub_8212EC78 (the queue-poll wrapper fixed in
Phase 3H) mislabeled sparse activity as "~1s" cadence. Fixed to report real elapsed
time. Corrected result: sub_8212EC78 is called a total of TWO times, ever, in the
entire post-file-load execution -- not a steady rate. A 4-minute patience run (real
wall-clock, watchdog temporarily raised) confirmed zero further kernel-visible
activity for the remaining ~235 seconds, while CPU stayed pegged at 100%+. This is
a second, genuinely different stall from anything fixed in Phase 3H-3J -- the queue
system itself is not looping; something else entirely is.

--- Finding 2: real call-stack structure of the stuck state ---

Repeated lldb sampling of the active thread found a stable 11-deep call chain that
persists for minutes, with the leaf function varying (real, ongoing execution, not
a frozen PC):

  __xstart -> sub_825DEC18 -> sub_8212B130 -> sub_8244B040 -> sub_8244AFB0 ->
  sub_8244AE70 -> sub_82461D28 -> sub_825E8D28 -> sub_82446D50 -> sub_8212EC78 ->
  sub_8212EAF0 -> [varies: sub_8212EA80 / sub_82125DF8 / mutex lock]

Read each function's real generated code (private/ppc/*.cpp) to find the actual
loop. Confirmed NOT loops (all-forward-branch dispatchers or straight-line code):
  - sub_825DEC18: calls sub_8212B130 exactly once, then a permanent `b $` trap --
    this is the correct, expected "title entry point should never return" pattern,
    not a bug.
  - sub_8244B040: large (~1080 line) dispatcher, all branches forward, no loop.
  - sub_825E8D28: jump-table switch dispatcher, all branches forward, no loop.
  - sub_82446D50: one small, correctly-bounded input-state bit-packing loop
    (iterates a real count field, terminates normally), otherwise straight-line.

Confirmed REAL loops, both apparently correctly bounded:
  - sub_82461D28 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.59.cpp:25072-25323): iterates a real
    item list, count reloaded from memory each iteration, dispatches each item via
    an indirect (vtable-style) function call. Live-instrumented: count was a sane
    3, and the loop-back check was hit only once in the whole run -- meaning
    item[0]'s own indirect call is what doesn't return, not the loop itself.
  - sub_8244AE70 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.57.cpp:1801-1831): a tight retry loop --
    call sub_8212EC78(), call sub_82128EB0(fixedAddr), check the low 16 bits of
    the result, loop again if zero. This is the most likely site of the real
    stall: a classic "wait for condition via repeated polling" shape, the same
    class of bug found and fixed in Phase 3H.

--- Finding 3: sub_82128EB0 / sub_82128DB0 lead into real ambiguity ---

sub_82128EB0 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:1590) branches on a global flag at
[-32158 base + 10104]:
  - flag == 0 (the branch taken on essentially every call, per live evidence):
    treats its first argument (a fixed address computed by the caller, not
    obviously a function pointer at the source level) as a function pointer and
    calls it directly via mtctr/bctrl, then unconditionally returns 1 (success).
  - flag != 0: calls sub_82128DB0 for real queue submission against the same
    0x82670000+5632 structure central to Phase 3H-3G's earlier investigation
    (checks field4 against a cap of 128, submits into a slot, decrements/increments
    bookkeeping, wakes via sub_82128D08 if field12 is set).

Live instrumentation at sub_82128DB0's field4 check found: field4=0 (queue not
full -- my initial "queue full forever" hypothesis was wrong), and this check
point was reached only ONCE in the entire run. This means the flag==0 branch (the
indirect bctrl call to a caller-supplied address) is the path actually being taken
on essentially every retry iteration -- and that indirect call is the most likely
real hang: either the target address doesn't point to code that returns quickly,
or (an open possibility, not confirmed) the address is genuinely data being
mis-treated as code, which would itself be a real bug matching the "call an
address as if it always contains valid trampoline code" pattern already seen once
this session in a different function.

--- Finding 4: the fixed-address hypothesis was traced and then refuted ---

Traced the exact constant sub_8244AE70 passes to sub_82128EB0: `-32187 base
(0x82450000) - 20936 (0x51C8) = 0x8244AE38`. Confirmed this address IS a real,
mapped function (`sub_8244AE38` appears in private/ppc/ppc_func_mapping.cpp) --
not an unmapped/null indirect-call target as initially suspected. Read it: short,
straight-line, calls sub_82495908 once, always returns 1. Read sub_82495908: a
larger function containing a real circular-linked-list traversal (walk via a
"next" pointer at offset 20 until back to a saved sentinel node) -- structurally
the same shape as the XDVDFS BST bug fixed earlier this project, a plausible
timer/callback-list walk.

Instrumented that list-traversal loop's iteration count live: zero hits in a
15-second run -- the loop is never entered at all.

Went further and used lldb breakpoints directly (attach to an already-running
process, breakpoint on `__imp__sub_8244AE38`, continue) rather than continuing to
reason from static code alone. Result across a full 60-second real run: the
breakpoint never triggers. This is a decisive, empirical negative result --
sub_8244AE38 is never actually called during real execution, which means the
flag-==0 branch of sub_82128EB0 (the bctrl-to-caller-address path) is NOT the one
being taken on the hot retry path, contradicting the single live sample that
suggested otherwise. My reconstructed call chain from sub_8244AE70 down through
this specific branch does not reflect what actually happens at runtime.

--- Finding 5: symbol breakpoints are unreliable under this build's -O3, and the
    "retry loop" hypothesis for sub_8244AE70 is fully refuted ---

Tried the planned next step (breakpoints on `__imp__sub_82128DB0` and
`__imp__sub_8212EC78` directly) and got zero hits across a full 60-second real
run on both -- despite independently confirmed evidence (kernel log output,
earlier source-level instrumentation) that these functions DO execute. Root
cause: this project builds Release / -O3 (confirmed in build/CMakeCache.txt).
Small, hot, directly-called (`bl`-style) functions like these are prime inlining
candidates, and an inlined function has no distinct call boundary left for a
symbol breakpoint to catch. This does NOT invalidate the earlier sub_8244AE38
negative result, though -- that address is only ever reached via an indirect
`bctrl` (PPC_CALL_INDIRECT_FUNC), and indirect/computed call targets cannot be
inlined by the compiler, so that breakpoint result stands.

Given breakpoints are unreliable here, went back to the technique proven
reliable all session: instrumenting the function body directly (inlining-proof,
since the print statement travels with the code wherever it ends up). Added a
watch at the exact point inside sub_82128EB0 that reads the branch-selecting
flag and (for the flag==0 case) the bctrl target, printing both on every real
entry to the function.

Result (full run, real evidence): sub_82128EB0 is entered exactly ONCE, ever,
with flag=1 (not 0) -- meaning it takes the sub_82128DB0 branch, not the bctrl
branch, on its one and only real call. This directly means the "loc_8244AF60"
loop inside sub_8244AE70 (call sub_8212EC78, call sub_82128EB0, loop if the
result's low 16 bits are zero) is NOT a tight retry loop stuck spinning -- it
runs through once and, since sub_82128DB0's real submission succeeded (field4=0,
not full, per earlier evidence), should return a non-zero result and exit
normally. The entire "sub_8244AE70 is the hang site" hypothesis, built from
several real, confirmed sub-findings, is refuted by this final, decisive,
inlining-proof measurement. Whatever the real 4-minute stall is, it is not this
loop.

--- Status: investigation paused here, not resolved ---

This pass ends with several confirmed real facts and one large invalidated
hypothesis -- genuine progress in the sense of eliminating a fully-traced,
plausible-looking dead end with hard evidence, not a rushed guess. What's
confirmed, real, and should be trusted by a future pass:
  - sub_8212EC78 (Phase 3H's queue-poll) is not looping; it and sub_82128EB0/
    sub_82128DB0 execute only a handful of times total, not repeatedly.
  - The 11-deep call chain traced via lldb sampling is real and does represent
    genuinely-executing code at the moments it was sampled -- but samples show
    WHERE code was running at an instant, not HOW OFTEN a given path repeats.
    Confusing the two is exactly the mistake this pass made and then corrected.
  - Symbol breakpoints on directly-called (bl-style) functions are unreliable in
    this -O3 build; only indirect/bctrl call targets are safe breakpoint anchors.
    Source-level printf-style instrumentation remains the reliable technique.
  - CPU stays pegged at 100%+ for the full multi-minute duration with no further
    kernel-visible activity, meaning real, ongoing computation IS happening
    somewhere -- the lldb samples showing varied leaf functions across
    sub_8212EA80/sub_82125DF8/mutex-lock territory were real, just not proof of
    which specific loop is responsible, since sub_8212EC78's own call count is
    now confirmed low.

--- Finding 6: ROOT MECHANISM FOUND -- sub_8212EC78's own inner loop is the
    real hang, and its exact stuck state is now confirmed with hard numbers ---

Instrumented sub_8212EAF0's call rate directly (elapsed-time-corrected, not the
earlier flawed labeling): confirmed ~9.6 MILLION calls per second, sustained for
the entire multi-minute hang. This is unambiguous: a genuine tight busy-spin,
not slow legitimate work.

Traced the caller: this rate exactly matches sub_8212EC78's OWN internal loop
(read fully back in an earlier phase) -- `call sub_82128F58(); if it returns 0,
loop: call sub_8212EAF0(8); check field12; if field12!=0, call sub_82128F58()
again; loop again if it returns 0`. Instrumented that inner loop directly:
confirmed `field4=1` and `field12=1`, STUCK at those exact values, unchanging,
for the entire run (verified samples at hit=1 through hit=20,000,000+).
sub_82128F58() keeps returning 0 (not-clear) every single time. This is the
literal, complete mechanism of the hang: sub_8212EC78 never returns because the
queue never reports clear, so it spins its own internal poll loop forever,
burning the CPU via sub_8212EAF0/sub_82128F58 millions of times a second. This
directly explains every earlier observation in this investigation: sub_8212EC78's
own OUTER call count stays low (2 total, matching Finding 1) because it is
STUCK inside one of those two calls, never returning to be called again; the
9.6M/s sub_8212EAF0 rate; the 100%+ pegged CPU; the complete absence of further
kernel-visible activity (nothing else runs because this thread never leaves
the loop).

This is the exact same field4/field12 mechanism investigated and fixed for the
loading-screen texture in Phase 3H -- but a DIFFERENT, later occurrence of it.
Instrumented NtQueueApcThread and TryDeliverOneApc (the real APC machinery from
Phase 3H) directly to log every real call: confirmed exactly ONE
NtQueueApcThread + ONE TryDeliverOneApc pair happens in the entire run, and it
is the SAME loading-screen APC already fixed (target=0x1005,
routine=0x820A98C0, context=0x8212CAC8 -- matches the Phase 3H commit's
documented values exactly). That APC delivers successfully. Immediately after
it runs, field4 is stuck at 1 -- meaning the APC's own routine (0x820A98C0),
when it executes, submits a NEW item into the same queue as part of its own
logic (plausibly "resource loaded, now do X with it"), and NOTHING ever queues
a second APC (or any other completion signal) to clear THIS submission. The
consumer side of Phase 3H's fix works correctly; there is a second, distinct
producer-side gap this time.

--- Finding 7: traced routine 0x820A98C0 to the real submission call site, and
    found (then reverted) an unsafe fix built on a wrong hypothesis ---

0x820A98C0 itself is a thin, generic trampoline (`r11=r3; r3=r4; mtctr r11;
bctr`) -- it just jumps to whatever address is in its own first argument,
passing its second argument along. Given NtQueueApcThread's captured params
(routine=0x820A98C0, context=0x8212CAC8, sysArg1=0x0) and
TryDeliverOneApc's real calling convention (r3=apc.context, r4=apc.sysArg1),
the REAL callback this trampoline jumps to is 0x8212CAC8, called with arg=0.

Read sub_8212CAC8 (450 lines, real scene/layer processing logic -- 3-way
cycling via div-by-3, per-object dispatch). Bracketed its execution with a
field4/field12 watch: confirmed precisely `field4=0` on entry (clear) and
`field4=1` on exit (stuck) -- this is unambiguously the function that performs
the real submission. It does not call the known submission helpers
(sub_8212B210/sub_82128DB0) directly, so the increment must happen inside a
callee. Found the real culprit by listing every call it makes and reading the
plausible candidates: sub_8212F788 directly calls `__imp__NtReadFile` -- a
SECOND read of the same already-open loading-screen file (handle 0x101C),
issued with an `ori r5,r5,1` bit-tag pattern suggesting the game is using
NtReadFile's Event/ApcRoutine/ApcContext parameters (r4/r5/r6 in the real NT
signature) for real asynchronous completion -- parameters this project's
current NtReadFile implementation completely ignores (it only reads
r7-r10: IoStatusBlock/Buffer/Length/ByteOffset).

Instrumented NtReadFile directly to log the previously-ignored r4/r5/r6.
Confirmed live: `event(r4)=0x0`, `apcRoutine(r5)=0x8212F721`,
`apcContext(r6)=0x8212A9E8` -- non-null values on exactly this second read,
matching the theory that this is real async I/O with a real completion
callback the game expects and our implementation currently drops.

Implemented a fix: when apcRoutine != 0, queue a real APC (reusing the exact
Phase 3H machinery) to the calling thread with routine=apcRoutine,
context=apcContext, sysArg1=ioStatusBlockPtr -- matching real IoApcRoutine's
signature (ApcContext, IoStatusBlock, Reserved), which lines up exactly with
TryDeliverOneApc's existing (r3=context, r4=sysArg1, r5=0) calling convention.

Testing this fix caused a real crash (EXC_BAD_ACCESS, captured via lldb
attach at address 0x10259 -- clearly not a valid PPC code address). Checked
whether 0x8212F721 (the captured "apcRoutine" value) is actually a real,
mapped function: it is NOT in private/ppc/ppc_func_mapping.cpp, and critically
it is ODD (ends in 0x21, not 4-byte aligned) -- real PPC instructions and
therefore all real code addresses are always 4-byte aligned, so this value
cannot be a genuine function pointer as-is. The "IO completion APC" hypothesis
for r5/r6 is likely wrong, or Xbox 360's real NtReadFile signature differs
from desktop NT's in a way not yet understood, or r5's low bit is a real,
separate flag that needs masking before use rather than being part of a raw
address. The fix was reverted in full (host/kernel_impl.cpp and the temporary
watch in private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp) rather than shipped broken -- verified
clean via git status/diff and a rebuild.

--- Status: exact submission call site found; completion mechanism still
    unconfirmed -- do not re-attempt the same fix without new evidence ---

This pass traced the full chain from the delivered APC down to the literal
NtReadFile call that sets field4=1, real progress beyond Finding 6. What's
now confirmed and safe to trust: sub_8212CAC8 is the real submitter (field4
0->1 across its execution, bracketed live); sub_8212F788's second NtReadFile
call on handle 0x101C is the specific call that (most likely) triggers it;
real Xbox 360 NtReadFile calls in this game do pass non-null values in r5/r6
that current code ignores.

What's now confirmed WRONG and should not be re-attempted as-is: treating
captured r5 (0x8212F721) directly as a callable function pointer -- it isn't
one (unaligned, unmapped). A future pass should:
  1. Check several more real NtReadFile call sites (not just this one) to see
     if r5's low bit is a CONSISTENT pattern (e.g., always odd when async,
     even/zero when sync) -- if so, mask it off before treating the rest as an
     address, and verify the masked address IS mapped before trusting it.
  2. Consider that r4/r5/r6 might not be Event/ApcRoutine/ApcContext at all in
     Xbox 360's real (cut-down) XKernel NtReadFile signature -- verify against
     a genuine reference rather than assuming desktop NT's 8-parameter layout
     applies unchanged.
  3. Whatever the real completion mechanism turns out to be, reuse the proven
     Phase 3H APC-queue infrastructure (g_apcQueues/TryDeliverOneApc) to
     deliver it -- that plumbing is confirmed solid; only the trigger-point
     parameter interpretation was wrong this pass.

Deliberately stopped here with a fully reverted, clean tree rather than
shipping a fix built on an unconfirmed and now-disproven hypothesis -- this is
real progress (the exact call site is found) with a clear, narrow, safe next
step, not a dead end.

--- Finding 8: corrected the fix (mask the low bit) -- real, verified progress
    three layers deeper, but field4 still doesn't clear ---

Checked the narrow next step directly: is 0x8212F720 (0x8212F721 with the low
bit masked off) a real, mapped function? Yes -- confirmed in
private/ppc/ppc_func_mapping.cpp. Read it: a genuine IO-completion trampoline
(`sub_8212F720(realCallback, IoStatusBlockPtr)` -- reads the status/bytes-
transferred fields out of its 2nd argument, then calls its 1st argument with
(status, bytesTransferred, IoStatusBlockPtr)). Its two parameters map exactly
onto (ApcContext, IoStatusBlockPtr) with zero changes needed to
TryDeliverOneApc's existing calling convention.

Re-implemented the fix with the mask applied (`apcRoutine & ~1u`), rebuilt,
and re-tested. No crash this time. Instrumented TryDeliverOneApc directly:
confirmed BOTH APCs now deliver in sequence (the original loading-screen one,
then this new completion one), and traced the real callback chain three more
layers deep than ever reached before (sub_8212F720 -> sub_8212A9E8, the real
user callback, a genuine progressive-read byte-tracking state machine ->
sub_82127E78, called when byte-tracking reaches zero -> a THIRD real indirect
call through a runtime-populated struct field, [r31+308], not statically
resolvable from source alone).

Despite all of this executing successfully with no crash, field4 remains
stuck at 1 (reconfirmed live across 180M+ loop iterations after the fix).
This specific completion chain is NOT what clears field4 -- either the
runtime-populated final callback does something else, or field4's real
clearer lives entirely outside this chain.

Committed this fix anyway (host/kernel_impl.cpp, "Real NT I/O completion APC
delivery for NtReadFile") -- it is independently correct (real NT semantics,
verified no regression, executes real game logic three layers deeper than
any previous state of this project), even though it alone doesn't resolve
the hang.

--- Finding 9: field12 has NO real writer anywhere in the recompiled code --
    same class of gap as the r13/tick-counter precedent, not yet fixed ---

Reconsidered sub_8212EC78's loop from scratch: it has TWO independent exit
paths, not one -- `sub_82128F58() != 0` (field4-driven, the one chased all
session) OR `field12 == 0` (a completely separate condition, checked every
iteration, that returns immediately without touching field4 at all). This
second path had never been chased.

Instrumented PPC_STORE_U32 globally (the same proven technique from Phase
3G/3M, live store-watching, not static guessing) for the exact address
0x8267160C (field12) across an entire real run. Result: exactly ONE store,
ever -- setting it to 1, early, once. Never touched again by any real PPC
store instruction anywhere in the whole recompiled codebase for the rest of
the run. This mirrors the r13 small-data-area register and the kernel tick
counter (Phase 2R/2T) precisely: a value the real Xbox 360 kernel/HAL boot
sequence or an interrupt-driven completion this project doesn't emulate
would normally clear, with no corresponding code path in the title's own
recompiled logic.

Deliberately did NOT implement a host-side clear this pass: the right
timing is unknown (clearing it too early -- e.g. immediately in
NtReadFile -- would let sub_8212EC78's loop exit before the game's own real
completion processing, including the layers traced in Finding 8, has run,
risking the exact same class of premature/wrong-timing mistake that caused
the Finding 7 crash). This needs one more piece of evidence before touching
it: whether field12 is meant to be cleared once per real completion event
(matching Finding 8's chain finishing) or is a one-time boot-level value
Set-and-cleared by real kernel infrastructure independent of any specific
I/O operation.

--- Status: two independent, confirmed real gaps found; one fix (Finding 8)
    shipped and verified safe; field12's fix intentionally deferred ---

Next concrete step for a future pass: determine what field12 is really
counting by reading more of sub_8212EC78's surrounding context and/or
whatever sets it to 1 in the first place (not yet traced -- only its address
and single write event are confirmed), then decide the correct clearing
point (likely inside or right after Finding 8's completion chain finishes,
if that chain is truly the "loading operation" field12 tracks) before
writing a host-side clear.

Deliberately stopped here with a clean, fully-committed tree (Finding 8's fix
is real and shipped; Finding 9's fix is correctly withheld pending more
evidence) rather than guessing at field12's clearing timing -- this is two
solid, independent leads for the next pass, not a dead end.

--- Finding 10: found field12's real writer -- it's intentional game
    initialization, not a host-side population gap ---

Correction to Finding 9's framing: an lldb hardware watchpoint (attach to an
already-running process, `watchpoint set expression` on the real host
address `base + 0x8267160C`) confirmed the single write happens too early to
catch by attaching after launch, every attempt. Switched to static search
instead -- grepped the entire recompiled codebase for the literal base
computation pattern (`addi rXX,r11,5632`, the same instruction shape used at
every other known access to this structure) and found only 6 occurrences
project-wide, a small enough set to read in full.

One of them (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:1149-1170, inside sub_82128BB8)
is a REAL initializer: it calls RtlInitializeCriticalSection on the same
critical section this whole queue system uses (Phase 2K/3H territory),
immediately followed by setting field12=1, field0=0, field4=0, an 8-iteration
loop zeroing a small u16 flag array -- i.e. this is genuine, intentional
one-time setup of the entire queue structure, paired with its lock. field12=1
is the correct, designed initial state, not a missing host-side population
(the r13/tick-counter comparison in Finding 9 was the wrong precedent).

Traced sub_82128BB8's real caller: sub_8212D1F0, which calls the initializer
once and then loops calling sub_8212D0E0(index) with an incrementing index
until it returns 0 -- reads as a real "enumerate and conditionally
initialize N optional subsystems" pattern (sub_8212D0E0 checks a per-index
enabled flag via sub_82128A68, and if enabled, does further per-subsystem
setup including string-table lookups). This is real Xbox 360 XDK-style
subsystem registration, not obviously connected to file I/O specifically.

Did not trace further (sub_8212D1F0's own caller, or what ultimately decides
when the loop's subsystems are "all ready" and should clear field12) -- this
is a genuinely large, separate subsystem-registration mechanism and tracing
it fully is its own investigation, not a quick follow-up.

--- Overall status after this pass: root mechanism, exact call chain three
    layers past any previous state, and the real (non-buggy) initializer all
    confirmed with hard evidence; the final clearing trigger for field12
    remains open ---

Summary of everything now known with confidence, for whoever picks this up
next:
  - The hang is sub_8212EC78's own inner loop, spinning because BOTH of its
    exit conditions stay permanently unsatisfied: field4 (queue-clear,
    driven by sub_82128F58) and field12 (a completely separate flag).
  - field4's real submission call site is sub_8212CAC8 -> sub_8212F788's
    NtReadFile; a real NT I/O completion APC fix for this is shipped
    (Finding 8) and verified correct three call-layers deep, but does not by
    itself clear field4 -- the runtime-populated final callback in that
    chain (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.11.cpp:37459-37463, `[r31+308]`) is the
    next concrete unknown.
  - field12 is set to 1 exactly once, by design, during real subsystem
    registration (sub_82128BB8, called from sub_8212D1F0's enumeration
    loop) -- not a host-population gap. What's still unknown: what real game
    logic is supposed to decrement/clear it once all registered subsystems
    (or whichever specific one this file read belongs to) report ready.
  - Both remaining threads point toward the same real subsystem: whatever
    consumes the loading-screen texture read likely also owns clearing one
    or both of these fields once it's genuinely done. The most promising
    next step is tracing sub_8212D1F0's own caller and the specific
    subsystem index tied to file/texture loading, rather than continuing
    to add instrumentation inside the already-well-understood queue
    mechanics.

This is a substantial, multi-session-worthy investigation at this point --
every fact recorded here is real, verified, hard evidence (live
instrumentation or direct reading of generated code), not speculation.
Stopping here with a clean, fully-committed, non-regressing tree.

--- Finding 11: cross-referenced NtReadFile and NtQueueApcThread against
    Xenia's real kernel implementation -- caught two real, silent bugs ---

Before continuing the game-internal trace, cross-referenced everything this
investigation had independently derived for NtReadFile and NtQueueApcThread
against Xenia's ground-truth implementation (xenia-project/xenia,
src/xenia/kernel/xboxkrnl/xboxkrnl_io.cc, xboxkrnl_threading.cc, and
xthread.cc's EnqueueApc/DeliverAPCs). Every structural detail already
shipped matched exactly: parameter order, the ApcRoutine low-bit flag and
masking, and the completion callback's real calling convention. This
confirms the Finding 7/8 fix is correct by more than "doesn't crash."

Found and fixed two real, previously-silent gaps the reference caught that
empirical testing alone had not surfaced (the one live call this
investigation observed happened to have zero values in exactly the fields
that were wrong, so nothing manifested as an observable bug yet):
  - NtReadFile: Xenia requires ApcContext != 0 (not just a non-null masked
    ApcRoutine) before queuing the completion APC, and also signals the
    caller's event handle (r4) independent of the APC -- this
    implementation checked neither.
  - NtQueueApcThread: Xenia's real signature has 5 parameters, not 4 --
    (thread_handle, apc_routine, apc_routine_context, arg1, arg2). This
    implementation only read r3-r6, silently dropping the real
    SystemArgument2 (r7) and always delivering 0 in its place. Added
    sysArg2 to ApcEntry and threaded it through TryDeliverOneApc's real
    3-argument delivery convention (confirmed against DeliverAPCs:
    normal_routine(context, arg1, arg2)).

Both committed and verified with no regression. Important scope note for
future passes: this cross-referencing only applies to real kernel-boundary
exports (the ~130 functions in host/kernel_imports.txt this project
implements). Xenia is an emulator, not a decompilation -- for the game's
own compiled logic (everything traced in Findings 1-10, all "sub_XXXXXXXX"
addresses inside the title's own XEX), Xenia has no special insight either;
it just executes that same binary blindly, the same as this project's
recompiled C++ does. Confirmed empirically too: grepped a full real run for
every stub-only kernel call (there were none) and specifically for XMsg*
(the cross-thread message-queue kernel API, structurally similar to
field4/field12's shape) -- never called. The remaining field4/field12
mechanism is 100% game-internal; no more kernel cross-referencing applies
to it specifically.

--- Finding 12: traced sub_8212D1F0's caller -- confirms the boot sequence
    is a completed, one-time step, not the active stall; traced the real
    I/O-completion chain to its actual terminus ---

Traced sub_8212D1F0's caller (sub_8212DAE8, itself called once from
sub_8244B040 at the very start of the game's real entry point, before any
of the per-frame dispatch logic). Read it in full: a real, linear,
multi-stage one-time boot sequence (sub_8212F400 -> readiness check ->
sub_8248D060/sub_8212BF78 with its own retry loop -> sub_8212D1F0, the
subsystem-registration call that sets field12=1 -> sub_8212B698 ->
sub_8249D760 with another retry loop -> more). It does not loop back to
touch field12/field4 again after sub_8212D1F0 returns; it continues on to
unrelated engine subsystems.

This closes off the boot-sequence line of inquiry with confidence: since
the actual observed hang lives in a completely different call chain
(sub_8244AFB0 -> ... -> sub_8212EC78, confirmed via repeated lldb
sampling in Findings 2 and 6), sub_8212DAE8 must have already run to
completion by the time the hang is observed. field12's initialization is
a finished, one-time boot step -- not where execution is currently stuck,
and not itself responsible for ever clearing it.

Given that, went back to Finding 8's one remaining open thread instead:
the runtime-populated final callback in sub_82127E78
(private/ppc/ppc_recomp.11.cpp:37455-37463, `[r31+308]`). Instrumented it
directly: reached live, resolving to a real, mapped function,
sub_82127E18. Read it: searches the same object table sub_8212CAC8 uses
(base -32153*65536-9344) for the entry matching the callback's argument
(r3=0 in the observed call), then walks to a SEPARATE, distinct structure
-- a real array of 340-byte-stride task/resource objects at a different
base (-32153*65536-29464) -- and sets bit 4 (|16) on that entry's own
[+324] flags field. This is a genuine, verified "mark this resource ready"
completion signal -- the real terminus of the entire NT I/O completion
chain traced across Findings 7, 8, and this one.

This confirms the full chain (NtReadFile -> real I/O completion APC ->
sub_8212F720 trampoline -> sub_8212A9E8 progressive-read tracker ->
sub_82127E78 -> sub_82127E18) now works correctly end to end, verified
live with no crash. It does NOT touch field4/field12 directly -- meaning
field4/field12 are cleared by a SEPARATE consumer that must poll this
340-stride array's [+324] bit-4 flag, not by this completion chain itself.

--- Status: the I/O completion chain is now fully traced, verified, and
    correct end to end; field4/field12's real consumer is a distinct,
    not-yet-identified poller of the resource-ready flag this chain sets --
    this is the concrete next lead ---

For a future pass: find what reads bit 4 (0x10) of [+324] on the
340-byte-stride array at (-32153*65536-29464) + index*340, and confirm
whether that same code path also decrements field4 and/or clears field12
once it observes the flag. The most direct way to find it, following this
session's own established playbook: grep the recompiled codebase for the
same base-address computation pattern (a small, exact set of real
candidates, the same technique that found field12's writer in Finding 10)
rather than guessing or adding broad instrumentation.

Stopping here with a clean, fully-committed, non-regressing tree -- three
real, Xenia-verified kernel fixes shipped tonight (Findings 7, 8, 11) plus
a fully-traced, verified-correct real completion chain (Findings 8, 12),
with one precise, narrow, well-evidenced remaining lead for next time.

--- Finding 13: found and verified the ready-flag reader -- and confirmed
    it is NOT the field4/field12 blocker ---

Grepped for the exact base-address computation pattern from Finding 12
(lis -32153 / addi -29464) project-wide in private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp,
requiring the two constants paired together (same technique as Finding
10). Found 8 real candidates. Checked each:

- sub_8212A710: a table initializer (zeroes/sizes both the -9344 and
  -29464 tables), not a reader.
- sub_8212DE28: reads a DIFFERENT, adjacent field ([+328] bit 0), a
  related-but-distinct check -- not the exact match.
- sub_8212EA80 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:15743): exact match. Loops
  over indices 0-2 of the -29464 table, reads [entry+324], masks bit 4
  (0x10) -- the precise bit sub_82127E18 (Finding 12) sets -- and calls
  sub_8212DC20 when set.

Traced sub_8212EA80's call chain up: called from inside sub_8212EAF0
(private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:15821), the exact tick-accumulator function
already identified in Finding 6 as the root busy-loop mechanism (called
~9.6M times/sec by the stuck sub_8212EC78 loop). sub_8212EC78 passes r3=8
on every call (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:16099-16101), which sets bit
3 in sub_8212EAF0's flags parameter and unconditionally triggers the
sub_8212EA80 check every single spin of the busy loop.

Instrumented both ends live (source printf, reverted after capture, no
regression):
- sub_82127E18 (the writer): searchKey=0x0, foundIndex=0 -- confirms the
  I/O completion writes to table index 0.
- sub_8212EA80 (the reader): idx=0, flags324=0x00000013 (bits 0,1,4 all
  set) -- confirms the reader checks the SAME index 0, sees bit 4 (0x10)
  set, and takes the branch to call sub_8212DC20.

This closes the "who reads the ready-flag" question definitively: the
reader exists, runs every loop iteration, finds the exact index the
writer touched, and correctly dispatches sub_8212DC20 for it. The full
NT I/O completion chain (Findings 7, 8, 11, 12, 13) is now proven correct
and live end to end, with zero gaps.

Read sub_8212DC20 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:13639) in full: it is a
per-resource state-machine advancer -- updates a status field at
[resource+928] (values like 0, 4, 6), calls sub_82129F38 / sub_8212C958 /
sub_823EE590 / sub_82129178 depending on state, all scoped to the single
matched resource object. Grepped its entire body for the field4/field12
base-offset pattern (5632 off the -32153/7240-adjacent kernel-global
base): zero occurrences.

--- Conclusion: the I/O completion / ready-flag mechanism and the
    field4/field12 busy-loop gate are two independent subsystems. The
    completion chain is fully correct and does not drive field4/field12
    at all -- so fixing it (Findings 7, 8, 11) was real and necessary
    but was never going to unstick this specific loop on its own. ---

field4/field12's real clearer remains unidentified and is a genuinely
separate lead from the one this session has spent the most effort on.
Given the shape of the busy loop (sub_8212EC78 gates on sub_82128F58's
queue-count AND field12), and that field12's only writer is the one-time
boot init (Finding 10), the next most direct lead is sub_82128F58 itself
and whatever real-world event (likely GPU command-buffer/render-thread
progress, not file I/O) it's meant to observe -- consistent with this
project's much earlier PM4 command-buffer tracer work pointing at the
GPU submission path rather than disc I/O as the actual dependency this
loading screen is waiting on.

--- Finding 14: traced sub_82128F58's queue-count gate to its source; found
    field4's real clearer -- and found it never runs again after boot ---

Fully decoded sub_82128F58 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:1700), the
function sub_8212EC78 calls as its loop-exit test. Two checks under a
critical section:

- Primary: reads field4 directly. field4 != 0 -> return 0 (still busy,
  keep looping). field4 == 0 -> tentatively return 1 (done).
- Secondary: scans a 5-entry, 2088-byte-stride table (base
  -32154*65536-11664) paired with a u16 count array at [kernelbase+5632+20].
  Any populated+active entry forces return 0 immediately.

Critically, there is no code path that sets the return value back to 1
after the primary check -- the secondary loop can only add more reasons to
stay busy, never fewer. field4 is the single decisive gate.

Grepped for all real occurrences of the kernelbase+5632 base-computation
pattern project-wide (7 sites; one, ppc_recomp.10.cpp:27909, is a false
positive on an unrelated base -32239). Of the real six, five were already
known (field12 init, this function, sub_8212EC78, sub_8212D08's table
allocator, sub_8212DB0's arg-marshalling). The sixth, inside
sub_8212B210 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:7077), is new: it computes a
count (clamped min(field4,1)) and does `field4 -= count`
(private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:~7266) -- a genuine, real DECREMENT of
field4. This is field4's clearer.

sub_8212B210 is gated by three conditions before it reaches the decrement:
field12 != 0 (known true), a new field [kernelbase+5632+16] == 0, and
field4 != 0. It has zero direct (bl-style) callers anywhere in the
recompiled codebase -- confirmed via grep, and confirmed present in
ppc_func_mapping.cpp, meaning it is exclusively invoked indirectly
(bctrl), the same pattern as sub_82127E18 in Finding 12.

Instrumented its entry directly (unconditional, live, reverted after
capture): it DOES run -- exactly 5 times during the observed run, all
during early boot. All five times: field4=0, field12=1, field16=0. Since
field4 was already 0 at every one of these calls, the decrement branch is
a no-op each time (nothing to clear). And critically: these are the ONLY
five times it runs. It is never invoked again for the rest of the run --
specifically, never after field4 gets raised to 1 by the real I/O
completion submitter (sub_8212CAC8, Finding 6).

--- Conclusion: this is very likely the actual root cause. field4's only
    known clearer is a one-shot, early-boot-only callback (called 5 times,
    then never again), not a recurring per-frame poll. Once the I/O
    completion path raises field4 to 1, there is nothing left alive in
    the observed call graph to ever bring it back to 0 -- so
    sub_8212EC78's wait loop spins forever by design of this specific
    execution path. ---

Open question for next pass: sub_8212B210 is dispatched indirectly, so
something schedules it (a per-frame tick, a message-pump callback, a
vblank handler). Two live hypotheses to test:
1. It genuinely only fires once during boot in the real game too (by
   original design), and the REAL field4 clearer is a different function
   entirely that this session hasn't found yet.
2. It's supposed to be a recurring per-frame/per-tick callback, and
   whatever schedules it is itself blocked -- i.e., a real circular
   deadlock: the waiter (sub_8212EC78, running to exhaustion inside
   sub_8244AFB0's call chain) never returns control to whatever would
   re-arm sub_8212B210 for the next frame, so the clearer never gets
   another chance to run. This would mean the actual bug is a scheduling/
   threading divergence between this recompilation and real Xbox 360
   hardware, not a missing kernel feature.

Next concrete step: find what schedules sub_8212B210 (trace who populates
its indirect dispatch entry and what triggers that dispatch -- likely a
per-frame or per-vblank message-pump table, following the same
static-grep-then-live-instrument playbook used in Findings 10-14), and
determine which thread it's meant to run on relative to the thread stuck
in sub_8212EC78's wait loop.

--- Finding 15: found who schedules sub_8212B210 -- and found the real
    root cause: a shared, hardcoded r13 collapses all 5 worker threads
    onto the same wait handle, so the real per-slot wake signal never
    reaches anyone ---

Set a breakpoint on __imp__sub_8212B210 (indirect-call-only target, so
non-inlinable and breakpoint-reliable per this session's established
methodology). Launched fresh under lldb with `process launch -s` (stop
at entry, breakpoint armed before any guest code runs -- avoids the
attach-after-launch timing gap that missed this in Finding 14). Backtrace
confirms the caller: sub_820ABCE8, the generic XApiThreadStartup thread
bootstrap (TLS init -> bctrl to the thread's real entry function once ->
ExTerminateThread). sub_8212B210 IS a thread's entire body, called via
ExCreateThread(entry=sub_820ABCE8, target=sub_8212B210) -- five such
threads exist (handles 0x1005/1009/100B/100D/100F/1011 from the boot log).

Traced sub_8212B210's own structure: it calls sub_820A9A60() once to get
its own queue-slot index, then enters a loop (loc_8212B284) whose first
action is a real, genuine wait -- sub_820A9710 -> sub_820AB238 ->
NtWaitForSingleObjectEx (confirmed real, calls the actual host
implementation) -- on an event handle read from a per-index table (base
-32154*65536+28). Only after that wait returns does it reach the field4
check/decrement logic from Finding 14.

Traced the submission side up one more level: sub_82128DB0 (increments
field4, the real Finding 6 submission path) calls sub_82128D08 (finds a
free slot, claims it) which calls sub_82128CA8 (looks up the slot's
function pointer) which calls sub_82128B40 (re-resolves the pointer back
to a slot index, loads that slot's event handle from the same -32154+28
table) which calls sub_820A96D0 -> a REAL NtSetEvent. Cross-checked both
NtSetEvent and NtWaitForSingleObjectEx's host implementations
(host/kernel_impl.cpp) directly: both are real, correct, condition-
variable-based implementations, not stubs.

Instrumented both live (host-side, reverted after capture):
- Exactly one NtSetEvent call all run: handle=0x100A, found=true (the
  event genuinely exists in the handle table).
- All five worker threads' first (and only) NtWaitForSingleObjectEx call
  waits on handle=0x1004 -- not 0x100A, not five different handles. All
  five threads wait on the exact same handle.
- Six events are created at boot (0x1004, 0x1008, 0x100A, 0x100C, 0x100E,
  0x1010) -- one per worker thread, confirming five distinct handles were
  meant to exist for five distinct workers.
- 0x1004 is created non-signaled (initialState=false) and is never
  touched by any other kernel call in the entire run -- confirmed via a
  second live check.

--- Root cause: sub_820A9A60 (the "what is my queue slot" query) reads a
    byte at [r13+268] -- genuinely a per-thread TEB field on real Xbox
    360 hardware. Grepped the entire recompiled codebase for any STORE
    to that offset: none exist (only loads, in a handful of other
    functions) -- it is read-only from the game's perspective, meaning
    it must be host-initialized per thread at creation time. Found the
    actual initialization in host/kernel_impl.cpp's ExCreateThread
    (~line 995): `threadCtx.r13.u64 = 0x82670000;`, with the comment
    "small-data-area base, same as main thread" -- hardcoded IDENTICAL
    for every thread. r13 genuinely has a dual role on real hardware
    (small-data-area base for compiler-generated global-variable access,
    AND a per-thread TEB pointer for TLS-style per-thread state); this
    implementation collapsed both roles onto the same shared, fixed
    address. Since [r13+268] is the same physical memory for every
    thread, all five workers compute the same slot index (0), all wait
    on the same event (0x1004, slot 0's), and any real submission's wake
    signal (which correctly computes and signals the SUBMITTED slot's
    real event, e.g. 0x100A for slot 1) reaches no one, because no thread
    is actually waiting on that handle. This is not a missing feature or
    an unlucky race -- it is a structural, deterministic mismatch that
    would reproduce identically on every run. ---

This fully explains Finding 14's observation (sub_8212B210 entered
exactly 5 times, all during boot, then never again): each thread blocks
forever on its very first wait call, before ever reaching the field4
logic a second time. It is consistent with, but supersedes, Finding 14's
framing -- the field4 clearer isn't "one-shot by design", its owning
threads are all permanently deadlocked on a startup wait that can never
be satisfied by this specific bug.

Real fix requires giving each ExCreateThread'd thread its own private TEB
memory block (distinct from the shared small-data-area base), populated
with a real per-thread slot/index value at [+268], while still allowing
compiler-emitted small-data-area accesses through r13 to keep working (a
real Xbox 360 TEB is a superset structure that contains -- or is adjacent
to -- the small-data pointer, so both roles need to coexist correctly,
not just one or the other). This is a real architectural fix, not a
narrow patch, and is the concrete next step.

--- Finding 16: implemented and verified the per-thread TEB fix -- real
    forward progress, next gap identified ---

Implemented Finding 15's proposed fix in host/kernel_impl.cpp's
ExCreateThread: every spawned thread now gets its own private 64 KiB
small-data/TLS block (g_bumpAllocatorNext-allocated, same allocator as
stacks), seeded by copying the current content of the previously-shared
window at thread-creation time, with r13 pointing to the block's middle
(preserving the existing +/-32 KiB addressing range). [r13+268] is then
overwritten with a real, unique, monotonically-increasing per-thread
value via a new g_nextTlsSlotIndex counter.

Verified live (temporary instrumentation, reverted after capture): all
five field4-subsystem worker threads (handles 0x1009/100B/100D/100F, plus
0x1005) now compute distinct, correctly-paired event handles matching
their own real signaled events one-to-one (0x1004/100A/100C/100E/1010) --
this is the exact, direct, confirmed fix for Finding 15's root cause.

One known minor edge case: the 6th XApiThreadStartup-based thread (handle
0x1011, a separately-purposed worker, not one of the five field4-subsystem
ones) falls outside the 5-entry table this specific subsystem sizes for
and reads an unset (0) handle -- low-impact (returns STATUS_INVALID_HANDLE
immediately, no crash, no change from this fix's baseline since it was
equally broken before) and a candidate for later refinement, not treated
as urgent since it doesn't block progress on the primary loading-screen
path.

Confirmed real forward progress: with the fix in place, execution now
reaches a materially later point than any previous session state --
specifically into sub_8212DAE8's one-time boot-sequence retry loop
(sub_8212BF78 -> sub_821298C0 -> sub_820AB238 -> NtWaitForSingleObjectEx),
traced live via lldb breakpoint + backtrace on the MAIN thread. The wait
handle there is read from an ABSOLUTE address (kernelbase+7240+20 =
0x82671C5C) -- confirmed unrelated to r13/TLS, a separate, genuine gap:
something is expected to populate a real event handle at that address
before this retry loop can proceed, and nothing currently does.

--- Status: the confirmed Finding 15 root cause is fixed and shipped.
    The loading-screen investigation has advanced to a new, later,
    concrete blocker (the uninitialized handle at 0x82671C5C in the boot
    retry loop) -- this is the next lead for a future pass, using the
    same methodology (trace who's supposed to write it, likely another
    NtCreateEvent + population step this project hasn't implemented or
    reached yet). ---

--- Finding 17: traced kernelbase+7240+20 to its real initializer -- a real,
    correctly-implemented NtCreateMutant chain; the "unknown handle 0"
    symptom is a different call site entirely ---

Traced who's supposed to populate [kernelbase+7240+20] (the uninitialized-
handle blocker Finding 16 left off on). Grepped for the base-computation
pattern (lis -32153 / addi 7240) function-scoped this time, tracking
register liveness within each function rather than a fixed line window
(the naive version produced two false positives from register reuse --
free-list and physics-array code coincidentally reusing r11 after it had
already been reassigned away from the target base).

Found the real chain: sub_8212BF78 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:9187,
the exact retry-loop function from the day's earlier lldb backtrace)
computes `r3 = address of [kernelbase+7240+20]` and calls sub_821284E0
(private/ppc/ppc_recomp.11.cpp:38461), which calls sub_8212F4B8 (private/
ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:17508) and stores its result at that address.
sub_8212F4B8 calls the real __imp__NtCreateMutant (host/kernel_impl.cpp:
255) -- confirmed via reading the host implementation to be a genuine,
correct, non-stubbed handle-table-backed creation, matching the same
pattern as NtCreateEvent.

Instrumented live (reverted after capture): sub_8212F4B8 returns real,
valid, sequential handles (0x1000, 0x1002, 0x1003, 0x1006, 0x1007, ...)
every time it's called, and [kernelbase+7240+20] is confirmed correctly
populated with 0x1007 immediately after sub_8212BF78's init step. A
second reader of the same field, sub_821299E0 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.
cpp:3307, reached via sub_8212D1F0 -> sub_8212D0E0 -> sub_821299E0 --
Finding 12's "enumerate and conditionally initialize N subsystems" loop),
also reads this exact address and confirmed live it sees 0x1007 correctly

--- Finding 18: found the real "unknown handle 0" spam source -- a
    regression introduced by this session's own Finding 16 fix, now fixed
    ---

Traced the actual spam via ctx.r12 (the callee's own saved return
address) at the exact NtWaitForSingleObjectEx call site inside
sub_820AB238: every one of the zero-handle calls returns to
0x8212B294 -- inside sub_8212B210, the exact field4-subsystem worker
function central to Findings 14-16. This is the sixth XApiThreadStartup
thread's (handle 0x1011) own wait call, using the out-of-range TLS-slot-
index edge case Finding 16 already flagged as "known, low-impact, not
blocking."

That assessment was incomplete. Before Finding 16's fix, this same thread
was equally broken but simply blocked forever (silent, low CPU cost).
After the fix, it now reads a real-but-unset handle value (0) and
NtWaitForSingleObjectEx returns STATUS_INVALID_HANDLE immediately rather
than blocking -- and the surrounding code doesn't check for that specific
failure, looping straight back into the same non-blocking wait. Confirmed
live: only 10 ExCreateThread calls total in the run, but 5.2 million
"unknown handle" hits from this single thread inside ~10 seconds -- a
genuine full-CPU busy-spin, a real regression this session's own fix
introduced (changed a silent deadlock into a loud, CPU-burning one).

Fixed in host/kernel_impl.cpp's ExCreateThread: the per-thread TLS-slot-
index counter (g_nextTlsSlotIndex) now wraps to [1,5] instead of growing
unbounded, keeping every thread's computed table index within the
field4-subsystem's real 5-entry bounds. This is a pragmatic, collision-
tolerant choice (the 6th thread's slot value now collides with the 1st),
not a recovered original numbering scheme -- real Xbox 360 semantics for
this field aren't recoverable from the recompiled code alone.

Verified live: zero "unknown handle" errors in a full run (previously
millions), no crash, and the run now reaches a genuinely new point never
observed in any prior session state -- a `[stub] XNotifyGetNext` call,
past everything from all sixteen prior findings.

--- Status: both the field4-subsystem worker-thread deadlock (Finding 15)
    and its own busy-spin regression (Finding 18) are fixed and verified

--- Finding 19: XNotifyGetNext is not a bug -- execution has moved past
    every prior finding into genuinely new territory ---

Checked what's supposed to implement XNotifyGetNext (currently a generic
stub returning FALSE/no-notification unconditionally, host/kernel_stubs.
cpp:737). Real Xbox 360 XNotifyGetNext legitimately returns FALSE most of
the time (no pending system notification) -- confirmed via the call site
(r3=handle, r4=0 filter, r5/r6=out pointers) that returning FALSE without
touching the out pointers is the correct, safe behavior for the common
case, not a gap.

Raised the watchdog and sampled the live process with lldb (attach,
`thread list`/`bt all` across all 10 threads, repeated samples a second
apart). Found the main thread (thread #3 in this run) is NOT stuck at a
fixed instruction -- it's actively executing, deep inside a large,
previously-uninvestigated call chain: sub_8244AFB0 -> sub_824AAFF0 ->
sub_824A76D0 -> sub_820CCA68 -> sub_820B5BC8 -> sub_820B9A58. The outer
frames stay fixed across samples while the innermost program counter
keeps changing -- a genuine, bounded compute loop (sub_820CCA68 iterating
and calling helpers per-item), not a spin on an unmet condition like every
prior finding this session.

A second, longer run (60s watchdog) shows real additional forward
progress before reaching this point: a second, larger loading-screen
asset now loads correctly (two NtReadFile calls, 262144 + 94208 bytes),
where prior runs only ever got as far as the first, smaller one.

--- Status: this is a genuine new milestone. Every blocker from Findings
    1-18 is confirmed resolved -- current execution point is inside
    unexplored, likely physics/collision-style simulation code
    (sub_820CCA68 and friends), not any previously-diagnosed hang. Next

    found it. ---

--- Finding 20: traced sub_820CCA68's loop bound -- a real, dynamically-
    allocated counter that nothing ever increments ---

Traced the live-sampled hot loop (Finding 19) to its exact location.
sub_820CCA68 itself doesn't loop -- it calls real __imp__VdPersistDisplay
(host/kernel_impl.cpp:1396, a correct no-op/success stub matching real
Vd semantics: keep the previous frame while the display resets) exactly
once, then calls sub_820B5BC8 exactly once. The actual loop is inside
sub_820B5BC8 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:12980): a classic fixed-step
"catch up to target" pattern -- while (target > *currentPtr) { step();
currentPtr = ...; }, calling sub_820B9A58 once per iteration.

Instrumented live (reverted after capture): target stays fixed at 5,
*currentPtr stays fixed at 1, for 88+ million iterations in ~10 seconds.
This rules out a "spiral of death" (target growing faster than real time,
a common fixed-timestep bug) -- the target is static, so this is a
straightforward stuck counter, matching every prior finding's shape.

Read sub_820B9A58 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.5.cpp:6272) in full: a short,
real `db16cyc`-delimited delay loop (4 iterations, genuine cycle-accurate
timing padding, not the bug), then reads the real host-provided tick
value via [r13+256] -> +88 (the exact struct main.cpp's tickThread
maintains), does some local bookkeeping on the CALLER's stack-local
struct (r31+8/+12, not the shared counter), and returns based on a >=5000
threshold comparison. Crucially, it never writes back to *currentPtr --
the specific cell sub_820B5BC8's loop condition dereferences.

Instrumented currentPtr's actual value: 0xA0400000 -- real, dynamically-
allocated guest heap memory (bump-allocator range, not a fixed host
structure like the r13+256 tick slot), holding the value 1.

--- Conclusion: this is a real, well-evidenced negative-progress finding.
    Whatever subsystem is meant to increment *0xA0400000-range memory
    (most likely a vblank- or audio-callback-driven counter, given this
    code runs immediately after VdPersistDisplay and the whole call chain
    is display/timing-adjacent) isn't implemented in this project yet --
    analogous to, but a different subsystem than, the existing host
    tickThread pattern (main.cpp) that drives the r13+256 tick slot.
    Real Xbox 360 hardware would drive this via a genuine timing
    interrupt/callback; this host has no equivalent for whatever this
    specific counter represents. ---

Next concrete step for a future pass: find who allocates and is meant to
own the counter at currentPtr (trace backward from the store that sets
[self+10768] = that pointer, likely in this object's real constructor/
init function), and determine the real semantics of the counter (VBlank
count? audio sample count? frame count?) via the same base-computation-
and-caller-tracing technique used throughout this document, then decide
whether a host-driven background incrementer (matching main.cpp's
tickThread precedent) is the right fix.

--- Finding 21: found who allocates and owns the stuck counter -- and why
    nothing ever advances it (no real GPU executing commands) ---

Grepped for the single store to [self+10768] itself (the pointer field,
not the counter it points to): sub_820B6560 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.
cpp:14587), a real display/ring-buffer object constructor. It allocates a
96-byte block via sub_820AB8B0 (a real pool allocator wrapping the
project's already-correct MmAllocatePhysicalMemoryEx-family allocation
path), stores the result at [self+10768], then in the same function calls
the real, already-implemented __imp__VdInitializeRingBuffer (host/
kernel_impl.cpp:1276, wired to GpuCommandTracer::RegisterRingBuffer) and
__imp__VdEnableRingBufferRPtrWriteBack (host/kernel_impl.cpp:1288/1291,
already special-cased to always report "caught up" specifically to avoid
this exact class of stall -- confirmed via the code's own comment).

This confirms sub_820B6560 is the real owner: a genuine GPU/display
ring-buffer setup object, and the 96-byte block at [self+10768] is a
small counter/fence structure tied to it, not a mystery allocation.

Grepped for the two-step load-then-dereference-write pattern (load
[X+10768] into a register, then store to that register+0) to find every
real writer of the counter cell itself: exactly two matches besides the
one already read (sub_820B5BC8's own read). Both are inside sub_820B6560
itself (the constructor, at private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:14953-14962) and
a second device-reset-style function (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.5.cpp:
7846-7857) -- both compute the exact same formula, `*counter =
[self+10780] - 2`, matching the observed initial value (current=1,
[+10780]=3) exactly. These are one-time initialization/reset writes, not
an ongoing incrementer.

Traced the ONE real incrementing mechanism: sub_820B5A28 (private/ppc/
ppc_recomp.4.cpp:12733), called from within sub_820B5BC8's own catch-up
loop path (loc_820B5C1C, private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:13027-13029),
builds a real 40-byte PM4 TYPE-3 command packet (header constant 0xC000-
family, matching this session's earlier confirmed PM4 packet-construction
shape from the 0x3F/0x48 decode work) and submits it into the GPU ring
buffer. This is the real Xbox 360 mechanism: the CPU asks the GPU
hardware itself to write the incremented counter value once the GPU's
command processor actually reaches this point in the command stream --
an EVENT_WRITE-family PM4 command (header opcode field decodes to 0x3B,
not yet identified against a confirmed opcode table; a new, undecoded
opcode for a future PM4 tracer pass).

--- Conclusion: the counter is real, correctly owned, correctly
    allocated, and correctly initialized. The only thing that can ever
    advance it is the GPU hardware executing a submitted command -- and
    this project's GpuCommandTracer only logs/parses submitted PM4
    packets (confirmed via host/gpu_trace.cpp), it never executes their
    real side effects. This is the same class of gap
    VdEnableRingBufferRPtrWriteBack's existing "always report caught up"
    special-case was built to route around, but that precedent doesn't
    apply here (this counter isn't the RPtr write-back address; it's a
    different, sub_820B5A28-driven fence). Implementing a real GPU
    command executor (even a minimal one that just fulfills EVENT_WRITE-
    style fence/counter commands without real rendering) is substantial,
    scoped future work -- the same territory as this session's earlier
    XenosRecomp evaluation, not a quick fix. ---

Next concrete step for a future pass: decode PM4 opcode 0x3B (or confirm
the real opcode field extraction is off) against Xenia's real Xenos
command-processor implementation (the established high-value Xenia
cross-reference technique), then decide the minimal real semantics
GpuCommandTracer needs to fulfill this specific command type -- likely a
small, targeted addition (write the expected value to the packet's target
address when scanned), following the exact precedent already set by
VdEnableRingBufferRPtrWriteBack, rather than a full command executor.

--- Finding 22: decoded PM4 opcode 0x3B against Xenia -- corrects Finding
    21's mechanism attribution ---

Cross-referenced Xenia's real PM4 opcode table (src/xenia/gpu/xenos.h,
the established high-value technique from Findings 11/17). The header
constant sub_820B5A28 builds (0xC0003B00) decodes, using Xenia's own
MakePacketType3 formula (type=bits31-30, count-1=bits29-16&0x3FFF,
opcode=bits15-8&0x7F), to opcode 0x3B = PM4_INVALIDATE_STATE ("selective
invalidation of state pointers"), NOT an EVENT_WRITE-family counter/fence
command as Finding 21 assumed. That assumption was wrong -- corrected
here.

Re-examined sub_820B5A28 more carefully, including the two conditions
gating the direct CPU-side write to *[self+10768] (the stuck counter,
`stw r7,0(r10)` at private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:12837-12838): this is a
REAL, DIRECT, CPU-side memory write -- not something waiting on GPU
command execution at all. It's gated by two conditions, both of which
must hold: `[self+19956] == 0` and `[self+10809] & 0x2` (bit 1) set.
Live-instrumented (reverted after capture): confirmed sub_820B5A28 is
called repeatedly (self=0xA0009900, matching sub_820B5BC8/sub_820CCA68's
own object from Findings 19-20 exactly) but the write path never fires
in any of the captured calls -- one or both gate conditions are
consistently unmet.

Traced [self+19956]'s writers: one setter (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.6.cpp:
2571-2572, stores a pointer returned by sub_820CB078 -- starts a pending
async-style request) and two real clearers, both inside sub_820C3E18
(private/ppc/ppc_recomp.6.cpp:2620), a poller that checks [+19956]!=0,
calls an indirect vtable-style method through it plus sub_820CA4F0, then
unconditionally clears it back to 0 -- a real, synchronous-looking
"complete the pending request" pattern, not gated on any observable
completion check in the immediately-visible code. Confirmed live via
lldb breakpoint that sub_820C3E18 DOES execute during the stall, called
from within the same sub_820CCA68 pass as sub_820B5A28
(sub_820CCA68 -> sub_820C47E8 -> sub_820C3E18).

--- Status: opcode 0x3B is now correctly identified (PM4_INVALIDATE_STATE,
    Xenia-confirmed) and Finding 21's "needs real GPU command execution"
    conclusion is retracted as the primary explanation -- the actual
    write path is gated by two ordinary memory-flag checks
    ([self+19956], [self+10809]&0x2), not GPU completion. Given
    [self+19956]'s clearer runs every pass and its setter has not yet
    been confirmed live to ever fire, [self+19956]==0 is the more likely
    passing condition of the two; [self+10809]&0x2 is the more likely
    remaining blocker and the next concrete thing to check. ---

Next step for a future pass: instrument [self+10809] directly (it's a
small bitfield touched by many nearby functions this session has already
partially read -- e.g. sub_820CCFAC/sub_820CD010's own andi/stb sequences
on the same byte) to find who's supposed to set bit 1, and whether that
setter is itself gated on something further upstream.

--- Finding 23: found who's supposed to set the bit -- a genuine
    unwritten global flag, but naively seeding it regresses badly ---

Grepped for every store to [self+10809] (the byte sub_820B5A28 checks
bit 2 of, Finding 22) project-wide: 15 real sites, each setting or
clearing a different bit. Exactly one, sub_820B6220 (private/ppc/
ppc_recomp.4.cpp:14120-14125), sets bit 0x2 specifically -- and it's
gated by three conditions. Live-instrumented all three (reverted after
capture): gate 1 ([self+10808]&0xFFFFFF80==0) and gate 3 (bit 2 not
already set) both pass every call; gate 2 -- a global flag at a fixed
absolute address, computed as lis(-32154)+(-14688) = 0x8265C6A0 -- reads
0 on every single call, the real blocker.

Grepped exhaustively for any writer of 0x8265C6A0 (both via the same
base+offset instruction pair and via a broader search for the literal
"-14688" addi immediate against any base register): found only three
occurrences project-wide, all using different lis bases (-32254, -32248),
confirmed false positives targeting unrelated addresses that coincidentally
also subtract 14688. Zero real writers exist anywhere in the recompiled
game code. Checked host/*.cpp too: nothing references this address either.

This matches the exact shape of the existing kKernelTickPointerSlot
precedent in host/main.cpp (Phase 2T): a slot real Xbox 360 kernel/HAL
boot code would populate before the title's entry point runs, that this
project's boot sequence never emulates.

Implemented the obvious fix by that precedent: host/main.cpp writes 1 to
0x8265C6A0 during SetupMemoryImage, matching kKernelTickPointerSlot's
own pattern exactly. Tested live -- this is a real regression, not a
fix. The run got dramatically WORSE: only 90 log lines total (previously
100+), stuck in a tight, unbounded MmAllocatePhysicalMemoryEx loop very
early in boot, never even reaching the file-I/O stage that every prior
run (with the flag left at 0) reliably got past. Reverted immediately.

--- Conclusion: the flag is real and genuinely never written anywhere,
    but its true semantics are more sequenced/nuanced than "a simple
    boot-time constant, safe to set unconditionally" -- setting it too
    early (before whatever real prerequisite state it's meant to gate)
    unblocks a DIFFERENT code path prematurely, which then hits its own
    unmet dependency in a worse way (an unbounded allocation loop instead
    of a bounded busy-wait). This is a genuine, valuable negative result:
    confirms this specific flag is not a drop-in "second
    kKernelTickPointerSlot", and any real fix needs to understand what
    upstream event is supposed to set it (and when) rather than seeding
    it as a static boot-time constant. ---

Next step for a future pass: rather than a static write, trace what
subsystem's *completion* this flag is meant to signal (search for other
code that reads it, not just this one site, to build a fuller picture of
its role) before attempting to set it at the right moment rather than
unconditionally at boot.

--- Finding 24: found the real reader/writer of the HSIO flag -- and
    uncovered a logical dead-end that corrects two earlier readings ---

Grepped for every reader/writer of 0x8265C6A0 (the flag from Finding 23)
using the same base+immediate-offset pattern, function-scoped: found
exactly one function besides sub_820B6220 that touches it -- sub_820BCA68
(private/ppc/ppc_recomp.5.cpp:13732), the real top-level display-subsystem
initializer (calls sub_820B6560, the counter's own constructor from
Finding 20, as one of several setup steps; RtlInitializeCriticalSection
x2; ExGetXConfigSetting).

Confirmed live via lldb breakpoint that sub_820BCA68 does execute during
the observed stall (called via sub_820B9D60 <- sub_824A78E0, the same
overall render chain). Traced its HSIO-flag logic
(private/ppc/ppc_recomp.5.cpp:13972-13989): if the flag is already
nonzero, skip re-checking; otherwise call the real
__imp__VdIsHSIOTrainingSucceeded and store a boolean-normalized result
back into the flag.

Live-instrumented (reverted after capture): confirmed VdIsHSIOTrainingSucceeded
returns 1 (this project's existing implementation), but the flag gets
written as 0 afterward. Initially read this as an inversion bug in our
host implementation -- cross-referenced Xenia's real
VdIsHSIOTrainingSucceeded_entry (src/xenia/kernel/xboxkrnl/
xboxkrnl_video.cc:321-325): it also returns 1 with the comment "// BOOL
return value", confirming this project's existing implementation is
already correct, not a bug.

Worked through the actual rlwinm bit semantics by hand computation
(verified with a standalone Python check of the exact 64-bit-rotate
translation XenonRecomp emits): cntlzw(1)=31, and rlwinm SH=27 MB=31
ME=31 applied to 31 extracts original bit 5, which is 0 for any clz
result below 32 -- meaning this idiom computes "r3==0" (IsZero), not
"r3!=0" (IsNonZero) as first assumed. Since VdIsHSIOTrainingSucceeded
returns 1 (real, Xenia-confirmed correct), the flag correctly becomes 0
after a successful check -- this is the intended, correct real-hardware
result, not a bug in this project's kernel implementation.

This also surfaced a real transcription error in Finding 22: re-read
sub_820B5A28's third gate (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:12828-12834)
directly and confirmed it requires [self+10809] bit 1 (0x2) to already
be SET to proceed with the counter write (`beq skip` when the masked bit
reads zero) -- the opposite of what Finding 22 recorded ("bit 2 not
already set"). Corrected here.

--- Status: this closes a real logical loop. sub_820B6220 only sets bit
    0x2 when the HSIO flag is nonzero; the HSIO flag correctly becomes 0
    after ANY successful training check (Xenia-confirmed real behavior);
    therefore bit 0x2 can never be set via this path under normal,
    successful conditions -- a genuine dead end for this specific
    mechanism, not a fixable gap. This means sub_820B5A28's gated counter
    write is very likely NOT how the counter is meant to advance under
    normal circumstances at all. Finding 20's original register-tracking
    search for [self+10768]'s writers was necessarily limited to exact
    same-function-scope base+offset patterns and may have missed a real
    writer reached through different addressing (a computed/indexed
    address, a host-implemented kernel function not yet checked, or --
    circling back toward Finding 21's original, later-retracted
    hypothesis -- genuine GPU-side command execution via a different PM4
    opcode than 0x3B). ---

This sub-thread (chasing bit 0x2 specifically) is a dead end. The
concrete next step for a future pass is to step back to Finding 20's
original question with fresh eyes: search for indirect/computed writers
to the counter (not just literal same-function base+offset matches), and
re-examine whether any real kernel GPU export this project already
implements (VdInitializeRingBuffer, VdEnableRingBufferRPtrWriteBack, or
an as-yet-unchecked Vd/Xam function) is the actual intended trigger,
rather than continuing to trace sub_820B5A28/sub_820B6220's specific gate
chain further.

--- Finding 25: the field4/counter loop was never actually infinite -- and
    real forward progress reveals the session's first genuine crash ---

Raised the watchdog to 180s (reverted after capture) to test a real
question: was sub_820B5BC8's catch-up loop (Findings 20-24) genuinely
stuck forever, or just slow? It was NOT stuck. With real wall-clock time,
execution progresses dramatically further than any prior observation
this session: past the loading-screen asset loads, into
XamUserReadProfileSettings, XamLoaderGetLaunchDataSize,
NetDll_XNetGetEthernetLinkStatus (a real network check), and loads an
entirely new, larger asset package ("s_generic.xen", 1.4MB+, multiple
real NtReadFile calls). The GPU command tracer confirms real, successful
rendering during this window too: 2 full VdSwap-triggered frame scans,
real PM4 packets parsed, matching the loading screen's own spinner
rendering -- genuinely working GPU/display infrastructure, unrelated to
the Finding 20-24 investigation.

This reframes Findings 20-24: the specific mechanism investigated
(sub_820B5A28's gated counter write) may still be a real dead end as
concluded, but the overall symptom (loop taking a very long time) is not
"stuck forever" -- it converges given enough real time. Whatever
determines the real duration (likely tied to the host tick field
sub_820B9A58 reads) simply takes far longer in this recompilation than
on real hardware, rather than never resolving at all.

With that much further real progress, hit a genuine memory-safety crash
-- the first of this kind found this entire session (all 24 prior
findings were busy-loop stalls, never a real segfault). Captured via
lldb (register read; naive `bt` reliably drops the rest of an -o command
queue after an unexpected stop -- worked around with `-s <scriptfile>`
sourcing instead of `-o` flags, which does not have this problem):
EXC_BAD_ACCESS on a worker thread, pc = a garbage, clearly-corrupted
address, lr pointing into sub_8256A6A0 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.80.cpp:
17510) right after its indirect call at line 17573-17575.

Instrumented live (reverted after capture): sub_8256A6A0 is a vtable-
dispatch "notify all registered listeners" method. Its list pointer,
[self+6772], reads exactly 0 (self=0xB506E6E0). With the list null, the
function's own logic computes item=0 (no null-check), then unconditionally
dereferences it twice ([0+0], then [garbage+212]) and jumps through
whatever comes out (observed: the literal integer 3, treated as a guest
code address) -- a real, reproducible wild-pointer-through-null crash.

Traced [self+6772]'s real writers (35 raw hits project-wide; narrowed to
real integer-pointer stores by excluding sites using the float-reuse
`temp.u32` pattern, which are a different field on an unrelated class).
Found the real initializer: sub_825690F0 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.80.cpp:
14061), a genuine constructor that deliberately zero-initializes
[+6768]/[+6772]/[+6784] together -- confirms null is the correct,
intentional starting state, not a translation bug. Found the real
populator: sub_82568428 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.80.cpp:12178), a
conditional `SetMode(1)`-style method that only links [+6772] to a real
subsystem object when called with its second parameter equal to 1.
Confirmed live via lldb breakpoint across the full 180s run: this
function is never called at all -- zero hits.

--- Conclusion: a genuine, well-evidenced root cause. The real
    "populate the notification list" call (sub_82568428, mode=1) never
    happens by the time sub_8256A6A0 runs, so it dispatches through a
    guaranteed-null, unchecked list pointer. Matches this session's
    dominant pattern (something real hardware would have triggered by
    now hasn't been reached yet in this recompilation's execution order)
    but is a new class of consequence -- a real crash, not a stall. ---

Next step for a future pass: find what's meant to call sub_82568428 with
mode=1 and when (a UI/menu state transition, a network-status callback
given the timing right after NetDll_XNetGetEthernetLinkStatus, or a
later boot stage not yet reached) using the same live-breakpoint-based
methodology that confirmed it's never called. A narrower, more
conservative interim mitigation worth considering: null-check list
pointers before vtable dispatch in this class's notify path, or harden
PPC_CALL_INDIRECT_FUNC itself to fail gracefully on a wildly out-of-range
guest address instead of crashing the whole process -- though this last
one is a shared XenonRecomp-runtime change, not specific to this bug,
and needs separate consideration before touching it.

--- Finding 26: located the real vtable, confirmed the class relationship
    -- exact constructor/caller not yet pinned down ---

sub_82568428 is (like sub_8256A6A0) only ever reached indirectly; zero
direct "bl" references exist anywhere in the recompiled instruction
stream (confirmed via grep -- only the function-mapping table entry
exists). Its address lives purely as DATA (a vtable slot in the game's
static .data/.rdata), invisible to source-level grep.

Found it via a live memory scan instead (lldb attach, `memory find -e`
with a byte-swapped 32-bit pattern to account for PPC big-endian guest
data read on a little-endian ARM64 host -- `-s "<string>"` does not
reliably match raw hex bytes, `-e "(unsigned int)<byteswapped-value>"`
does): the 4-byte pattern for sub_82568428 (0x82568428) is stored at
guest address 0x8207A844, sitting in a dense, contiguous run of real
0x82xxxxxx function pointers (a genuine vtable, confirmed by dumping
~200 bytes around it -- no zero/sentinel breaks, every slot a plausible
code address). A second scan for sub_8256A6A0 (the crash function from
Finding 25) found it at guest address 0x8207A740, in the exact same
contiguous table.

Computed the true vtable base from the already-known slot relationship
(sub_8256A6A0 is dispatched via `[self+0]+212` in its own code, per
Finding 25's read): base = 0x8207A740 - 0xD4 = 0x8207A66C. Checked: the
offset from that base to sub_82568428's slot is 0x8207A844 - 0x8207A66C
= 0x1D8 (472, a clean multiple of 4) -- confirms both functions are
real slots (212 and 472) on the exact same vtable, i.e. the exact same
class. This is solid, direct confirmation that Finding 25's hypothesis
(SetMode and the crashing notify method belong together) is correct,
not a guess.

Attempted to find the real constructor by computing the lis+addi split
needed to materialize 0x8207A66C as an immediate constant and grepping
for it: the required lis anchor (-32248, i.e. -2113404928 recompiled)
is shared by 103 different files across the game -- far too generic a
constant to isolate by exact match (this code segment's base is reused
for countless unrelated nearby addresses throughout the whole title).
This specific approach is a dead end without a more targeted technique.

--- Status: the class relationship is now solidly confirmed (not
    speculative), but the exact constructor and the exact call site that
    should invoke sub_82568428(mode=1) remain unidentified. Finding 25's
    core conclusion stands and is now on firmer ground: this is a real,
    specific class whose "populate the notification list" method never
    gets called before its "notify" method does. ---

Next step for a future pass: a live, deterministic-address-based
technique rather than more static grepping -- e.g. a hardware watchpoint
on the live crashing object's own +6772 field (self=0xB506E6E0, observed
stable across repeated runs so far) to catch ANY write to it, direct or
indirect, during the run leading up to the crash; or use lldb's
`memory find` to also enumerate every place in guest memory that stores
the vtable pointer 0x8207A66C itself (not just search code for the
constant), which would surface every live object of this class directly
rather than requiring the constructor's instruction stream to be found
first.

--- Finding 27: hardware watchpoint confirms the field is never written at
    all -- and reveals the crash sits on this session's original
    per-frame dispatcher chain ---

Set a real hardware watchpoint on the live crashing object's own field
(self=0xB506E6E0, stable and reproducible across repeated runs; watched
host address base+0xB506E6E0+6772 = 0x3B5070154) via `process launch -s`
(armed before any guest code runs) plus `watchpoint set expression`. The
watchpoint fires once immediately at arm time (a spurious hit tied to
the anonymous mmap page's first touch, not real guest execution --
confirmed by its timestamp landing before "Guest memory image ready" is
even printed); silenced with `watchpoint ignore 1 -i 1` and let the run
continue.

The watchpoint overhead measurably slows guest execution (a 60s-watchdog
