run under it didn't even reach the first loading-screen file, versus
reaching a whole second, larger asset package without it) -- raised the
watchdog to 600s (reverted after capture) to give real wall-clock time
to compensate. With that, the run reaches the exact same crash as
Findings 25-26, and the watchpoint never fires a second time across the
entire ~10 minutes of real guest execution leading up to it.

This is stronger, more direct evidence than Finding 26 (which only
confirmed one specific function, sub_82568428, is never called): a real
hardware watchpoint watching the exact memory cell for a write from ANY
source, direct or indirect, the whole run, confirms nothing ever writes
it. Finding 25's root cause is now confirmed as solidly as this kind of
investigation allows without decompiling the game's real intended
control flow by hand.

The crash's full backtrace was also captured cleanly this time (`bt`
survives after a `continue`-then-crash when commands are sourced via
`-s <scriptfile>`, unlike raw `-o` queues which reliably drop everything
after an unexpected stop -- reconfirms Finding 25's own methodology
note): sub_8256A6A0 <- sub_8244DD08 <- sub_82461D28 <- sub_8244AE70 <-
sub_8244AFB0 <- sub_8244B040 <- sub_8212B130 <- sub_825DEC18 <- __xstart.

Three of these frames -- sub_8244AFB0, sub_8244AE70, sub_82461D28 -- are
the exact same functions Findings 1-6 identified at the very start of
this investigation as the real per-frame update dispatcher (the chain
the original "physical memory allocation stall" symptom traced back to).
This confirms the crash sits on that same real dispatcher, just one
level deeper and through a different sibling call (sub_8244DD08, not the
sub_82446D50/sub_8212EC78 path Findings 1-6 originally got stuck in) --
concrete, structural evidence that fixing the Finding 15/18 deadlocks
let real per-frame execution reach further into the SAME dispatcher's
other subsystem updates, one of which (sub_8244DD08's own callee chain,
ending in sub_8256A6A0) is this session's newest blocker.

--- Status: root cause confirmed via the strongest evidence practical
    (a whole-run hardware watchpoint, not just one function's call
    count). The remaining open question -- who should call
    sub_82568428(mode=1), and when -- needs the game's real, intended
    control flow (likely a menu/profile/network-readiness callback,
    given XamUserReadProfileSettings and NetDll_XNetGetEthernetLinkStatus
    both run immediately before this crash in every observed run) rather
    than more memory forensics on this recompiled binary alone. ---

--- Finding 28: exhausted the practical static/dynamic search for
    sub_82568428's real caller -- genuine dead end for this technique,
    proposing a defensive alternative ---

Tried finding the caller by searching for ANY code dispatching through
vtable slot 472 (sub_82568428's slot on our class's vtable, confirmed in
Finding 26), regardless of which object -- reasoning that if slot 472
is unique to this method, any such call site would be a real candidate.
Filtered 47 raw "+472" load hits down to 3 that genuinely match the full
vtable-dispatch shape (load [obj+0], then [+472], then mtctr+bctrl).

All 3 are false positives: identical shared pattern (a preceding call to
sub_820D5E30, then dispatch through +472 with r4 set to a raw stack
buffer address, not the integer 1 sub_82568428's own code checks for).
This is a different, unrelated class whose own vtable happens to also be
at least 472 bytes and have some other method at that exact slot --
confirms vtable slot numbers are only meaningful within one class's own
layout, not globally, so this search technique cannot distinguish real
candidates from coincidental slot collisions across different classes
without already knowing the object's real type at each call site.

--- Status: this closes off the last practical avenue for finding
    sub_82568428's real caller through this session's available
    techniques (static grep, live breakpoints, memory scanning, hardware
    watchpoints). Combined with Finding 27's whole-run watchpoint (the
    strongest possible confirmation available without hand-decompiling
    surrounding game logic), sub_82568428 is a real, legitimate,
    indirect-only game method that is simply never invoked in any
    observed run. Identifying its true intended trigger would require
    either manually reading substantially more of the surrounding game
    logic than is practical to do blind, or comparing against a related
    Xbox 360 title's real decompilation (e.g. the dc3-decomp reference
    from earlier this session) if an analogous class exists there. ---

Practical alternative worth considering for a future pass, distinct from
finding the "true" original caller: harden the consumer instead of the
producer. sub_8256A6A0 (Finding 25) already treats an empty list
(count==0) as a legitimate no-op via its r3=0 fallback path -- it simply
never checks whether r3 itself is null before dereferencing it. A
defensive null-check there (skip the vtable dispatch entirely when the
list pointer is null, matching the same "nothing registered yet, nothing
to notify" semantics the count==0 path already expresses) would directly
prevent this specific crash without needing to know what the real,
intended trigger for sub_82568428 is or when it should fire. This
targets the crash's proximate cause rather than restoring the game's
original control flow, and is offered as a pragmatic option, not a
replacement for finding the real root trigger if that's ever identified.

--- Finding 29: shipped the defensive null-check fix -- real, verified
    forward progress past the crash ---

Implemented Finding 28's proposed fix in private/ppc/ppc_recomp.80.cpp's
sub_8256A6A0: added `if (ctx.r3.u32 == 0) goto loc_8256A758;` at both
loc_8256A700 and loc_8256A740 (the two identical unchecked-dereference
sites, the second discovered while re-reading the function in full to
place the first guard correctly -- same bug shape, same fix). Both jump
to the function's existing, real early-exit path (already used by three
other legitimate skip conditions in the same function), not a
newly-invented fallback.

Note on persistence: private/ppc/*.cpp is XenonRecomp-generated output,
gitignored (matches this whole session's established handling of that
directory). Confirmed via CMakeLists.txt that the normal build only
globs existing files as source -- it does not invoke the recompiler as
part of routine `cmake --build`, so this edit is safe from being
silently overwritten by rebuilding, but it is not tracked by git. This
finding entry is the durable record of the exact change, matching how
this session has documented every other private/ppc modification.

Verified live with a 90s watchdog (reverted after capture): zero
crashes, exit code 2 (clean watchdog timeout, not a crash signal) on
repeated runs. Execution now sails straight past the exact point Finding
25 crashed at and reaches genuinely new territory: a second
NetDll_XNetGetEthernetLinkStatus call, then a request for
"speech_generic.xen" -- an entirely new asset category (speech/audio
data) never seen in any prior run this session. That specific file isn't
found on this disc image (status=0xC0000034), handled gracefully by the
game's own existing not-found path (matching every other missing-file
case observed all session), not a new crash.

--- Status: the session's first real crash (Finding 25) is fixed and
    verified. This is a defensive fix at the crash's proximate cause,
    not a restoration of the game's original intended control flow
    (Finding 28 established that finding sub_82568428's true caller is
    not practical with current techniques) -- but it's a real, working,
    low-risk unblock that lets genuine further execution happen. Next
    milestone reached: speech_generic.xen, a new asset category
    entirely. ---

--- Finding 30: found and fixed a real bug in host/xdvdfs.cpp -- BST
    directory navigation compared case-folded names, but the on-disc
    tree is sorted by raw bytes ---

speech_generic.xen genuinely exists on the disc (confirmed via `strings`
on the raw ISO and a temporary linear-scan directory dump added to
host/xdvdfs.cpp -- both found it plainly, sitting directly under
Packages\ alongside ~60 other real files). Yet
XdvdfsImage::ResolvePath consistently failed to find it, while
succeeding for many sibling files (e.g. w_apploadingscreen_fetm.xen,
looked up successfully dozens of times earlier this session).

Root cause: SearchDirectory's binary-search-tree traversal (host/
xdvdfs.cpp) decided which child to descend into by comparing
lowercase-folded strings (`targetLower < entryNameLower`), but the
Packages directory holds a real mix of casings -- many "s_Xxx" entries
(capital S: s_Generic.xen, s_DeathMatch.xen, s_CharacterTool.xen, etc.)
alongside all-lowercase "speech_xxx" entries (speech_generic.xen,
speech_hockey.xen, etc.). The genuine on-disc BST was built by
comparing the ORIGINAL, case-preserved bytes (uppercase ASCII sorts
before lowercase), not a case-folded order -- so our case-insensitive
navigation decision sent the search down the wrong subtree for entries
whose case-sensitive and case-folded orderings disagree, even though
the final name-equality check (correctly case-insensitive, matching
every prior successful lookup) would have accepted the match if the
traversal had ever reached it.

Confirmed directly: a temporary linear (non-BST) scan of the same table
found speech_generic.xen instantly; the BST-based search did not.
Diagnostic instrumentation eliminated other candidate explanations first
(stream error state: ruled out, `good()`/`fail()` clean on every call;
a bad drive-prefix in my own test path: real bug found and fixed in the
test itself, host/kernel_impl.cpp's OpenGuestFile already correctly
strips "d:\\"/"d:/" via `path.substr(3)` before calling ResolvePath).

Fix (host/xdvdfs.cpp, SearchDirectory): switched the tree-navigation
comparison to the raw, case-preserved name (`name < entryName`),
keeping the final match check case-insensitive as before. Also added a
linear-scan fallback over the same table if the BST walk doesn't find a
match (covers any other real ordering edge case this specific
hypothesis doesn't explain, at the cost of a full table scan only on
the failure path -- this function isn't called often enough for that to
matter).

Verified live with an extended watchdog (180s, reverted after capture):
speech_generic.xen now resolves (status=0x0) and reads completely (36
real NtReadFile calls, ~9.46 MB total, consistent with a real generic
speech/audio pack). Execution reaches a new kernel call never seen in
any prior run this session: KeResetEvent.

--- Status: a real, generically-applicable bug fixed at the host's disc-
    reader layer (not a per-file gap like every other finding this
    session) -- likely also fixes other, not-yet-reached files whose
    names happen to collide the same way under case-folded comparison,
    not just this one. Next milestone: KeResetEvent, a currently-stubbed
    kernel import, is the new frontier. ---

--- Finding 31: KeResetEvent is not a new blocker -- execution loops back
    into the exact same unresolved GPU counter from Findings 20-24 ---

After Finding 30's fix let speech_generic.xen load fully, execution
reaches a genuinely new kernel call (KeResetEvent, currently stubbed as
a no-op) and then stalls. Live-sampled the stuck thread: executing
sub_820B9A58, the same tick-catchup helper from Findings 20-22.

Instrumented sub_820B5BC8's loop the same way as Finding 20's WATCH-11A
to check whether this is a new object/counter or the same one. It's the
same one: self=0xA0009900, target=5, current=1, delta=4 -- identical
values to the very first capture in Finding 20. This is not a new
subsystem's counter; it's the exact same, never-advancing GPU/display
ready-counter recurring, just reached via a different call path now that
Findings 29 and 30 unblocked more of the boot sequence. Ran a full 300s
watchdog window (reverted after capture) with zero progress -- confirmed
genuinely stuck this time, not merely slow.

This corrects part of Finding 25's framing: that finding's conclusion
("the loop was never actually infinite, just slow") was based on a
single observation where more real time happened to let execution
continue past this exact point. In hindsight this was very likely a
different code path reaching a *different* exit condition (or a
different, smaller-target call to the same shared helper elsewhere)
resolving in that instance -- not this specific counter genuinely
advancing. The counter itself, as thoroughly traced in Findings 20-24,
still has no real writer: its one direct CPU-side write path
(sub_820B5A28) is permanently gated behind a real dead end (Finding 24:
the HSIO-flag boolean-normalize logic correctly produces 0 on a
successful check, by design, matching Xenia's own real implementation --
not a bug to fix), and no GPU command-execution path exists to advance
it via the hardware-fence mechanism either (Finding 22's corrected
understanding).

--- Status: Findings 20-24's root cause is confirmed to still be the
    active, current blocker -- not resolved, not superseded. The
    session's other fixes (Findings 29, 30) were real and valuable
    (a genuine crash and a genuine disc-reader bug, both fixed and
    verified), but they cleared different, independent obstacles on the
    way back to this same still-open one. This is the real next target:
    either find the counter's true intended writer (a lead not
    exhausted -- Finding 26 only searched for code, not for other
    objects sharing this vtable elsewhere in memory; Finding 22's
    "different PM4 opcode" angle was never followed up with a real
    Xenia cross-reference), or apply the same kind of defensive,
    proximate-cause fix used in Finding 29 (e.g. capping how long
    sub_820B5BC8's loop will spin before giving up, matching how real
    game engines commonly bound a catch-up loop to avoid exactly this
    kind of stall) as a pragmatic unblock. ---

--- Finding 32: Traced the Finding 20-24/31 fence object to its real owner
    -- root cause is a missing GPU/vblank interrupt callback, not the
    HSIO gate ---

Re-analyzed the WATCH-16A dump (watch16_run.log, 8363 samples, iter 1
through ~4.18 billion) with fresh eyes instead of assuming target was
fixed at 5 like the original Finding 20 capture:

    iter=1           target=5  current=1
    iter=259000001   target=11 current=1
    iter=517500001   target=17 current=1
    iter=776000001   target=23 current=1
    ...              (climbs by +6 roughly every 258-259M iterations)
    iter=3851000001  target=91 current=1
    iter=4100000001  target=95 current=1141840128  <- garbage, see below

target climbs steadily; current never moves off 1 until the very last
sample, where it reads 1141840128 (0x44100000 -- an IEEE-754 float
bit-pattern, not a real count; almost certainly the object's backing
memory got reused/overwritten by something else very late in this
abnormally long stress run, not a real signal).

This means sub_820B5BC8 is NOT stuck in one single call for 5 minutes --
it is being invoked repeatedly (once per real frame/tick), each time
with a HIGHER target (read fresh from a caller-side object's +8 field,
confirmed at the traced call site private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:9556-9572,
one of several similar call sites at lines 9556, 9600, 9627 etc feeding
sub_820B5BC8 via r5=13/9/11-tagged branches of the same outer dispatcher).
Every call still blocks forever internally (current never reaches even
the first target of 5), but something lets the caller loop move on to
issue the next, higher-target call anyway -- matching the "the loop just
needed more time" illusion from Finding 25, and consistent with Finding
31's correction: forward motion here is the outer loop re-issuing calls,
not this fence object actually advancing.

Traced "current" precisely: it is NOT a field of self directly. Reading
sub_820B5BC8 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:12980-13101) closely:

    current = *(uint32_t*)( *(uint32_t*)(self + 10768) + 0 )

self+10768 holds a POINTER to a separate, smaller kernel object, not an
inline field. Found the real writer of that pointer -- private/ppc/
ppc_recomp.4.cpp:14798-14832, inside sub_820B6560:

    li r3,96 ; bl sub_820AB8B0        // allocate 96 bytes (pool alloc w/ tag)
    stw r3,10768(r31)                 // self+10768 = pointer to that block
    li r3,32 ; bl sub_820AB8B0        // allocate 32 bytes
    stw r3,10772(r31)                 // self+10772 = pointer to that block
    ... sub_823F0DF0(ptr, 0, size) x2 // zero-init both blocks
    bl __imp__MmGetPhysicalAddress    // physAddr of r27 (the REAL ring buffer,
                                       // a separate, earlier-allocated buffer)
    bl __imp__VdInitializeRingBuffer  // real GPU ring buffer registration

sub_820B6560 sets up the GPU ring buffer AND, right next to it, allocates
these two small (96-byte, 32-byte) kernel objects -- sized and
zero-initialized like classic dispatcher/event objects, not raw scalars.
current is literally the first DWORD of the 96-byte object: its signal
count. It reads 1 for the entire run -- consistent with an event/fence
object that gets signaled exactly once at creation and never again.

Who's supposed to signal it again: on real Xbox 360 hardware, this class
of object is advanced by the GPU's vblank/completion interrupt, delivered
to a callback the title registers via VdSetGraphicsInterruptCallback.
Checked this project's implementation -- host/kernel_impl.cpp:1249-1255:

    PPC_FUNC(__imp__VdSetGraphicsInterruptCallback)
    {
        // Real semantics: registers a callback a GPU interrupt handler (e.g.
        // vblank) would invoke. No-op for now -- nothing yet requires us to
        // actually invoke it. ...
    }

Confirmed no-op, confirmed via grep across all of host/*.cpp that nothing
anywhere ever calls back into that registered address. VdSwap (kernel_impl.
cpp:1360-1370) is the real per-frame present call and DOES run every
frame, driving GpuCommandTracer::ScanAndTraceFrame (host/gpu_trace.cpp:
185-199) -- but that function only does PM4 command-stream trace logging
and ring-buffer read-pointer writeback (rptrWriteBackAddr_, a distinct
mechanism, itself already real/working per Phase 3H). It never touches
self+10768's object, and identifierAddr_ (the "GPU identifier address"
slot from VdSetSystemCommandBufferGpuIdentifierAddress, also already
wired up) is stored but never read back either -- both already-implemented
mechanisms are for different purposes than this fence.

--- Root cause, now precisely localized: this project never simulates a
    GPU/vblank interrupt firing. Any game code that waits on a kernel
    dispatcher object meant to be signaled by that interrupt (this fence
    object being one concrete instance, likely not the only one) will
    spin forever, because nothing on the host side ever invokes the
    callback registered via VdSetGraphicsInterruptCallback. This
    supersedes Finding 24's HSIO-gate dead end as the real explanation
    for this class of stall -- Finding 24's analysis was correct but was
    examining a different, unrelated gate on the same general subsystem.

    Real, well-scoped fix for next session: call the registered graphics
    interrupt callback once per real presented frame -- the natural place
    is host/gpu_trace.cpp's GpuCommandTracer::ScanAndTraceFrame (or right
    after it, from __imp__VdSwap in kernel_impl.cpp), synchronously, on
    the calling PPC thread, simulating a vblank interrupt firing once per
    VdSwap. That requires: (a) storing the callback address + context
    registered by VdSetGraphicsInterruptCallback (currently discarded),
    and (b) invoking it as a real guest function call (PPC_CALL_INDIRECT_
    FUNC or equivalent) from VdSwap. The game's own compiled ISR logic
    should then handle signaling this and any similar fence objects
    itself -- no need to hand-simulate individual dispatcher objects. ---

--- Finding 33: wired up the real GPU interrupt callback (genuine gap
    closed) -- confirmed empirically it does NOT reach Finding 32's fence;
    found the actual reason why (indirect GPU buffers read back all-zero) ---

Implemented Finding 32's proposed fix: host/kernel_impl.cpp's
__imp__VdSetGraphicsInterruptCallback now stores the registered callback
address + context (via new GpuCommandTracer::SetGraphicsInterruptCallback/
GraphicsInterruptCallback()/GraphicsInterruptContext(), host/gpu_trace.h/
cpp) instead of discarding them. __imp__VdSwap now invokes that callback
for real once per frame, using the same direct PPC_CALL_INDIRECT_FUNC
pattern already established for APC delivery (host/kernel_impl.cpp's
TryDeliverOneApc, real precedent, not a new pattern):

    ctx.r3.u64 = 1; // source
    ctx.r4.u64 = g_gpuTracer.GraphicsInterruptContext();
    PPC_CALL_INDIRECT_FUNC(callback);

Confirmed the real ABI before writing this by tracing the game's own
registration call site (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.5.cpp:13631-13637, inside
sub_820BC968) through to the callback's own real code (sub_820B5160,
private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:11402) rather than guessing -- the callback
genuinely takes (r3=source, r4=context), and source==1 is a real,
confirmed non-crashing branch in the game's own compiled logic (checks a
0x0BADF00D uninitialized-memory guard, then would call a nested function
pointer). Also statically confirmed the SAME object instance is both the
callback's registered context AND the object sub_820B6560 configures
fields +10768/+10772/+10776/ring-buffer on (sub_820BC968 and sub_820B6560
are both called with r3=r31 from the same enclosing init routine,
private/ppc/ppc_recomp.5.cpp ~13780-13825) -- not a coincidence of
similar addresses.

Built and ran live (90s watchdog) to verify empirically rather than trust
the static trace alone. Real result: the callback DOES fire every frame
(18 times over the run, confirmed via temporary WATCH-17 instrumentation,
since reverted) with no crash, and execution progressed further than any
prior run (deep into audio asset streaming, s_generic.xen, handle 0x1028
reads up to ~9.4MB). But Finding 32's specific fence
(self=0xA0009900, current=*(*(self+10768))) did NOT move -- stayed at 1
before and after every single callback invocation.

Traced why, precisely, instead of guessing: sub_820B5160's source==1
branch touches *(context+10772)'s object (a different, sibling 32-byte
block), not *(context+10768)'s -- confirmed by reading the actual
instructions, not assumed. Also instrumented (temporary WATCH-18, since
reverted) the branch's own nested indirect call (structPtr+16, guarded
by a null check) -- it never fired across the whole run, meaning that
function-pointer slot is never populated for this object. Also confirmed
via grep that VdSetGraphicsInterruptCallback is registered exactly once
in the whole game (the second call site, private/ppc/ppc_recomp.5.cpp:
14289, passes r3=0,r4=0 -- a teardown/reset call, not a second real
registration). So no code path anywhere in the compiled game ever writes
self+10768's first dword, even with real interrupt delivery now wired up
correctly.

Found where self+10768's OTHER offset actually IS used for something
real: private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:14873-14889, sub_820B6560 itself
derives an address as (*(self+10768) + 60), tiled/address-mangles it, and
passes it to VdEnableRingBufferRPtrWriteBack -- a real, already-working
mechanism (host/gpu_trace.cpp's rptrWriteBackAddr_, in place since
Phase 3H). So the 96-byte block has at least two live fields: offset 0
(our still-unwritten fence) and offset 60 (the ring-buffer read-pointer
writeback, already real). Offset 0's real writer is something else
entirely -- most likely a GPU command (e.g. a PM4 EVENT_WRITE-style
fence packet embedded in the ring buffer's own command stream) that this
project's tracer would need to execute, not just log.

That led to the most concrete new lead this session: checked the actual
captured gpu_trace.log from this run. Every INDIRECT_BUFFER target the
tracer follows (0x18D4D000, 0x18D2CF80, 0x18D6D120, etc, all in the real
Xenos physical/EDRAM 0x18Dxxxxx address family) reads back as all zero
bytes -- "(zero padding at offset 0, stopping)" on every single one, every
frame. Only two opcodes were ever observed all run: ME_INIT (once) and
INDIRECT_BUFFER (referencing these always-empty targets). No fence/event
write packet has ever been observed, which is now explained: if the real
command content lives in those indirect buffers and this project's
identity-mapped MmGetPhysicalAddress doesn't correctly resolve the
0x18Dxxxxx physical/EDRAM address family to wherever the game actually
wrote that data, every packet inside them is invisible to the tracer --
not because the game isn't submitting real GPU work, but because this
project can't see it yet.

--- Status: real infrastructure gap closed (graphics interrupt delivery,
    verified live, zero regressions, deeper progress than any prior run)
    -- keeping this fix. Finding 32's specific fence remains unresolved;
    exhaustively confirmed (both static and live) that no code path in
    the compiled game ever writes it via the interrupt callback mechanism,
    ruling that avenue out completely rather than leaving it open. New,
    more promising lead identified for next session: the 0x18Dxxxxx
    physical/EDRAM address family used by the game's indirect GPU command
    buffers reads back as all-zero through this project's identity
    address mapping -- likely address-translation gap, not a timing
    issue. Worth checking whether real Xenos EDRAM/tiled addresses need a
    real translation (not identity passthrough) in MmGetPhysicalAddress
    or wherever these 0x18Dxxxxx addresses actually get resolved, before
    assuming any GPU command execution semantics need implementing at
    all -- can't know what commands are really being submitted until the
    tracer can actually read them. ---

--- Finding 34: re-confirmed Phase 3K's "nothing ever writes into the
    0x18Dxxxxx indirect-buffer range" result, now with much deeper
    execution -- timing is ruled out, address translation is the
    remaining live hypothesis ---

Phase 3K (docs/superpowers/specs/phase3k-gpu-trace-findings.txt) already
ran the decisive experiment once: instrument every PPC_STORE_U32/U64 (the
only two guest-memory store paths that exist anywhere in this project's
recompiled code, confirmed by grep) to watch the full 0x10000000-
0x20000000 range, and found zero writes over a 10s run. That run left two
live hypotheses open: (1) the real fill just hasn't happened yet at that
point in boot, or (2) the address needs a translation this project
doesn't apply.

Since that run, this session's Findings 29/30/32/33 unblocked a lot more
of the game (XDVDFS BST fix, crash null-check, real GPU interrupt
delivery) -- execution now reaches much deeper (past the loading screen
entirely, ~9.4MB into real audio asset streaming, past KeResetEvent).
Re-ran the exact same experiment (temporary instrumentation added to
private/ppc/ppc_context.h's PPC_STORE_U32/U64 macros directly, so every
one of the ~28,000 recompiled functions' stores were covered, not just
one file) over a 120s watchdog run, deliberately covering far more real
execution than Phase 3K's original 10s/60s snapshots.

Result: zero hits. Not one store anywhere in a 120-second run, deep into
new territory well past every previous checkpoint, ever lands in that
256 MiB range. Instrumentation reverted immediately after (private/ppc/
ppc_context.h back to its pristine macros, confirmed via grep and a
clean rebuild).

--- This rules out hypothesis (1) with much stronger evidence than Phase
    3K had -- it isn't that the fill happens later in a boot sequence
    this project hadn't reached yet; the game now runs dramatically
    further than that original snapshot and the indirect buffers this
    session's own gpu_trace.log still shows (0x18D4D000, 0x18D2CF80,
    0x18D6D120, etc, Finding 33) are still all-zero. Hypothesis (2) --
    the INDIRECT_BUFFER address field needs a translation this project
    doesn't apply -- is now the more likely explanation, or possibly a
    close cousin of it: these particular indirect buffers may be
    populated by something other than a normal PPC store entirely (GPU-
    side microcode-driven writes, or a bulk-copy idiom this project's
    recompiler doesn't lower through PPC_STORE_U32/U64 at all -- worth
    re-checking XenonRecomp's current codegen for that possibility
    before assuming translation is the only remaining explanation). Not
    pursued further this session -- this is real, separate follow-on
    work, not a quick fix, and deserves its own focused investigation
    rather than being rushed. ---

--- Finding 35: found and fixed the real address-translation bug --
    INDIRECT_BUFFER targets are physical addresses, missing the
    0xA0000000 segment offset -- real Xenos GPU command stream now
    visible for the first time in this project's history ---

Chased Finding 34's remaining live hypothesis directly: does the
INDIRECT_BUFFER opcode's target address (0x18Dxxxxx family) need a
translation before being read via base+addr?

Reasoning that led here: this project's own host-side allocators
(NtAllocateVirtualMemory, MmAllocatePhysicalMemoryEx, both backed by
host/kernel_impl.cpp's g_bumpAllocatorNext) hand out addresses starting
at exactly 0xA0000000 -- confirmed live (first-ever allocation in every
run's log: "NtAllocateVirtualMemory: 1048576 bytes -> 0xA0000000"). That
matches the real Xbox 360 PowerPC memory model: 0xA0000000-0xBFFFFFFF is
the "uncached, direct-mapped" segment where virtual address = physical
address + 0xA0000000. Meanwhile __imp__MmGetPhysicalAddress (host/
kernel_impl.cpp:1138-1143) is a hard identity no-op ("no real
physical/virtual distinction in this harness"), and the game's own code
already does its own inline virtual-to-physical conversion via bit
masking in at least one confirmed real spot (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.
cpp:14873-14889, the VdEnableRingBufferRPtrWriteBack address-mangling
call site: "r10 = r11 & 0x1FFFFFFF", i.e. clear the top 3 bits, the
textbook segment-strip). If the game embeds its own computed "physical"
addresses directly into GPU command data (a real, correct thing to do --
real Xenos hardware GPU commands reference true physical RAM, not CPU
virtual addresses), then this project reading them raw via base+addr
would be looking at the wrong location entirely: physical address
0x18D4D000 corresponds to guest virtual 0xB8D4D000, not 0x18D4D000.

Tested empirically before touching the real fix (temporary WATCH-20
instrumentation, host/gpu_trace.cpp): for every INDIRECT_BUFFER target
observed live, dumped the first dword read three ways -- raw, |0x80000000,
|0xA0000000. Raw and +0x80000000 both always read 0x00000000 (matching
every prior run's all-zero result). +0xA0000000 read real, non-zero,
well-formed data at every single target: 0xC0006000 (decodes as a valid
TYPE3 header, opcode=0x60 count=1), 0x00000A31/0x00000D02 (valid TYPE0
register writes to plausible real register indices), 0x0001057C (valid
TYPE0, count=2). Decisive, not ambiguous.

Applied the real fix: host/gpu_trace.cpp's ScanBuffer now ORs
0xA0000000 onto the INDIRECT_BUFFER target address before following it.
Rebuilt, ran live (30s watchdog, reverted to 10s after) -- no crash, same
clean completion as every prior run, and the trace log now shows a real,
rich, structurally sane Xenos GPU command stream for the first time ever
in this project's history: dozens of TYPE0 register writes across the
real vertex/rasterizer register block (0x2000-0x2312), repeated TYPE3
opcode=0x36 (25 occurrences, clearly a common/frequent state-set command),
and a spread of other TYPE3 opcodes (0x21, 0x2B, 0x3B, 0x3C, 0x48, 0x54,
0x58, 0x60-0x63) -- a plausible, non-random command mix, not noise.
Frame count and overall shape otherwise match Phase 3K/Finding 33's
already-established pattern (content only grows across the first two
frames, then goes quiet -- unrelated, already-understood gate, not
affected by this fix).

--- Real fix, verified live, shipped. This resolves Finding 34's open
    question in favor of hypothesis (2) -- address translation, not
    timing -- and closes a foundational gap that likely affects any
    other GPU-command-embedded address this project encounters going
    forward (not just INDIRECT_BUFFER), since the same "game computes
    a true physical address inline" pattern is now confirmed real and
    reproducible. This does not by itself resolve Finding 32's
    still-unwritten fence object (that fence lives in a CPU-visible
    kernel object, not GPU command data, and Finding 33 already
    exhaustively ruled out every callback path for it) -- but it does
    mean any FUTURE investigation into PM4 opcode semantics (decoding
    what 0x36, 0x60, etc. actually do) now has real data to work from
    instead of an empty buffer. Next natural step: decode the newly
    visible opcodes' real semantics (cross-reference Xenia's opcode
    table, as already done successfully for 0x3B/0x3F/0x48) to find
    whether any of them are the fence-signaling command Finding 32 was
    looking for. ---

--- Finding 36: full Xenia opcode cross-reference for the newly-visible
    command stream (Finding 35); found and fixed a real interrupt-source
    bug (source=0 is vblank, not 1); Finding 32's fence still
    unaffected by either source ---

With Finding 35's address-translation fix unlocking real command data,
cross-referenced every opcode observed in the live trace against Xenia's
real source (src/xenia/gpu/xenos.h's Type3Opcode enum, fetched live via
`gh api repos/xenia-project/xenia/contents/...`, not assumed from memory
or generic docs). Every opcode this project has ever observed now has a
confirmed real identity:

  0x21 PM4_REG_RMW              register read/modify/write
  0x2B PM4_IM_LOAD_IMMEDIATE    load sequencer instruction memory (embedded)
  0x36 PM4_DRAW_INDX_2          draw using supplied indices (25 occurrences --
                                 by far the most common opcode in the trace,
                                 exactly as expected for real draw calls)
  0x3B PM4_INVALIDATE_STATE     (already known, Finding 22/25)
  0x3C PM4_WAIT_REG_MEM         wait until a register/memory location hits a
                                 specific value (6 occurrences -- a real GPU-
                                 side synchronization primitive)
  0x3F PM4_INDIRECT_BUFFER      (already known)
  0x48 PM4_ME_INIT              (already known)
  0x54 PM4_INTERRUPT            generate interrupt from the command stream
                                 (1 occurrence)
  0x58 PM4_EVENT_WRITE_SHD      generate a VS|PS_done event, writes memory on
                                 completion (5 occurrences)
  0x60/0x61/0x62/0x63           PM4_SET_BIN_MASK/SELECT_LO/HI

No unparsed/unknown opcodes remain in any trace captured so far.

Two of these (0x54 INTERRUPT, 0x58 EVENT_WRITE_SHD) are real hardware
synchronization/signaling primitives -- exactly the class of mechanism
Finding 32 went looking for. Pulled Xenia's real execution semantics for
both (src/xenia/gpu/command_processor.cc):

  ExecutePacketType3_INTERRUPT: reads a cpu_mask dword, and for each set
  bit n (0-5) calls DispatchInterruptCallback(1, n) -- source=1.

  GraphicsSystem::DispatchInterruptCallback(source, cpu) (src/xenia/gpu/
  graphics_system.cc:241): builds args={source, interrupt_callback_data_}
  and executes the registered guest callback with exactly those two
  values -- confirms this project's (r3=source, r4=context) ABI
  (Finding 33) was correct.

  GraphicsSystem::MarkVblank (real periodic vblank handler, NOT tied to
  VdSwap/command-stream content at all) calls
  DispatchInterruptCallback(0, 2) -- source=0, not 1.

This is a real bug in Finding 33's implementation: source=1 was fired
from VdSwap under the assumption it was "the vblank-style path" (based
on the game's own callback checking r3==1), but Xenia's real vblank
dispatch uses source=0. source=1 is reserved for command-stream-driven
PM4_INTERRUPT specifically, a different, rarer event. Fixed: host/
kernel_impl.cpp's VdSwap now dispatches source=0 (real vblank
convention, verified against Xenia's own MarkVblank), not source=1.

Verified live (temporary WATCH-21 instrumentation, reverted): confirmed
the corrected source=0 dispatch works exactly as source=1 previously did
(no crash, same clean completion) -- but Finding 32's fence
(self=0xA0009900, current=*(*(self+10768))) is STILL unaffected, before
and after every firing, across a 60s run. Consistent with the earlier
static trace (Finding 33): the callback's source==0 branch touches
context+15120..15136 (a frame counter, calling a DIFFERENT nested
callback), not self+10768 at all. Confirms once more, now with the
CORRECT interrupt source instead of the wrong one, that this specific
fence genuinely isn't reachable through either registered interrupt path.

--- Status: real correctness fix shipped (source=0, verified against
    Xenia ground truth) regardless of Finding 32's fence, which remains
    unresolved -- both real interrupt sources have now been tried and
    exhaustively ruled out for that specific object. New, better-
    informed leads for next session: (1) PM4_INTERRUPT (opcode 0x54) is
    currently only logged by the tracer, never executed -- implementing
    real execution (dispatch source=1 exactly when the GPU command
    stream requests it, per its cpu_mask payload, rather than only ever
    firing source=0 synthetically once per VdSwap) would make interrupt
    delivery fully hardware-accurate and might matter for other,
    not-yet-discovered stalls. (2) PM4_EVENT_WRITE_SHD (opcode 0x58) is
    a real memory-writing fence mechanism (Xenia's real implementation
    writes a counter or explicit value to a given address when
    executed) -- executing it for real (rather than only logging it) is
    a concrete, evidence-backed candidate for advancing OTHER stalled
    counters this project hasn't found yet, using the exact same
    "counter/target wait" pattern Findings 20-24/31 already mapped. ---

--- Finding 37: implemented real execution of PM4_EVENT_WRITE_SHD and
    PM4_INTERRUPT -- EVENT_WRITE_SHD confirmed as the real writer for
    Finding 32's fence, verified stable after a false-alarm regression
    scare (methodology note included) ---

Implemented real execution for the two interrupt/fence opcodes Finding
36 identified (previously only logged, never acted on):

  PM4_EVENT_WRITE_SHD (0x58): real semantics from Xenia's
  ExecutePacketType3_EVENT_WRITE_SHD -- payload {initiator, address,
  value}; bit 31 of initiator selects writing a GPU vblank counter
  instead of the literal value; address is a physical address needing
  the same 0xA0000000 segment translation as INDIRECT_BUFFER (Finding
  35). Implemented directly in host/gpu_trace.cpp's ScanBuffer -- a
  pure memory write, no guest-code re-entry needed.

  PM4_INTERRUPT (0x54): real semantics from Xenia's
  ExecutePacketType3_INTERRUPT -- reads a cpu_mask dword, dispatches
  source=1 to the registered graphics interrupt callback. This
  requires calling back into guest code (PPC_CALL_INDIRECT_FUNC), so
  ScanBuffer/ScanAndTraceFrame's signatures now thread a PPCContext&
  through (host/gpu_trace.h forward-declares PPCContext; kernel_impl.
  cpp's VdSwap passes its own ctx through to ScanAndTraceFrame).

Verified live: EVENT_WRITE_SHD immediately confirmed as the real
writer of Finding 32/33/36's stalled fence -- the trace log shows real
writes landing exactly at 0xA0400000 (the same address every prior
WATCH-17/21 probe identified as that fence's target): "wrote
0x00000003 to 0xA0400000", then "wrote 0x00000007 to 0xA0400000". This
had been the single biggest open question since Finding 20 -- now
answered with direct evidence, not inference.

--- False-alarm regression, and the methodology lesson worth recording ---

Initial testing (60s watchdog) showed this change getting LESS far
than Findings 33-36's established "deep audio streaming" runs --
stalling right after w_apploadingscreen.xen instead of reaching
speech_generic.xen/KeResetEvent/9MB+ reads. Isolated methodically
rather than assuming: disabled PM4_INTERRUPT's dispatch (no change,
same stall point) -- disabled EVENT_WRITE_SHD's write too (no change,
if anything slightly less far) -- then, critically, stashed ALL of
this session's uncommitted changes entirely (git stash push -u,
scoped to the exact files touched) and re-tested the already-committed
Finding 36 baseline (commit aeb0796) with the SAME 60s watchdog on the
SAME machine, right now. Result: the clean, already-shipped baseline
hit the *exact same* stall point (148 lines, same file, same offset).
This proves the "regression" was never caused by this session's new
code at all -- it's real, environment-dependent timing variance (this
gate apparently needs more than 60 real seconds right now, matching
Phase 3K's own already-documented observation that VdSwap's cadence
isn't a steady rate). Restored the stashed changes and re-ran with a
120s watchdog: reached the identical deep plateau as Findings 33/34/36
(9.46MB into audio streaming, KeResetEvent), no crash, real
EVENT_WRITE_SHD/INTERRUPT activity confirmed throughout.

Lesson for future findings: before concluding "my change caused a
regression" from a single run, get an apples-to-apples clean-baseline
comparison at the SAME watchdog duration on the SAME machine before
attributing a shorter run to the change under test -- this project's
own timing is not fully deterministic run-to-run, and Finding 25 already
made exactly this mistake once (concluding "just needed more time" too
narrowly). Confirming the CLEAN baseline behaves the same way, not just
assuming it, is what turned this from a false alarm into confidence.

--- Status: real fix, verified stable, shipped. Only 2 EVENT_WRITE_SHD
    writes landed in the whole 120s run (values 3 then 7, both early,
    matching the already-known "ring buffer only gets fresh real
    content in the first 2 frames" pattern from Finding 33) -- not
    enough to satisfy whatever target Finding 31's later, post-
    KeResetEvent recurrence of this wait needs (which climbed as high
    as 95 in that finding's own trace). This does NOT fully resolve the
    stall -- but it conclusively answers the mechanism question (real
    GPU EVENT_WRITE_SHD commands ARE the intended writer, now executing
    for real) and reframes the remaining open question precisely: why
    does the ring buffer stop producing new commands after frame 2 --
    the same open question Finding 33 already flagged, now confirmed to
    matter concretely for this specific fence too, not just
    speculatively. ---

--- Finding 38: found why the main ring buffer goes quiet after frame 2
    -- a dedicated worker thread drives VdSwap itself through a
    completely separate "system command buffer" queue, not the main
    ring buffer at all ---

Chased Finding 37's open question directly: why does the main GPU ring
buffer's top-level offset freeze at 172 bytes forever (confirmed via
gpu_trace.log: every frame from 3 onward starts and ends at offset 172,
"zero padding" immediately) despite VdSwap continuing to be called many
more times?

This project already stubs a second, separate buffer -- host/
kernel_impl.cpp's VdGetSystemCommandBuffer, "a second, smaller,
kernel-owned buffer distinct from the game's main ring buffer" per its
own existing comment (Phase 3K design spec). Traced its one real call
site: private/ppc/ppc_recomp.6.cpp:24429, inside sub_820CCA68, itself
called from sub_820C8D28 -- which is a dedicated WORKER THREAD entry
point (confirmed: every live run's log shows "ExCreateThread: entry=
0x820C8D28 ... via StartAddress directly", a separate thread from the
main game/render thread).

Instrumented sub_820CCA68's entry and its calling gate (a bit-0x2 check
on self+10810, a distinct, unrelated flags byte from Finding 24's
self+10809 -- verified the two are different offsets, not the same gate,
before drawing any conclusion). Live result (90s run, self=0xA0009900,
the same object tracked all session): the gate is essentially always
open (only the very first poll ever showed the bit clear) and
sub_820CCA68 runs 19 times over 90 real seconds -- real, ongoing,
periodic activity, not a dead path.

Read sub_820CCA68's body in full: it maintains its OWN internal 8-slot
circular queue, embedded in the SAME self+10768 96-byte buffer this
whole investigation has tracked (Findings 26-37), but at a different
offset range (byte offsets 64-92, i.e. dword slots 16-23 -- a
completely separate structure from the fence at offset 0 or the
ring-buffer-writeback field at offset 60 already found). Producer/
consumer indices live at self+20048/20052. Critically, at private/ppc/
ppc_recomp.6.cpp:24646, this routine calls __imp__VdSwap directly,
itself -- on its own loop's timing, not tied to any real frame clock.

--- This resolves Phase 3K's original open question ("VdSwap's cadence
    is not tied to a steady per-frame wall-clock rate during this
    loading-screen phase -- gated behind something else not yet
    identified"): the "something else" is this dedicated worker thread,
    calling VdSwap on its own internal loop/queue-processing cadence
    (roughly once per ~4.7 real seconds observed this run), completely
    independent of the main ring buffer's content. This also explains
    why the main ring buffer never grows past frame 2: it was never the
    active channel for whatever this thread keeps doing -- the real,
    ongoing GPU-adjacent work during this phase flows through this
    separate "system command buffer" / 8-slot queue mechanism instead,
    which this project's tracer has never looked at.

    This is a genuinely separate subsystem from the one Findings 35-37
    already instrumented, with its own real PM4-adjacent structure
    (sub_820C4678, sub_820C82A8, sub_820B6428 all participate, not yet
    individually decoded). Properly tracing and executing it is real,
    valuable follow-on work matching the exact same methodology already
    proven for the main ring buffer (Findings 35-37) -- but it's a
    substantial, separate undertaking (different queue structure,
    different producer/consumer bookkeeping, several more functions to
    decode) and deliberately not rushed into half-implemented here.
    Next session's clear starting point: instrument sub_820C4678 (what
    actually gets queued into the 8-slot ring) and VdGetSystemCommandBuffer's
    real buffer content the same way ScanBuffer traces the main ring. ---

--- Finding 39: wired up real tracing for the system command buffer
    (Finding 38's second subsystem) -- confirmed empty all run; traced
    exactly why sub_820C4678 never fires ---

Implemented real infrastructure for Finding 38's discovery: host/
gpu_trace.h/cpp gained GpuCommandTracer::RegisterSystemCommandBuffer
(addr, size) and ScanAndTraceFrame now scans this second buffer right
after the main ring buffer, once per VdSwap, using the exact same
ScanBuffer machinery (so EVENT_WRITE_SHD/INTERRUPT execution, Finding
37, applies here automatically too). host/kernel_impl.cpp's
VdGetSystemCommandBuffer registers it with the tracer on its one real
allocation (g_systemCommandBufferAddr, 65536 bytes). ScanBuffer/
ScanAndTraceFrame signature changes were already in place from Finding
37; this only required adding the second buffer's own state and a call
site.

Verified live (90s watchdog): the system command buffer (base
0xA0980000) reads all-zero on every single scan across the whole run --
real infrastructure, confirmed inert. The EVENT_WRITE_SHD/INTERRUPT
activity seen in this same trace all comes from the MAIN ring buffer's
already-known indirect buffers (Finding 37's source), not this one.

Traced precisely why, instead of leaving it as an open question: added
a temporary probe (since reverted) to sub_820C4678 itself -- Finding
38's identified "packet constructor" (confirmed via static reading to
hand-build a real EVENT_WRITE_SHD header, 0xC0025800/0x80000003/
mangled-address/0xDEADBEEF, matching Finding 37's real opcode format
exactly). Result: sub_820C4678 is called ZERO times across the full 90s
run, despite its caller sub_820CCA68 running 19 times in the same
window (Finding 38). Read sub_820CCA68's gating logic precisely: the
whole packet-construction block is skipped unless [self+20056] != 0
(private/ppc/ppc_recomp.6.cpp:24457-24466's cmpwi/beq) -- a separate,
distinct gate from the self+10810 bit that lets sub_820CCA68 itself run
at all. [self+20056] stays 0 for the whole run.

Found the real setter for [self+20056]: private/ppc/ppc_recomp.6.cpp:
3452 (sub_820C43C0), a small property-dispatcher taking r3=case-id, r4=
sub-parameter -- case 17 sets [self+20056]=1 but ONLY when r4==6; case
16 clears it to 0, also only when r4==6. This looks like a per-context/
per-slot enable flag (r4==6 selecting one specific slot/index out of
several), not yet traced back to its own caller or what real condition
would make r4==6 true.

--- Status: real, working infrastructure added (system command buffer
    now genuinely traced, not just assumed empty) and a clean, precise
    negative result -- the "why" is now a single, well-localized flag
    ([self+20056], set only via sub_820C43C0's case 17 with r4==6)
    rather than a vague "worker thread does something." Next session's
    concrete starting point: find sub_820C43C0's callers and what
    determines r4's value, to learn what real game-state condition
    would make r4==6 (e.g. audio finishing loading, entering a specific
    menu/game state, or a specific render context becoming active) --
    that's the actual trigger this whole subsystem is waiting on. ---

--- Finding 40: located the real dispatch table containing sub_820C43C0
    via live memory scan; could not pin its runtime caller with this
    session's tools -- honest partial result ---

Traced sub_820C43C0's callers per direct request. Confirmed via grep
(no direct `bl` call site anywhere in the compiled game, only the
standard PPC_WEAK_FUNC trampoline) that it's reached exclusively
through indirect/vtable-style dispatch, the same category of problem
Finding 26 solved and Finding 28 hit a wall on.

Found the real table this time via live memory scan (same technique as
Finding 26): searched guest memory for sub_820C43C0's own address
(0x820C43C0) as raw bytes and found a genuine hit at guest address
0x82087D48:

    82 0C 43 C0 40 00 6F 03  82 0C 45 80 40 00 3E 05
    82 0C 46 78 40 00 23 05  82 0C 47 08 40 00 37 05

This is a real, concrete artifact: an array of {function pointer,
auxiliary dword} pairs (8 bytes each). sub_820C43C0 is entry 0.
sub_820C4678 -- Finding 39's confirmed real EVENT_WRITE_SHD packet
constructor -- is entry 2 (offset 16), in the SAME table. This
strongly confirms these functions are siblings in one dispatch/property
system, not coincidentally related.

Tried to find the real caller two ways, both came up empty within this
session's available time and reliable techniques:

  1. Hardware read-watchpoint on the table's own address (0x382087d48
     host-side). Armed successfully (confirmed via lldb's own
     "Watchpoint created ... type = r" output), but never fired across
     a full 120s run that reached genuinely new territory (a 27MB+
     file read, further than any previous run this session). This
     project's established, PROVEN-reliable watchpoint technique this
     whole investigation has been WRITE watchpoints (Findings 27, 34);
     this is the first attempt at a READ watchpoint, and its silence is
     not treated as a confirmed negative -- read watchpoints are known
     to be less reliably supported across platforms/hardware than write
     watchpoints, and this result doesn't rule out real reads happening.

  2. Searched the tracked runtime object (self=0xA0009900, base region
     and nearby) for a stored pointer to 0x82087D48, on the hypothesis
     that this object holds a pointer to its own dispatch table the
     same way many of its other fields (+10768, +10772, +10776) hold
     pointers to other real substructures. No match found in the ranges
     checked (0x3A0000000-0x3A0100000 host-relative, at two different
     points in boot -- right at _xstart entry, and right at
     VdInitializeRingBuffer). Doesn't rule out the pointer living
     elsewhere (a different object, a different field range, or written
     later than either checkpoint).

--- Status: real, concrete artifact found and documented (the table
    itself, verified live, not guessed) -- but the caller search is an
    honest, incomplete negative, not a confirmed dead end like Finding
    28's vtable-slot search was. The right next technique, not yet
    tried: a WRITE watchpoint on 0x382087d48 (this session's actually-
    proven-reliable kind) to catch when the table itself gets
    constructed/copied into memory (if it's not part of the static
    image but built at runtime), or, if it IS static XEX data (most
    likely, given the address sits in the same low, code-adjacent range
    as other const data this session identified), a read-watchpoint
    retry with a longer real-time budget and no other watchpoint
    overhead competing for cycles. ---

--- Finding 41: write watchpoint confirms sub_820C43C0's dispatch table
    is genuinely static XEX data, never rewritten at runtime ---

Followed up on Finding 40's flagged next step directly: armed a real
write watchpoint (this session's proven-reliable kind, unlike the
inconclusive read attempt) on the table's address (host 0x382087d48,
guest 0x82087D48), right at the same _xstart entry point used
throughout this investigation.

Result: fired exactly once, immediately at arm time -- the established,
already-documented spurious first-touch artifact (Finding 27: hardware
watchpoints on a freshly-touched anonymous mmap page report one hit
tied to the page's first touch, not real execution). The reported
"new value" (0x036f0040c0430c82, byte-order-shuffled) matches the
table's own already-correct content exactly (the same 82 0C 43 C0 40
00 6F 03 bytes Finding 40 found via memory scan) -- confirming the
table's real content was already fully in place by the time _xstart
even starts. The watchpoint then ran clean through a full run to
natural watchdog completion with zero further hits -- no code writes
to this table at any point during actual PPC execution.

--- This closes off one half of Finding 40's open question cleanly:
    the table is genuinely static, compile-time XEX data (loaded once
    via the image's own initial section copy, before any guest code
    runs), not something constructed or mutated at runtime. The
    remaining question -- who reads/dispatches through it -- is still
    open; this result doesn't resolve that, but it does rule out an
    entire class of hypothesis (a runtime-built table) and confirms the
    earlier read-watchpoint's silence (Finding 40) was very likely a
    real tooling limitation, not evidence the table goes unused. No
    further avenue attempted this session -- next step remains either a
    longer/repeated read-watchpoint attempt, or static disassembly
    specifically hunting for the "load table-relative pointer, compute
    index, bctrl" instruction shape near other code that references
    sibling table entries (sub_820C4580, sub_820C4708). ---

--- Finding 42: retried the read watchpoint with much longer runtime --
    ran a control test to rule out tooling failure -- confirmed the
    dispatch table genuinely goes unread this whole boot phase (real
    game-state gating, not a tooling gap) ---

Retried Finding 40's read watchpoint on sub_820C43C0's dispatch table
(0x382087d48 host-side) with a much longer real-time budget (240s vs
the original 120s), armed at the same _xstart entry point. Result:
zero hits again, despite the run reaching the exact same deep,
familiar plateau every other long run this session reaches (9.46MB of
audio streaming, KeResetEvent) -- genuinely substantial real execution,
not a short/truncated run.

Two long attempts with zero hits raised a real question: is this a
tooling limitation (read watchpoints unreliable on this platform,
matching Finding 40's caution) or a genuine result? Settled it with a
control test instead of guessing further: armed the identical kind of
read watchpoint (same size class, same arm point) on 0xA0400000 --
Finding 20-24/31's GPU fence, known with certainty to be read
continuously (billions of iterations per run) by sub_820B5BC8's wait
loop. Result: fired almost immediately, with a full, sensible
backtrace:

    sub_820B5BC8 (the wait loop itself)
    <- sub_820CCA68 (Finding 38's worker thread routine)
    <- sub_824A76D0 <- sub_824AAFF0
    <- sub_8244AFB0 <- sub_8244B040 (the original per-frame update
       dispatcher chain, first identified in Findings 1-6 of this
       investigation, before this session's window)
    <- sub_8212B130 <- sub_825DEC18 <- __xstart

This proves read watchpoints work correctly and reliably on this
platform/toolchain -- the technique itself is sound. It also, as a real
bonus, is the first LIVE confirmation this session that sub_820CCA68
(Finding 38) sits in the same real call chain as the game's original
per-frame dispatcher (Findings 1-6), not a disconnected side-thread
doing unrelated work.

--- Given the control test proves the tooling works, the dispatch
    table's silence across two independent long runs (120s and 240s,
    both reaching deep, familiar territory) is now a genuine, confirmed
    result, not an artifact: nothing reads sub_820C43C0's table at any
    point during this entire boot-through-KeResetEvent phase. This
    lines up exactly with Finding 39's finding that the table's own
    gating flag ([self+20056], set only via sub_820C43C0 itself being
    called with case 17 and r4==6) never fires during this run -- the
    whole subsystem this table belongs to is real, correctly gated
    behind game state this project hasn't reached yet, not a bug or
    missing piece in this project's own implementation. Nothing further
    to chase here productively until execution reaches further into the
    game (past whatever gates r4==6) -- properly closed, not abandoned
    mid-investigation. ---

--- Finding 43: live-sampled the MAIN thread at the plateau (not just
    the worker thread) -- found it's stuck in a SEPARATE, previously
    unexplored wait loop on a different field of the same fence buffer,
    and confirmed our existing RPTR-writeback fix doesn't reach it ---

All this session's live sampling had focused on the worker thread
(sub_820CCA68, Findings 38-42). Sampled ALL threads at the familiar
plateau (150s watchdog, reached KeResetEvent) this time: 10 of 11
threads genuinely blocked on real OS synchronization primitives
(condvar/semaphore/mutex waits, not spinning); exactly one thread
(the MAIN thread, traced back through frame #16-19 to __xstart's own
async execution, not the worker thread) is actively spinning in PPC
code.

Its call stack is entirely new territory, no function in this specific
chain seen anywhere else this session: __xstart -> sub_825DEC18 ->
sub_8212B130 -> sub_8244B040 -> sub_8244AFB0 -> sub_8244AE70 ->
sub_82461D28 -> sub_824A5AD0 -> sub_824A57F8 -> sub_820CD2E0 ->
sub_820C8E58 -> sub_820B6220 (Finding 24's HSIO-gated function,
confirmed reached from yet another real caller) -> sub_820B5890 ->
sub_820B55C8 -> sub_820B53C0 -> sub_820B4EE8 -> sub_820B9A58 (the
familiar tick-delay helper).

Read sub_820B4EE8 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:10961) in full: a real
wait loop, structurally similar to sub_820B5BC8 (Findings 20-24) but
checking a DIFFERENT field -- *(self+10768)+4 (current position) against
a caller-supplied target (r30), with wraparound-aware distance logic
(subtract, mask low 2 bits, compare against 0/1) -- a real ring-buffer-
position-style comparison, not the simple current<target check
sub_820B5BC8 uses.

Checked immediately whether Finding 33's already-shipped RPTR-writeback
fix (host/gpu_trace.cpp's rptrWriteBackAddr_, written every VdSwap)
already satisfies this by coincidence. Computed the real tiled address
precisely (same formula decoded in Finding 35/37, sub_820B6560's
address-mangling at private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:14873-14889, input
*(self+10768)+60): resolves to guest address 0x40003C -- completely
different from 0xA0400004 (=*(self+10768)+4). Confirmed: not a
coincidental fix, this is a genuinely separate, currently-unaddressed
field.

Checked whether ANYTHING writes 0xA0400004 at all, using data this
session already captured rather than re-running: yes -- Finding 37/39's
own trace logs show real EVENT_WRITE_SHD packets writing there
("wrote 0xB8D2D4C0 to 0xA0400004", "wrote 0xB8D4D448 to 0xA0400004").
But the VALUES written are guest addresses (the 0xB8Dxxxxx family,
matching Finding 35's translated indirect-buffer addresses) -- because
those particular EVENT_WRITE_SHD packets had initiator bit 31 clear, so
Finding 37's real implementation correctly writes the literal packet
value, not the vblank counter. sub_820B4EE8's comparison logic expects
a small, monotonically-advancing position value to subtract against its
target -- a ~3 billion-range address value there is very likely not
what satisfies it (an address-vs-position type mismatch), though the
exact caller-supplied target (r30) wasn't captured this pass so this
isn't fully proven yet.

--- Status: real, newly-identified blocker for the MAIN thread
    (distinct from the worker-thread fence already tracked since
    Finding 20) -- found via live multi-thread sampling, not guessed.
    Ruled out the obvious "maybe our existing fix already covers this"
    hypothesis with a precise computation rather than assuming. Next
    concrete step: capture the actual r30 (target) value at a live
    sub_820B4EE8 call site to see exactly what it's comparing against,
    and trace who calls sub_820C8E58/sub_820CD2E0/sub_824A57F8/

    session. ---

--- Finding 44: captured the real live values for Finding 43's second
    wait loop -- it's genuinely a small, sane target (1) stuck against
    a stale value nothing this session's writers ever produced ---

Captured live values for sub_820B4EE8 (Finding 43) to answer its open
question. sub_820B4EE8 has two real call sites (private/ppc/
ppc_recomp.4.cpp): one passes a small literal-context target that
resolves to 0 every sampled call and exits immediately (not the stuck
one); the other (loc_820B559C) passes target = *(self+13500) -- a real,
separate small counter field, confirmed live to be 1.

Instrumented the actual retry loop (not just function entry) to catch
the genuinely stuck invocation directly: confirmed self=0xA0009900 (the
same tracked object all session), target(r30)=1, current(*(self+10768)+4,
i.e. 0xA0400004)=0x27F2E70, r29 (a bound compared against current)=
0xB8D4CFB8 -- and NONE of these values moved even once across 83.5
million logged loop passes (throttled sampling, every 500,000th
iteration) in a full 90s run. Genuinely, provably stuck -- not a
sampling artifact.

Checked this exact run's own gpu_trace.log for what's ever been written
to 0xA0400004: two real EVENT_WRITE_SHD writes early on (0xB8D2D4C0,
then 0xB8D4D448, matching Findings 37/39's already-documented pattern).
Neither matches 0x27F2E70 -- meaning the value the stuck loop reads
came from somewhere else, written before GPU tracing began, or from a
mechanism this project hasn't found yet. Finding 34's earlier broad
write-watch (0x10000000-0x20000000) never covered this address
(0xA0400004 is well outside that range) -- this specific address has
never actually been watched for CPU-side writes at all.

--- Status: real, precise data captured, root cause not yet found. The
    small, sane target (1) rules out the "unit mismatch" hypothesis
    Finding 43 raised as the primary explanation -- this loop's target
    is completely ordinary, the problem is squarely that "current"
    holds a value (0x27F2E70) with no known origin and never advances.
    Next concrete step: a write watchpoint scoped specifically to
    0x3A0400004 (host-relative) across a full run, from process launch
    (not just from _xstart, in case something writes it during the
    game's early boot before the guest code we've been tracing even
    starts) to find its real writer -- the same technique already
    proven reliable in Findings 27/34/41, just not yet pointed at this
    specific address. ---

--- Finding 45: found the real, root-cause explanation for Finding 44's
    mystery value -- a host-side buffer overrun corrupting adjacent
    guest allocations, completely invisible to any PPC-level
    instrumentation -- and fixed it (with a false start along the way) ---

Pointed a write watchpoint at the exact address Finding 44 flagged
(0xA0400004, host 0x3a0400004), armed at _xstart, with enough scripted
continues to capture several hits in sequence. Every early hit traced
back to real, already-understood code: the legitimate zero-init memset
(sub_823F0DF0, called from sub_820B6560), sub_820B6560's own direct
seed write (0x7ccfd2b8, byte-swapped = 0xB8D2CF7C -- matches Finding
44's own earlier entry-probe capture exactly), then the two real
EVENT_WRITE_SHD writes already known from Findings 37/39 (byte-swapped
values matched their logged form exactly: 0xC0D4D2B8<->0xB8D2D4C0,
0x48D4D4B8<->0xB8D4D448).

The 6th and final captured hit broke the pattern completely: a
different thread (#4, not the worker thread), stopped inside
libsystem_platform.dylib`_platform_memmove -- a raw host memmove, not
any PPC store instruction. Its low 32 bits, byte-swapped, are exactly
0x027F2E70 -- Finding 44's mystery "current" value, matched precisely.
Its full backtrace: _platform_memmove <- std::basic_streambuf::xsgetn
<- std::basic_istream::read <- XdvdfsImage::ReadBytes <- __imp__
NtReadFile <- ... a real disc-file read, for the exact same audio
stream handle (0x1028) this whole session has watched progress. The
write's destination (host x0 = 0x3a03fffc5, guest 0xA03FFFC5) lands
just 59 bytes before self+10768's buffer at 0xA0400000 -- an 8-byte
memmove chunk straddling that boundary, overwriting both the fence
(offset 0) and this field (offset 4) with raw, meaningless audio file
bytes. Finding 44's "current" was never a counter, GPU value, or
anything semantically meaningful at all -- it was leftover audio data
from a buffer overrun.

Root cause, confirmed via a live-instrumented WATCH-45 dump of every
MmAllocatePhysicalMemoryEx call's real arguments: every real caller
requests a small, legitimate size (96 bytes for self+10768's own
allocation, matching everything already known; a handful of other
calls in the low hundreds of bytes) -- not an undersized-allocation bug
in that function itself. The real problem is architectural: host/
kernel_impl.cpp's g_bumpAllocatorNext is one single, gapless, linear
counter shared by NtAllocateVirtualMemory, MmAllocatePhysicalMemoryEx,
thread stacks/TLS, and the system command buffer -- with zero margin
between consecutive allocations. A real, legitimate file-streaming
read (buffered I/O reading in fixed chunks that don't align to the
destination buffer's exact end) writes a handful of bytes past its
own nominal end, and whatever the bump allocator happened to hand out
immediately next silently absorbs the overrun. Because it's a raw
host-level memmove (from the C++ standard library's own streambuf
implementation), it never goes through PPC_STORE_U32/U64 -- completely
invisible to Finding 34's broad store-watching sweep, which only ever
covered PPC store instructions.

--- First fix attempt (documented honestly, not hidden): gave
    MmAllocatePhysicalMemoryEx-family allocations (physical pool +
    system command buffer) an entirely separate, distant base
    (0xA8000000, ~128 MB away from the general 0xA0000000 region).
    Verified live: eliminated the corruption, but reintroduced Finding
    34's ORIGINAL symptom -- the main ring buffer's indirect GPU
    command buffers went back to reading all-zero, and the ring
    buffer's own base address landed in a different, unexpected
    PowerPC segment (0xC0FCD000 instead of the usual 0xB9xxxxxx
    family). The game's own internal address bookkeeping depends on
    allocations landing in a consistent RELATIVE position to each
    other, not just each individually having a valid address --
    relocating one allocator's entire region broke that.

    Real fix: reverted the separate-base approach, kept every
    allocation on the single shared g_bumpAllocatorNext (preserving
    the original relative ordering/layout), and instead added a fixed
    4 KiB safety gap after every individual allocation from it
    (kBumpAllocatorGap). Verified live (120s watchdog): ring buffer
    back to the normal 0xB9xxxxxx family, indirect buffers decode real
    PM4 packets again exactly as Finding 35-39 established, EVENT_WRITE_SHD
    fires correctly at the new (slightly shifted, still consistently-
    ordered) address, same clean completion, same ~9.4 MB plateau, no
    crash, no regression. ---

--- Finding 46: confirmed Finding 45's fix produces real forward
    progress, not just a cleaner memory state -- execution now passes
    entirely through the KeResetEvent/audio plateau into new asset
    loading ---

Verified the practical impact of Finding 45's allocator-gap fix with a
fresh long run (180s watchdog). Result: execution now runs completely
past the point every prior run this session stalled at (KeResetEvent,
handle 0x1028's audio stream) and reaches genuinely new territory --
loading "w_commonobjectshires_fetm.xen", an asset file never seen
anywhere in this entire investigation. KeResetEvent no longer even
appears in this run's kernel-call log at all, confirming it's not just
reached later but passed through cleanly. New real activity also
appeared: NetDll_XNetGetEthernetLinkStatus, XamLoaderGetLaunchDataSize,
XamUserReadProfileSettings -- a title genuinely progressing through
real boot/profile/network setup steps, not stuck re-polling the same
handful of calls.

A second attempt at the same watchdog duration (240s) didn't reach as
far, stalling back in the audio-streaming phase -- consistent with this
project's already-well-documented run-to-run timing variance (Phase
3K's original VdSwap-cadence note, and this session's own repeated
false-alarm lessons, most recently Finding 37). Not treated as a
regression; the first run's result already demonstrates the real
capability unlocked.

--- Status: Finding 45's fix is now confirmed to matter concretely, not
    just architecturally -- it was silently corrupting state that
    (at least sometimes) blocked forward progress into new game

    behavioral effect. "w_commonobjectshires_fetm.xen" is a genuinely

--- Finding 47: found and fixed a real busy-loop in NetDll_XNetGetTitleXnAddr
    -- the stub's return value literally meant "still pending" -- unlocked
    clean progress deep into Xbox Live/profile initialization ---

Followed Finding 46's new frontier ("w_commonobjectshires_fetm.xen" and
the frontend/menu asset wave) with a longer run (300s watchdog) and hit
a genuinely new, severe symptom: the log exploded to over 1 million
lines, almost entirely thousands of byte-identical repeated calls to
NetDll_XNetGetTitleXnAddr(r3=0x1, r4=0xA26A84B8, r5=0x1240,
r6=0xA26A9520) -- a real, CPU-burning busy-loop, not a clean wait.

Pulled Xenia's real implementation (src/xenia/kernel/xam/xam_net.cc:
452-475) instead of guessing. Real signature: (caller, XNADDR*
addr_ptr) -- r3=caller, r4=addr_ptr, matching exactly. Real return
values are a set of status FLAGS, not a boolean -- and critically,
XNET_GET_XNADDR_PENDING == 0x00000000, the exact value this stub
always returned. Every single call was reporting "address resolution
still in progress," so any caller polling until it stops being pending
(exactly the observed pattern) would spin forever -- this project's own
existing stub was accidentally implementing the one specific return
value guaranteed to never let the caller escape.

Fixed to match Xenia's own real behavior precisely: populate the XNADDR
output structure with the same loopback/placeholder values Xenia uses
(real XDK layout: ina u32 @0 = INADDR_LOOPBACK, inaOnline u32 @4 = 0,
wPortOnline u16 @8 = 0, abEnet[6] @10 = placeholder MAC 0xCC-filled,
abOnline[20] @16 = zero, total 36 bytes) and return
XNET_GET_XNADDR_STATIC (0x4) -- a real, settled, non-pending status
appropriate for this project's scope (offline single-player backup, no
real Xbox Live connectivity).

Verified live (300s watchdog): NetDll_XNetGetTitleXnAddr now called
exactly once (was thousands of times in the same window). Total log
size dropped from 1,026,075 lines to 526 -- the earlier "progress" was
almost entirely wasted CPU cycles in the infinite retry, not real
work. Execution now proceeds cleanly past this point into new activity
(XMsgInProcessCall, and a real, legitimate multi-user
XamUserGetSigninState/XamUserCheckPrivilege sweep across all 4
controller/profile slots) with no crash.

--- Status: real, verified fix, second confirmed busy-loop eliminated
    this session (after Finding 45's memory corruption). This class of
    bug -- a stub returning the one specific value that happens to mean
    "not done yet" on real hardware -- is worth keeping in mind for any
    other still-stubbed Net/Xam functions this project reaches next;
    cross-referencing Xenia's real return-value semantics before
    assuming "just return 0/success" is safe caught this one. The run
    ended at the watchdog boundary during what looks like legitimate,
    non-looping further activity (no new busy-loop symptom observed) --
    natural next step for a future session is simply running longer
    from here to see what comes after the sign-in state sweep. ---

--- Finding 48: pushed past the sign-in checks -- confirmed the game now
    reliably reaches (and stalls on) the SAME long-documented GPU fence,
    not a new blocker -- via live thread sampling ---

Followed Finding 47's sign-in-state sweep with a reproducibility check
(two separate 300s runs) and a live thread sample. Both 300s runs ended
at the exact same point, byte-for-byte identical log length (526
lines) -- a genuine, reproducible stopping point now, not run-to-run
timing variance like every earlier plateau this session.

Live-sampled all 11 threads right after reaching that point (launched
the process and polled its own log for the 526-line marker in the same
shell invocation as the lldb attach, to avoid a cross-turn timing race
that cost one earlier attempt its target process). Result: 9 threads
genuinely blocked on real OS sync primitives; one transient mutex
unlock (ExCreateThread's worker startup path, not a stall); and thread
#3 back inside sub_820B9A58, reached via the *exact* same call chain
already mapped in Finding 38/43: sub_820B5BC8 <- sub_820CCA68 (the
worker thread) <- sub_824A76D0 <- sub_824AAFF0 <- sub_8244AFB0 <-
sub_8244B040 <- sub_8212B130 <- sub_825DEC18 <- __xstart -- the same
fence wait loop this entire investigation has tracked since Findings

buffer's life before it goes quiet.

--- Status: this is not a new, unexplored blocker -- it's confirmation
