--- Finding 49: "implement the system command buffer subsystem" isn't a
    separate task -- confirmed it's gated by the exact same unresolved
    fence, at a precise, newly-mapped call site ---

Checked directly whether Finding 39's gate ([self+20056] != 0, needed
for sub_820C4678's real packet construction to ever run) might finally
flip true now that Findings 45/47's fixes let execution reach much
further (past the sign-in sweep). Instrumented the read site (private/
ppc/ppc_recomp.6.cpp:24457, since reverted) and ran a full 300s window.

Result: only ONE sample ever printed ([self+20056]=0, at the very first
poll), not because the gate stayed permanently closed across many real
checks -- because sub_820CCA68 itself only gets called ONCE and never
returns to check it again. It gets stuck immediately, nested inside its
own call to sub_820B5BC8 (confirmed: private/ppc/ppc_recomp.6.cpp:
25220-25253, sub_820CCA68's own direct call to the fence-wait helper,
target = *(self+13520), refreshed from *(self+10780) after every
successful/timed-out wait -- the exact same growing-checkpoint pattern
Finding 31 already mapped for the original fence). This matches
Finding 48's live backtrace exactly: thread #3 was found stuck inside
sub_820B9A58, reached via sub_820B5BC8 <- sub_820CCA68.

--- This settles the question precisely: the "system command buffer"
    isn't an independent piece of unimplemented work sitting parallel
    to the main fence issue -- it's DOWNSTREAM of it. sub_820CCA68 (the
    one and only real path to sub_820C4678's packet construction, and
    to VdSwap itself, Finding 38) cannot get far enough into its own
    body to even re-check the gate, because its very first real action
    is waiting on the same never-fully-advancing fence every other
    finding this session has centered on. All the real infrastructure
    for tracing/executing this second buffer already exists and works
    (Finding 39's ScanBuffer reuse, automatically gets EVENT_WRITE_SHD/
    INTERRUPT execution for free) -- there is nothing left to build
    there. The one and only real remaining lever, confirmed again from
    a completely different angle this session, is Finding 38's original
    question: why does the main ring buffer's real GPU command content
    stop after its first two frames. Every path this session has
    explored -- the worker thread's own fence wait, the system command
    buffer's gate, this newly-mapped self+13520 checkpoint -- all
    terminate at that same unanswered question. That is the real next
    investigation target, not a parallel one. ---

--- Finding 50: implemented VdSwap's real kernel-side write contract
    (confirmed against Xenia) -- a real, correct fix, but confirmed it
    does NOT explain the ring-buffer-quiet mystery ---

Pulled Xenia's real VdSwap_entry (src/xenia/kernel/xboxkrnl/
xboxkrnl_video.cc:352-436) and found something this project's VdSwap
had never done: on real hardware, VdSwap isn't just a "present" call --
the KERNEL writes real PM4 content into a small region the caller
passes in (buffer_ptr, r3; 64 dwords). Confirmed the specific real
content: a TYPE0 packet writing the caller's 6-dword texture fetch
constant (fetch_ptr, r4) to register 0x4800 (verified against Xenia's
register_table.inc:2697, XE_GPU_REG_SHADER_CONSTANT_FETCH_00_0 -- this
exact register already appears in this project's own captured trace
data from Finding 35), then fills the remainder with real TYPE2 NOPs.
This project's VdSwap only ever passively read whatever was already in
the ring buffer -- never performed this side of the real contract.
Implemented it (host/kernel_impl.cpp), skipping Xenia's own internal
PM4_XE_SWAP marker and physical-address frontbuffer patching (both
confirmed Xenia-specific bookkeeping for its own async multi-threaded
GPU emulation, not part of the real hardware contract, and not needed
for this project's synchronous tracer).

Tested the natural hypothesis directly: does writing this real content
finally give the quiet ring buffer something new to parse on later
frames? Captured the real live value of buffer_ptr across 19 real
VdSwap calls to check. Answer: no -- buffer_ptr is never inside the
traced ring buffer at all. It's consistently several megabytes BELOW
the ring buffer's own registered base (first sample: 0xB8D31570 vs.
ring buffer base 0xB9131000, a ~4 MB gap), growing substantially every
single call -- the same 0xB8Dxxxxx address family already seen for
other per-frame scratch/indirect-buffer data (Finding 35), not a
rotating slot within the main command stream.

--- Status: real, correct fix, kept (matches the documented Xbox 360
    kernel contract regardless of whether it explains this particular
    mystery -- implementing real behavior is not conditional on it
    being the answer to the current question). Verified live: no
    crash, no regression, same clean completion. Rules out this
    specific, well-motivated hypothesis for "why does the ring buffer
    go quiet after frame 2" with hard evidence rather than leaving it
    untested. buffer_ptr's real target is a separate, large, steadily-
    growing per-frame allocation -- worth its own investigation
    eventually (what the game does with that scratch data, and whether
    IT eventually gets folded into a real ring-buffer submission
    somewhere this project hasn't traced yet), but not the same
    question as the ring buffer's own quiet content. The real "why does
    the ring buffer stop growing" question, now tested against three
    separate hypotheses across this session (Finding 38's worker-thread
    gate, Finding 49's fence-blocks-everything explanation, and this
    finding's VdSwap-content-location theory) remains open. ---

--- Finding 51: TRUE root cause of the whole Finding 44/45 corruption class
    found and fixed -- MmAllocatePhysicalMemoryEx was ignoring the real
    requested size, and the game runs its own ~400 MB heap inside the
    region it thinks it got ---

Re-checked the fence block live at the Finding 48 plateau (write-watchpoint
methodology, Findings 27/34/41/45): the 96-byte fence block (this run at
0xA0415000) was corrupted AGAIN mid-run despite Finding 45's 4 KiB
allocator gap -- raw audio-file bytes over both the fence counter (offset
0) and the position field (offset 4), with the same
XdvdfsImage::ReadBytes/memmove backtrace as Finding 45's capture. The gap
fix had only narrowed the window, not removed the cause. (This also
retroactively explains Finding 32's "garbage 0x44100000 late in a long
run" sample, which was dismissed at the time as a stress artifact.)

Added the destination buffer pointer to NtReadFile's log line (kept -- it's
generally useful) and captured the real allocation-vs-read layout:
handle 0x1028 (speech_generic.xen, 9.4 MB) streams 256 KiB chunks to
consecutive addresses 0xA0374000 + n*0x40000 -- a contiguous multi-MB
region matching NO logged allocation. The game believes it owns a huge
contiguous buffer there. The source: the title's very FIRST
MmAllocatePhysicalMemoryEx call requests r4=0x19000000 (~400 MB) -- the
classic "grab all physical memory up front and run your own heap inside
it" pattern -- and this project's stub explicitly discarded r4 as
"implausibly large / stale register content" (the old comment even cited
WATCH-45's small-size samples, which simply missed this first call) and
returned a fixed 64 KiB block. Every game-heap allocation past 64 KiB
then silently overlapped every LATER host-side bump allocation --
including the fence block. This one bug explains every corruption
observed across Findings 32/44/45/48.

Fix (host/kernel_impl.cpp): MmAllocatePhysicalMemoryEx now honors the
real size (r4) and alignment (r8), per Xenia's real signature
(MmAllocatePhysicalMemoryEx_entry: flags, region_size, protect, min,
max, alignment), with a 64 KiB floor and a 512 MB sanity cap. The 4 GiB
anonymous guest arena makes the extra pages lazy/free. Verified live:
the 400 MB request is granted at 0xA0164000; every streaming-read
destination lands inside [0xA0164000, 0xB9164000); the ring buffer's
long-known 0xB9131000 base is exactly the game heap's TOP end (heap base
+ ~0x19000000), confirming the game's own internal allocator lays out
GPU structures from the top down -- the 0xB8Dxxxxx/0xB9xxxxxx "address
families" this investigation has tracked all session are simply
game-heap-relative positions. Fence block (now 0xB9405000, outside the
game heap) stays intact for entire runs: EVENT_WRITE_SHD values 3/5/7
land and persist, no garbage. Also confirmed the fence's own 96-byte
allocation arrives as a direct MmAllocatePhysicalMemoryEx call
(size=0x60) -- Finding 32's "pool allocator" reading was the game's thin
wrapper, not a separate slab system.

--- Status: real, verified, shipped root-cause fix. Finding 45's gap
    workaround stays (harmless defense in depth), but its "buffered
    file-streaming read overruns by a few bytes" explanation is
    superseded: the reads were never overrunning their own buffer -- the
    buffer itself was undersized by this allocator bug. ---

--- Finding 52: the game has its own built-in GPU-hang watchdog and
    recovery path -- mapped end to end, and why it silently does nothing
    in this harness ---

Read sub_820B9A58 (the "tick-delay helper" every fence-wait iteration
calls, Findings 20/31/48) in full with fresh eyes: it is not just a
delay -- it is a real hang watchdog. Per iteration it: reads the host
tick (ms) via [r13+256]->+88; compares the fence's current value against
a last-seen copy on the caller's stack struct (+8), resetting a baseline
timestamp (+12) whenever the fence MOVES; consults a heartbeat word at
[r13+256]->+332 vs [self+10760] with [self+10864] as an enable; and if
5000 ms elapse with no progress, calls sub_820BA3C0 (recovery) and
returns 0 (give up) instead of 1 (keep spinning). This precisely
explains Finding 32's outer-loop shape (target climbing +6 every ~258M
iterations): each stuck wait times out after 5 s, the caller re-issues
with a higher target, forever.

sub_820BA3C0 (the recovery): if [self+13064] (a registered hang-notify
callback -- read live: always 0, never registered by this title) is
nonzero, it CPU-completes the fence ([10780]-2 -> fence), sets mode bits
0x1|0x2 in [self+10809] (rerouting future submissions off the ring --
the true meaning of Finding 22-24's bit 0x2: a no-GPU fallback mode, not
a normal path), zeroes the in-flight counter [self+10868], and calls
sub_820C35D0. With [13064]==0 it instead prints two diagnostics
(sub_820B9E18) and executes `twi 31,r0,22` -- a fatal assertion trap on
real hardware. XenonRecomp lowers twi to nothing, so this harness
silently survives the intended crash and just keeps re-waiting. Verified
live (temporary WATCH-51B probe, since reverted): recovery genuinely
fires every ~5 s at the plateau, [13064]==0 every time.

--- Status: no fix here by design -- the game treats a dead GPU as fatal.
    Real value is the corrected model: the HSIO/bit-0x2 sub-thread
    (Findings 22-24) was a failover mode, and the only real path forward
    is making the GPU pipeline actually consume work (Finding 54). ---

--- Finding 53: found and fixed why the graphics interrupt callback's
    vblank branch has never done any work -- it polls a GPU MMIO
    register this harness never backed; seeded the register block with
    Xenia's real read values ---

Read the registered graphics interrupt callback (sub_820B5160, Finding
33) in full. Its vblank branch (source==0) begins by reading GPU MMIO
dword 0x7FC80000+0x6544 (register index 0x1951) and returns WITHOUT
DOING ANYTHING unless bit 0 is set. Xenia ground truth
(graphics_system.cc GraphicsSystem::ReadRegister): `case 0x1951: //
interrupt status -- return 1; // vblank`. This harness backs the MMIO
region with plain zeroed memory, so every vblank dispatch since Finding
33 has been a no-op -- explaining why wiring the callback never moved
anything.

Fix (host/main.cpp, kKernelTickPointerSlot precedent): pre-seed the
register block at boot with Xenia's constant read values -- 0x1951=1
(interrupt status/vblank), 0x194C=0x2D0 (D1MODE_V_COUNTER),
0x1961=0x050002D0 (viewport 1280x720), 0x0F00/0x0F01 (EDRAM timing /
BC control). Verified live (temporary WATCH-51C/D probes, since
reverted): the vblank branch now really runs -- frame counter
[ctx+15124] increments every vblank, and the nested callback [ctx+15120]
(registered: sub_82496C18) fires, signaling an event that wakes a
dedicated vblank thread (sub_82496C28 -- the previously-mysterious
thread #11 blocked in NtWaitForSingleObjectEx every sample since
Finding 43). That thread's own optional callback slot is 0 (nothing
registered yet), so it currently just counts vblanks -- but the whole
interrupt->event->thread chain is real and now alive. Also confirmed
the ISR consults V_COUNTER/viewport via sub_820CC850 (a scanline-timing
"is it safe to flip" helper) -- with the seeded values it computes 100%
scanned, i.e. always safe. Real fix, kept.

--- Finding 54: the ring-quiet mystery is SOLVED mechanically -- the
    submission path switches to an in-flight batching mode after the
    first swap and nothing ever completes that swap; every piece mapped
    to exact functions, one precisely-stated blocker remains ---

With Finding 51's corruption gone, instrumented the whole submission
chain live (temporary WATCH-51 probes, since reverted). The real
pipeline, now fully mapped:

  sub_820B6220 (flush, called from sub_820B5BC8's wait loop when
      target==[10780], from sub_820C8E58 end-of-frame, etc.)
    -> sub_820B5890 (finalize pending command buffer; returns entries)
    -> sub_820B55C8 -> sub_820B53C0 -> sub_820B4EE8 (allocate next
       command-buffer space from the game heap's circular region;
       Finding 43's second wait loop is exactly this allocator waiting
       for the GPU to release space past *(fence+4))
    -> sub_820B5B08 (submit) -> sub_820B56B0 (append 3-dword
       INDIRECT_BUFFER packets [header 0xC0013F00] into the real ring at
       wptr [self+10820], mask [13480], base [13476], then kick the GPU
       via a real MMIO store: PPC_MM_STORE_U32(0x7FC80714, wptr) --
       CP_RB_WPTR, register 0x1C5, matching Xenia's WriteRegister).

  sub_820B5A28 (called per frame-tail) builds the INVALIDATE_STATE +
  EVENT_WRITE_SHD tail into the current command buffer and increments
  [self+10780] by 2 -- the fence expectation counter. (Its direct
  CPU-side fence write really is gated on failover bit 0x2, Finding 52.)

  sub_820B5B08's critical branch: if [self+10868] == 0, append directly
  to the ring; if nonzero, queue the entry into a spinlocked pending
  list at self+13000 (sub_820B52B8) and DON'T touch the ring.

Live captures (WATCH-51): the first three flushes appended directly
(ring wptr 19->22->...->40 dwords = exactly the 172-byte frozen offset
known since Finding 33 -- mystery answered to the byte), including the
first SWAP submission (sub_820C8F30, called from sub_820CD7D8, passes
r7=1, incrementing [10868] to 1). From then on [10868] != 0, so every
subsequent submission goes to the pending list -- the ring goes quiet
after "frame 2" not because the game stops producing (it produces
forever: [10780] reached 199+, the command-buffer circular allocator
wraps and keeps allocating cleanly) but because everything batches
behind an in-flight swap that never completes. A hardware write
watchpoint on [10868] (0x3a000c374) across a full run confirmed: only
sub_820B5B08's += sites ever write it (it climbed to 31), and the ONLY
zeroing writer in the entire image is Finding 52's fatal-recovery path.
The intended decrementer never runs.

The swap packet itself (23-dword indirect buffer, decoded live):
TYPE0 0x057C x2, 0x0578, WAIT_REG_MEM, PM4_INTERRUPT (source=1, executed
for real by this project's tracer since Finding 37 -- confirmed
dispatched), TYPE0 0x045E, WAIT_REG_MEM, TYPE0 0x057C. The ISR's
source==1 branch would call *([ctx+10772]+16) -- the swap-completion
callback -- but that slot is 0 (re-confirmed; Finding 33's WATCH-18
result stands), so the ISR just clears a pending bit in *[ctx+10776]
and returns. Swap-adjacent packet builders (sub_820CC8E0, sub_820B5C90)
embed the mangled physical addresses of ([10772]+4)/([10772]+0) into
GPU packets -- on real hardware the GPU itself writes swap-progress
markers there; the tracer currently only executes EVENT_WRITE_SHD and
INTERRUPT, not these TYPE0/other memory-writing swap packets.

--- Status: the ring-quiet question that every thread of this
    investigation has terminated at since Finding 38 is now reduced to
    one concrete, falsifiable question: what, on real hardware, clears
    the in-flight swap ([10868] decrement / ([10772]+4) swap-progress
    writeback / *([10772]+16) callback registration), and which of
    those pieces this harness must emulate. Concrete next steps, in
    order: (1) find who registers *([10772]+16) (grep for stores of 16
    into the 32-byte object, or write-watch 0x3b9416010 across a full
    boot) -- if the game registers it later/conditionally, find the
    gate; (2) decode what registers 0x057C/0x0578/0x045E are (Xenia
    register table) and what the two WAIT_REG_MEM packets in the swap
    buffer poll -- if they poll a register/memory value the host can
    satisfy statically (like Finding 53's 0x1951), the swap sequence
    may complete with another seed value or a small tracer addition;
    (3) consider executing the swap buffer's TYPE0 register writes into
    a real register file so ISR-side reads (e.g. current frontbuffer)
    see them. All infrastructure for testing any of these already
    exists and is verified live. ---

--- Finding 55: ring-quiet mystery (Finding 38/54) actually unblocked --
    host-level reset of the in-flight swap counter, not a PPC-level
    callback simulation, is what works.

    Re-confirmed live (fresh write watchpoint spanning full boot through
    the plateau) that nothing in the game's own compiled image ever
    registers *([10772]+16) -- third independent confirmation (Finding
    33's static trace, Finding 54's watchpoint, this one).

    First attempt: PPC-level hand-edit of sub_820B5160's null-callback
    branch (loc_820B51A0) to decrement [self+10868] by 1, simulating
    where the real swap-completion callback would fire. Tested live with
    a call-counter watchpoint on sub_820B5B08 (WATCH-55B): only fired
    ONCE per run (at inFlight=2), because after the first swap every
    later submission gets diverted into the self+13000 pending list
    (sub_820B52B8) instead of the real ring, and sub_820B5B08 has NO
    drain/replay logic anywhere for that list -- it only checks
    [10868] at its own entry on each NEW submission attempt. Since only
    the first swap's packet ever reaches the ring, only that swap's own
    PM4_INTERRUPT ever gets scanned/executed, so the decrement fires
    once while the real counter climbs unbounded (confirmed: 176 calls
    to sub_820B5B08 over a run, inFlight 0 -> 0x20). Reverted cleanly
    (private/ppc hand-edit, gitignored, not persisted anyway).

    Root problem: this harness has no real asynchronous GPU with its own
    per-submission completion timing to signal from, so a literal 1:1
    simulation of the missing callback can't work -- it only ever gets
    one chance to fire. Fix: in host/kernel_impl.cpp's __imp__VdSwap,
    unconditionally reset [self+10868] (self=0xA0009900, empirically
    stable since Finding 20) to 0 once per real VdSwap call. Honest
    approximation: "by the next vblank, whatever was in flight is done."

    Verified live (300s run, watchdog temporarily raised then reverted):
    ring buffer offset growth previously frozen dead at byte 172 forever
    (Finding 38/54) now grows continuously, byte 172 -> 131080, across
    1822 real frames (opcode variety: WAIT_REG_MEM 0x3C, INDIRECT_BUFFER
    0x3F, 0x58/0x3B/0x60/0x2B, INTERRUPT 0x54, 0x36/0x63/0x62/0x61,
    0x45, plus rare 0x21/0x48) before hitting a NEW, later plateau.
    Ring buffer's real decoded size (VdInitializeRingBuffer,
    sizeLog2Raw=14) is 1048576 bytes -- 131080 is nowhere near that, so
    this new plateau is NOT wraparound, it's a genuinely new stall.

    New plateau's signature: the last growing frame (1821) ends with
    `TYPE3 opcode=0x3F count=2 (INDIRECT_BUFFER) -> following indirect
    buffer at 0xA0000000 (0 dwords)` -- an indirect-buffer packet
    pointing at a suspicious near-null-looking guest address with a
    zero dword count -- immediately followed by 19+ more ScanBuffer
    calls (frames 1822-1841) that all report zero new bytes; wptr stops
    advancing entirely. Not yet investigated: whether 0xA0000000 is a
    real (if degenerate) target the game intentionally emits, or a sign
    that some upstream producer state went bad around frame ~1821.

    Status: this is genuine, major progress -- the ring-quiet mystery
    that blocked every thread of investigation since Finding 38 is
    resolved as designed-for-real-hardware behavior this harness now
    correctly approximates, unblocking over 1800 frames of real content
    flow that never happened before. Next concrete step: investigate the
    new frame-1821/0xA0000000 plateau -- dump the full packet stream for
    frames 1815-1822 to see what's feeding that INDIRECT_BUFFER pointer,
    and check whether it correlates with a specific game-side state
    (fence value, tick count, memory address family) rather than being
    a hard ceiling. ---

--- Finding 56: the frame-1821 plateau is a real, confirmed-live deadlock in
    sub_820B4EE8's ring-space wait loop -- a structural mismatch between
    this harness's synchronous fake GPU and real hardware's asynchronous
    one, not a data/seed bug like Findings 51/53.

    Dumped frames 1815-1822 in full (gpu_trace.log). Steady state
    (1815-1820) is a clean, healthy, identical-shaped repeating pattern
    each frame: two draw-call indirect buffers (127 and 253 dwords,
    covering real 3D state -- registers 0x2000-0x2300 range, viewport/
    shader/vertex setup) each ending in EVENT_WRITE_SHD to the fence at
    0xB9405000/0xB9405004 (value climbing +4/frame, healthy), plus the
    23-dword swap tail (registers 0x057C/0x0578/0x045E, PM4_INTERRUPT
    source=1) already mapped in Finding 54. Frame 1821 is a single
    degenerate `INDIRECT_BUFFER -> 0xA0000000 (0 dwords)` -- confirmed
    NOT corruption: 0xA0000000 is the real Xbox 360 physical
    write-combined alias base, i.e. this is a null/zero-sized target,
    and it's the last thing the ring ever receives.

    Traced why: sub_820B4EE8 (Finding 43/54's known ring-space
    allocator) loops calling Finding 52's hang-watchdog helper
    (sub_820B9A58) each iteration, comparing a caller-supplied target
    (r30, confirmed live as a small non-address value, e.g. 0x51) and a
    fence-progress value read from [[self+10768]+4] (r11). Added a
    temporary counter probe directly in the loop (private/ppc hand-edit,
    reverted after) and ran live: confirmed a SINGLE call spun past
    2.67 BILLION iterations with the fence value frozen dead at
    0xB8D96F08 for the entire span, while different retrying callers'
    target addresses (r29, the secondary exit check's operand) kept
    climbing (0xB8DAA578 -> 0xB8DB6D34 and beyond) -- the gap the loop
    is waiting to close only ever widens, never closes. This is a
    genuine infinite spin, not a slow-but-eventually-satisfied wait.

    Also confirmed (WATCH-57, temporary probe in host/gpu_trace.cpp,
    reverted after) that [self+10768] is a pointer directly AT the
    fence block (0xB9405000 family) -- the same fence Finding 51/54
    already established works correctly via EVENT_WRITE_SHD -- so this
    is not a second, independently-stale shadow value; it's the same
    fence, genuinely frozen because nothing new is executing to advance
    it. Separately (and NOT the cause of this stall, but a distinct
    real address worth knowing about for later work): rptrWriteBackAddr_
    (set via VdEnableRingBufferRPtrWriteBack, currently 0x1940503C this
    run) is a completely different guest location the harness already
    updates correctly every VdSwap (host/gpu_trace.cpp:324-327) -- cross-
    referenced against Xenia's real CommandProcessor::
    EnableReadPointerWriteBack (ptr is used as-is, no scaling), and
    this project's MmGetPhysicalAddress is a genuine identity no-op, so
    0x1940503C is confirmed to be a real, legitimately-computed guest
    address, not a translation bug.

    Root cause: this harness's fake GPU is entirely synchronous --
    GpuCommandTracer::ScanAndTraceFrame (which retires ring content and
    advances the fence) only runs from inside VdSwap, on the same CPU
    thread that just tried to submit. sub_820B4EE8's wait loop is
    reached DURING a submission attempt, before that thread can ever
    reach VdSwap again -- so once inside this loop, the one thing that
    could unblock it (a fresh fence advance) can structurally never
    happen. Real hardware doesn't have this problem because the GPU
    consumes previously-submitted ring backlog continuously and
    independently of whatever the CPU is currently doing.

    Status: root cause fully confirmed live, not yet fixed. This is a
    harder class of fix than Findings 51/53/55 (seed a register, honor
    a size, reset a counter) -- it needs the fake GPU's "consumption"
    step decoupled from being purely synchronous-inside-VdSwap, e.g. a
    periodic/background pump that can retire ring content even while
    the main thread is blocked in a wait loop like this one, or some
    other way to keep the fence moving independently of CPU submission
    progress. Not yet designed or attempted -- flagged for the next
    session as an architecture decision, not a quick patch. ---

--- Finding 57: built the async GPU pump thread (necessary, correct
    infrastructure) -- confirmed live it does NOT by itself resolve
    Finding 56's deadlock, and traced the real reason down to a single
    worker thread that owns both VdSwap and the stuck wait loop.

    Implemented Finding 56's proposed design: GpuCommandTracer::
    ScanAndTraceFrame (host/gpu_trace.cpp) no longer runs synchronously
    inside VdSwap. A dedicated detached thread (host/main.cpp, alongside
    the existing tickThread) now polls on a ~1ms cadence, with its own
    PPCContext (own stack at 0x90200000, same small-data-area base as
    the main context -- needed because ScanBuffer's PM4_INTERRUPT
    handling actually invokes real guest code, the registered graphics
    interrupt callback, which can't safely share the main thread's live
    register state). GpuCommandTracer gained a recursive_mutex (host/
    gpu_trace.h) since its state is now genuinely cross-thread: VdXxx
    setters write from the main thread, the pump reads/writes from its
    own thread; recursive because PM4_INTERRUPT dispatch runs guest code
    while the lock is held, and that guest code could in principle call
    back into another VdXxx export on the same thread. VdSwap
    (host/kernel_impl.cpp) no longer calls ScanAndTraceFrame at all --
    the pump thread is now the sole caller. Builds clean, runs clean
    (300s, no crash), pump confirmed live at the expected ~1ms cadence
    (475816 scans over 300s, then again 15786 over a 10s sanity run).

    Live-tested against the exact byte-131080 plateau: NO CHANGE.
    Bit-for-bit identical to the pre-fix state -- same frozen fence
    (0xB8D96F08), same stuck retry target sequence (0xB8DAA578 through
    0xB8DB6D34, 12 distinct values over 300s), same total spin count
    order of magnitude (~2.69 billion iterations). Faster/decoupled
    scanning changed nothing because there was never a scanning backlog
    to catch up on in the first place -- the CPU thread genuinely never
    writes anything past offset 131080, so there's nothing new for even
    an infinitely fast pump to find.

    Traced why, definitively, with a live thread-ID probe (temporary,
    reverted) on both VdSwap's entry and sub_820B4EE8's spin: BOTH run
    on the exact same thread. Confirmed the full static call chain
    produces this pairing: the dedicated worker thread (entry
    sub_820C8D28, Finding 38) runs sub_820CCA68, which calls sub_820B5EE0
    (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.6.cpp:25234) -> sub_820B53C0
    (ppc_recomp.4.cpp:13614) -> the stuck sub_820B4EE8. Finding 38 already
    established this same worker thread calls VdSwap directly on its own
    cadence. Live call-counting confirmed VdSwap calls hard-stop at
    ~1500-2000 total and never resume for the remaining ~280+ seconds of
    a 300s run, exactly when the spin count is climbing into the
    billions -- i.e. once this one thread wedges requesting its own
    ring space, it loses BOTH duties permanently: no more submissions,
    AND no more vblank interrupts (source=0, dispatched inside VdSwap),
    since VdSwap itself never runs again either.

    Also traced (statically) which buffer sub_820B4EE8 is actually
    gating in this call chain: NOT the main 1MB ring, NOT the 0xB8Dxxxxx
    scratch-chunk pool, NOT Finding 38's already-mapped 8-slot queue at
    self+10768+64..92 -- a third, distinct small structure embedded in
    self at byte offsets ~13484-13508 (fields observed: 13484 base/
    capacity-adjacent, 13488 capacity, 13496/13500/13504/13508 read/
    write-position bookkeeping). sub_820B53C0 computes a small candidate
    write position from a caller-supplied header (a count field decoded
    PM4-style from *(r5+0)) and this structure's own capacity, and only
    calls sub_820B4EE8 when that computed position doesn't already fit;
    r30 (target) is NOT a growing sequence counter as the static code
    first suggested -- live it was CONSTANT (0x51) across all 12 stuck
    retries, meaning self+13500's own source value is itself frozen,
    consistent with this thread being unable to make ANY forward
    progress on ANY of its own state once wedged. Not yet mapped: exact
    byte-level meaning/consumer of this specific sub-buffer, or what
    real hardware relies on to keep this specific wait satisfiable.

    Decision: keeping the pump-thread infrastructure -- it's correct,
    matches real hardware's asynchronous GPU-consumption model, and is
    necessary groundwork regardless (some future fix WILL need
    consumption decoupled from VdSwap; this just isn't sufficient alone
    for this specific deadlock). Not reverted. Next concrete step: fully
    map self+13484-13508's byte layout and real semantics (read
    sub_820B53C0/sub_820B5EE0/sub_820CCA68 in full, including whatever
    self+12944/12948 -- referenced alongside it -- actually tracks) to
    find a legitimate, non-guessed way to keep this wait satisfiable,
    the same class of problem Finding 55 solved for self+10868 but for a
    buffer whose real consumer/producer contract isn't understood yet. ---

--- Finding 58: mapped self+13484-13508 fully via sub_820B6560 (the GPU
    manager's own constructor) -- it's a dedicated scratch allocation
    with the SAME fence-based reclamation as the main ring. Implemented
    a targeted, Finding-55-style force-advance fix. Real, measurable
    progress (172 -> 1732 bytes vs. permanently frozen dead before) but
    not a full fix -- found and precisely characterized a THIRD layer of
    gating behind it.

    Static trace of sub_820B6560 (self's constructor -- also confirmed
    as the single place that allocates the 96-byte fence block at
    self+10768 and the 32-byte swap-completion slot at self+10772,
    Finding 33/54's already-known structures, plus writes the real
    0x0BADF00D guard magic Finding 54 already identified): self+13484,
    13504, and 13508 all initialize to the SAME freshly-allocated base
    pointer (a dedicated arena, separate from both the main ring and the
    heap); self+13488 initializes to that arena's own end address
    (base+size-4); self+13496 initializes to the RPTR-writeback address
    itself (a completely different, unrelated address family -- gets
    overwritten with provisional in-progress state during normal
    reservation attempts, not a fixed-purpose field after construction).

    sub_820B53C0's caller-side precheck (ppc_recomp.4.cpp:11913-11918)
    already rejects any candidate write position past self+13508 before
    ever calling sub_820B4EE8 -- so every value sub_820B4EE8 is asked to
    wait for is bounded above by self+13508 by construction. Fix
    (host/gpu_trace.cpp, inside ScanAndTraceFrame, once per pump tick):
    if self+13508 > the fence value at [self+10768]+4, force the fence
    forward to match. Same "no real async GPU, approximate completion"
    reasoning as Finding 55, targeting this second, independent
    consumer of the same missing swap-completion signal.

    Live-tested (temporary WATCH-61 probes, reverted): the fix
    definitely works AS DESIGNED -- confirmed firing repeatedly early in
    a run, each time immediately followed by real forward ring
    progress (wptr climbing 76 -> 1516 within about the first 20-30
    real seconds, a genuine burst of successful reservations that
    NEVER happened at all before this fix). But then both the fence AND
    self+13508 freeze together and progress crawls to a near-halt for
    the rest of a 300s run (final result: byte 172 -> 1732, vs. Finding
    56/57's hard-frozen-at-172-forever baseline -- real but marginal
    improvement, nowhere near Finding 55's pre-Finding-56 131080 result).

    Traced why self+13508 itself stops advancing: it only moves when
    sub_820B5380 runs (a double-buffer swap: copies a staging descriptor
    at self+13040-13060 into the active self+13496-13508 fields, plus
    self+40/44/48). sub_820B5380's one real call site is inside
    sub_820C8F30 (ppc_recomp.6.cpp:15239) -- Finding 54's own already-
    mapped swap-submission function -- gated behind a specific bit of
    the self+10809 flags byte (ppc_recomp.6.cpp:15355-15359: extracts
    one bit via rlwinm, branches to the OTHER path, sub_820B5278 on
    self+13000 -- Finding 54's spinlocked pending list -- when clear).
    Live sampling (WATCH-61, reverted) confirmed self+10809 transitions
    0x00 -> 0x24 -> 0x04 early in a run, exactly matching the successful
    burst, then STAYS at 0x04 for the rest of the run, exactly matching
    where progress stalls. Bit semantics not yet decoded precisely
    (manual PPC rotate/mask arithmetic is error-prone by hand -- didn't
    trust a derived bit number without live cross-check, and ran out of
    session budget to do that cross-check rigorously). This is a third,
    independent layer of the same fundamental problem (everything here
    ultimately traces back to swap completion never being signaled) --
    not yet a quick patch.

    Decision: kept the fix (host/gpu_trace.cpp) -- strictly better than
    the Finding 56/57 baseline, zero regression, no crash across a full
    300s live test plus a clean 10s sanity run. Reverted all temporary
    WATCH-61 probes. Next concrete step: decode self+10809's exact bit
    layout precisely (probably worth building a small standalone bit
    calculator rather than hand-deriving PPC rotate-and-mask math again)
    and find what's supposed to set/clear it during normal operation,
    to understand why it stabilizes at 0x04 instead of continuing to
    toggle whatever bit gates sub_820B5380. ---

--- Finding 59: corrects Finding 58 -- self+13508 does NOT actually
    freeze after the initial burst. It's genuinely, if slowly and
    irregularly, continuing to advance the entire time. The Finding 58
    sub_820B5380/self+10809-bit theory was also a dead end (that bit is
    unrelated -- see below); the real driver was misidentified.

    First: computed the rlwinm bit-extraction precisely with a Python
    rotate simulation instead of by hand (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.6.cpp:
    15355-15357, `rlwinm r27,r11,26,31,31` on a byte-loaded register) --
    it isolates standard bit 0x40, and every self+10809 value sampled
    live in Finding 58 (0x00, 0x24, 0x04) has that bit clear the entire
    time. So sub_820B5380's gate was constantly in the SAME state
    throughout Finding 58's whole test window -- it cannot be what
    explains a burst-then-stall pattern, and this bit isn't the one
    that matters here at all.

    Re-grepped every write site to self+13508 and found the real one:
    sub_820B6080 (ppc_recomp.4.cpp:13977), NOT sub_820B5380. Confirmed
    its caller is sub_820B6220 -- Finding 54's own top-level flush entry
    point, called routinely during normal operation, not some rare
    edge case. sub_820B6080 also writes self+40 (write cursor) and,
    when a separate size check passes, rounds self+13496 up to the next
    4096-byte boundary (ppc_recomp.4.cpp:13991-13996); when the check
    fails instead it calls back into sub_820B5EE0 -- the same stuck
    chain from Finding 57's call-chain trace.

    Live-resampled with self+40/44/48/13488/13496 alongside the
    existing fence/self+13508 pair (temporary WATCH-62 probe, reverted,
    120s watchdog): self+13496 climbs smoothly and continuously in
    small (~0x10-0x18) steps roughly every 2-4 real seconds, THE ENTIRE
    RUN, with no stall at all -- including through the exact 36-second
    stretch (pump frames 28000-64000) where wptr/fence/self+13508 sat
    completely still. self+13508 and wptr themselves DO keep moving
    too, just in bigger, much rarer jumps, not on any fixed interval.
    This is real, continuous, if slow, forward progress -- not the
    "freezes solid" characterization Finding 58 reported after a
    shorter observation window.

    Working theory, not yet confirmed: what looks like a stall is
    actually this worker thread's genuine, intended operating cadence.
    Finding 38 (much earlier in this investigation) independently
    clocked this exact same worker thread calling VdSwap roughly once
    per ~4.7 real seconds -- the same order of magnitude as what's
    observed here. If so, Finding 55/57/58 together may have already
    resolved the actual bug (the permanent deadlock), and what remains
    is simply this thread doing real work at its own real, always-slow
    pace, not a further defect needing a fix.

    Status: not chasing further this session -- diminishing clarity
    returns for the time invested, and the remaining question (is this
    genuinely the game's real cadence, or one more layer of an
    artificial gate) needs either a much longer live observation window
    (many minutes, to see if it ever meaningfully accelerates or hits a
    real ceiling) or full static mapping of self+13496/13000's exact
    contracts, neither attempted yet. Next session's concrete options,
    in order of cost: (1) run much longer (10+ minutes) and see whether
    throughput ever changes character; (2) map sub_820CCA68's own outer
    loop/timing precisely to confirm or refute the ~4.7s-cadence theory
    directly rather than inferring it; (3) full static trace of
    self+13000/13496/13040-13060's real contracts if (1)/(2) point to a
    genuine remaining defect rather than intended pacing. ---

--- Finding 60: ran the long test (option 1). Result: Finding 58's fix
    is a REGRESSION, not an improvement -- reverted it entirely.

    900s live run (Finding 58 enabled, same build as Finding 59):
    growth is real through frame ~699665 (byte 2380, ~445 real seconds
    in), then hits a genuine hard flatline for the remaining ~455
    seconds -- confirmed by dense resampling around the transition
    (last three distinct byte values: 2308 at frame 620993, 2332 at
    679967, 2356 at 687783, 2380 at 699665, then nothing ever again).
    This refutes Finding 59's "maybe it's just the real slow cadence"
    theory outright -- there IS still a hard ceiling, it just took much
    longer to hit.

    But the real surprise: 2380 bytes is FAR less content than Finding
    56/57's own pre-Finding-58 baseline (131080 bytes, reached in under
    150s). Ran a direct A/B comparison to confirm this wasn't noise --
    same build, same 900s window, Finding 58's fence-force code gated
    behind a local `constexpr bool kFinding58Enabled` flipped to false:
    result was 131080 bytes, matching the known-good Finding 56/57
    baseline exactly, and OVER 55x MORE content than with Finding 58
    enabled. Confirmed reproducible, not a fluke.

    Conclusion: forcibly advancing the shared fence at [self+10768]+4
    to satisfy self+13484-13508's own wait condition (Finding 58's
    fix) corrupts some OTHER consumer of that exact same fence value --
    almost certainly the main submission pipeline (sub_820B5B08,
    Finding 54) or Finding 55's own self+10868 logic, both of which
    also read this fence and presumably rely on it accurately
    reflecting real EVENT_WRITE_SHD execution, not an artificially
    inflated value. Pushing the fence ahead of what's actually been
    processed apparently causes one of those other consumers to reach
    an incorrect conclusion (e.g. treating unprocessed work as done)
    that manifests as an even earlier, tighter stall than doing
    nothing at all would have. Not yet root-caused which specific
    consumer or why -- reverted before investigating further, since the
    A/B result alone was decisive enough to act on immediately.

    Action taken: fully reverted Finding 58's code (host/gpu_trace.cpp)
    back to the Finding 57 state. Verified clean build, clean 10s
    sanity run (no crash), git diff shows exactly the 27 lines Finding
    58 added being removed, nothing else touched. Current committed
    state (Findings 55 + 57, NOT 58) is confirmed live to reach byte
    131080 before its own hard stop -- back to the best known-good
    baseline.

    Lesson for future fix attempts in this investigation: a short
    (300s) test showing "better than before, no regression" is NOT
    sufficient evidence to keep a fix in this specific class of problem
    -- Finding 58 looked strictly better at 300s and was actually much
    worse by 900s. Any future fence/counter-forcing fix here should be
    validated against a run at least as long as the best previously-
    known result took to reach its own plateau (~150s for 131080)
    before being trusted, and ideally cross-checked with a direct A/B
    comparison like this one rather than a single before/after run.

    Status: back to Finding 55+57 as the current best state (131080
    byte ceiling, real progress unblocked from the original Finding
    38/54 permanent freeze at byte 172, but not fully solved). The
    self+13484-13508 scratch-buffer deadlock (Finding 56) remains
    unfixed -- Finding 58's approach to it specifically is now a known
    dead end, not just unproven. Next attempt on this specific
    sub-problem should look for a way to let self+13484-13508's wait
    resolve WITHOUT touching the shared self+10768+4 fence value at
    all (e.g. a more targeted intervention scoped to only what
    sub_820B4EE8/sub_820B53C0 themselves read, not a value other real
    pipeline stages also depend on for correctness). ---

--- Finding 61: implemented Finding 60's suggested targeted intervention
    -- a durable, host-level WEAK-SYMBOL OVERRIDE of sub_820B4EE8 itself
    (new file host/game_overrides.cpp), not a memory poke. Confirmed
    live: real, safe, working improvement, though not sufficient alone
    to fully resolve the ring ceiling (see Finding 64).

    XenonRecomp's PPC_WEAK_FUNC macro (thirdparty/XenonRecomp/XenonUtils/
    ppc_context.h) makes every auto-generated PPC function's outer,
    plainly-C++-mangled name (e.g. `sub_820B4EE8`, distinct from its
    strong, extern-"C" `__imp__sub_820B4EE8` implementation) a weak
    symbol -- any strong definition of the same mangled name elsewhere
    in the link takes priority, standard toolchain behavior, already
    proven safe for a static-library object providing hundreds of
    OTHER unrelated symbols from the same translation unit. Added
    host/game_overrides.cpp with a real, non-extern-"C", non-__imp__-
    prefixed `void sub_820B4EE8(PPCContext&, uint8_t*)` and wired it
    into CMakeLists.txt's BigBumpinHost sources. Confirmed via `nm` +
    `otool -tv` on the built binary: the linker resolved every call
    site to this new definition (low host address, real disassembly
    showing our own chrono/register-load code), not the generated one.

    Reimplements sub_820B4EE8's real contract faithfully (same fence
    read at [[self+10768]+4], same two-part exit condition) but with a
    50ms host-timed bound instead of depending on the guest's own
    watchdog chain (Finding 52's escape valve, confirmed broken because
    the hang-notify callback it depends on is never registered). Writes
    NO guest memory at all -- purely bounds this one function's control
    flow, so nothing else in the pipeline can be corrupted by it, unlike
    Findings 55's first attempt and Finding 58.

    Live-tested rigorously, applying Finding 60's own lesson: 300s (real
    growth, still climbing at the end, unlike Finding 58's clear
    plateau), then 900s (linear growth the entire run, 0->82012 bytes,
    no plateau at all), then 1800s (crossed and matched the old 131080
    ceiling exactly, then stopped -- later understood via Finding 62/64
    to be a DIFFERENT blocker, not this one recurring). No crash in any
    run. Kept -- real, durable, safe infrastructure improvement,
    confirmed to eliminate the specific self+13484-13508 deadlock class
    it targets (Finding 56) even though a separate blocker remains
    (Finding 64). ---

--- Finding 62: corrected the ring buffer size formula against real
    Xenia source -- independently correct, briefly suspected (wrongly)
    to explain the ~131000 ceiling via wraparound, reverted that part
    after live evidence disproved it, kept the formula fix.

    host/gpu_trace.cpp's RegisterRingBuffer used `8u << (sizeLog2Raw+3)`
    for ringBufferSize_, its own comment flagging it as "not
    independently verified." Cross-referenced against real Xenia source
    (CommandProcessor::InitializeRingBuffer, command_processor.cc):
    `primary_buffer_size_ = 1 << (size_log2+3)` -- the project's formula
    was exactly 8x too large (1MB instead of the real 128KB, given the
    consistently-observed sizeLog2Raw=14). Corrected to `1u << (...)`.

    With the corrected 131072-byte size closely matching the long-
    observed ~131000-byte ceiling, initially implemented ring
    wraparound (once no room remained for one more packet header,
    resume scanning from offset 0) as the presumed root-cause fix.
    Live-tested (900s, then 2400s to give real margin): reproducibly
    landed just a few dozen bytes short of the true 131072-byte
    boundary (131068, then 131020 after fixing an off-by-one in the
    wrap condition) and got stuck there too, never actually reaching or
    exercising the wrap logic. Chasing this further (see Finding 63)
    confirmed it's a genuinely different, independent blocker -- and a
    live capture of the one run that DID reach the wrap threshold
    showed the "wrapped" region at offset 0 still holding the
    ORIGINAL, ancient ME_INIT packet from process start, referencing an
    indirect-buffer target (0xB8D51000) since recycled many times over
    by unrelated later content -- re-scanning it produced garbage, not
    real new data. The ring genuinely isn't being wrapped by real game
    behavior within any tested timeframe; the wraparound fix was based
    on a false premise. Reverted the wraparound logic entirely; kept
    the size-formula correction since it's independently verified
    correct against real hardware semantics regardless of whether ring
    wraparound is the active bottleneck here. ---

--- Finding 63: found and fixed a SECOND, independent instance of
    Finding 61's exact problem class, on a completely different call
    chain -- confirmed via live lldb thread backtraces, not guessed.
    Same override pattern, safe and working per-call, but the
    underlying wait condition turns out to never legitimately resolve
    in this project's current execution model (see Finding 64).

    With Finding 62's ring-size fix in place (ruling out wraparound),
    a fresh long run (2400s watchdog) still stalled, reproducibly, in
    the same ~131000-byte neighborhood. Rather than guess again, set up
    a monitor script to detect the stall live and auto-attach lldb
    (`thread backtrace all`) the moment offset held steady across three
    60s checks. Two samples 30 seconds apart showed the exact same call
    stack both times (moved by only 2 instructions within the same
    function, confirming a genuine sustained loop, not a coincidental
    snapshot): the MAIN game thread (not a worker thread -- traced back
    to __xstart directly), stuck inside __imp__sub_820B9A58, reached via
    __imp__sub_820B5BC8 <- __imp__sub_820CCA68 <- __imp__sub_824A76D0 <-
    ... <- __imp__sub_8212B130 <- __xstart -- the EXACT call chain
    Findings 19-24, 38, and 43 already mapped early in this
    investigation, well before this session's work.

    sub_820B5BC8's real contract (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:
    12980-13101, already precisely documented by Findings 20-24/43):
    given self and a target, wait until current -- *(*(self+10768)+0),
    a DIFFERENT field of the same 96-byte fence block sub_820B4EE8 also
    uses (that one reads offset+4) -- reaches target. Findings 20-24
    (predating this session, before the vblank interrupt fix existed)
    already established current's real nature: a dispatcher-object-
    style signal count, set once at creation, that nothing in this
    project's implementation was ever confirmed to increment again.

    Added a second override in host/game_overrides.cpp, same pattern as
    Finding 61: faithfully reimplements the real exit condition
    (target<=current), preserves the real function's one genuine
    producer side effect (proactively calling the real sub_820B6220
    flush entry point when target equals the current self+10780
    expectation and self+12944 is clear -- called directly via a normal
    PPC_EXTERN_FUNC declaration, not reimplemented, so its own real
    effects flow through the already-safe pipeline including Finding
    61's own override further down the call chain), then bounds the
    wait to 50ms instead of the guest's own watchdog chain. Writes no
    guest memory. Confirmed via nm/otool the override resolved
    correctly, same as Finding 61.

    Live-tested: no crash, confirmed via a second lldb sample that the
    override itself works exactly as designed (caught mid-sleep inside
    its own bounded wait, will return within 50ms) -- but total ring
    growth still stalled at the same ~131068 byte mark for the
    remainder of a 40-minute run. The override is not wrong; the
    caller (sub_820CCA68) has no alternative path when this wait
    "fails" and just retries forever, and the underlying condition
    genuinely never becomes true in this project's current execution
    model (see Finding 64 for why). Kept -- real, safe, correctly-
    bounded infrastructure, same standard as Finding 61, even though it
    alone doesn't unblock further progress. ---

--- Finding 64: precisely identified WHY self+10768+0 (Finding 63's
    "current") never advances -- a real, conditionally-gated CPU-side
    write exists, but its gate gets satisfied only in a no-GPU
    failover mode this project's healthy execution path never enters.
    Also discovered self+10768+0 is NOT safely isolated to
    sub_820B5BC8 -- multiple other functions read and write it, so
    directly forcing it (Finding 58-style) would be unsafe. Not fixed
    this session -- precisely scoped for next time.

    Searched every private/ppc/*.cpp file for code that dereferences
    the pointer at self+10768 specifically at offset+0 (distinct from
    Finding 56/61's offset+4). Found real hits beyond sub_820B5BC8's
    own three internal checks: __imp__sub_820B5A28 (write), __imp__
    sub_820B6340 (read, unexplored), __imp__sub_820B6560 (write, the
    constructor's own init-to-1), __imp__sub_820B98A8 (read, the
    wait-loop setup helper), __imp__sub_820B9A58 (read, the watchdog
    helper itself), __imp__sub_820BA3C0 (write, Finding 52's recovery
    path), __imp__sub_820BB930 (read, unexplored). Confirms this field
    is genuinely shared, multi-consumer state -- writing to it directly
    from the host would carry the same real risk Finding 58 proved out
    the hard way for the offset+4 fence, not a safe, scoped poke like
    Finding 61/63's control-flow-only overrides.

    Read sub_820B5A28 in full (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:2733-2850ish)
    -- this is the SAME per-frame-tail function Finding 54 already
    documented as incrementing self+10780 (the target/expectation
    counter) by 2 every frame. It ALSO writes self+10768+0 (and +4)
    directly, but only inside a gate (ppc_recomp.4.cpp:12822-2838):
    `if (self+19956 == 0 AND bit 0x2 of self+10809 is set)`. Bit 0x2 of
    self+10809 is Finding 52's own already-documented "no-GPU failover
    mode" flag -- normally CLEAR in this project's healthy execution
    (only ever set inside Finding 52's own fatal-recovery path, which
    itself never completes because it depends on the same never-
    registered hang-notify callback). This CPU-side write is a
    software fallback for when there's genuinely no GPU present, not
    the normal path -- on real hardware, self+10768+0 is expected to be
    written directly by the GPU itself, via a real EVENT_WRITE_SHD
    packet targeting that exact address (0xB9405000, matching the
    already-confirmed offset+4 target 0xB9405004 this project already
    executes correctly).

    Checked every EVENT_WRITE_SHD write logged across this whole
    session's traces (going back to Finding 37): every single one
    targets 0xB9405004 (offset+4). Not one ever targets 0xB9405000
    (offset+0) in any captured run. Either the game's own real packet
    stream never emits an EVENT_WRITE_SHD for that specific address in
    this execution path (a real, if unexplained, game-side gate,
    possibly game-state-dependent like several other gates this
    investigation has found), or this project's packet decoding/
    scanning is missing something that would normally produce or
    recognize one. Not yet determined which.

    Status: root cause of the remaining ~131000-byte-ish ceiling now
    precisely localized (not resolved) to "nothing produces a real
    write to self+10768+0 in this project's current, actually-executed
    packet stream, because the only write path that exists is gated
    behind a failover flag this project's healthy path never sets."
    Next concrete steps, in order of promise: (1) find whether the
    real swap/frame-tail packet sequence is SUPPOSED to include a
    second EVENT_WRITE_SHD targeting 0xB9405000 that this project's
    game code simply never gets far enough to emit (trace what gates
    that decision, similar to Findings 51/53's earlier seed-value
    fixes) -- if so, that's a real, principled fix, not a memory poke;
    (2) if no such packet is ever supposed to exist in this specific
    flow, investigate whether self+19956 (the OTHER half of the gate)
    is itself a fixable, legitimately-should-be-nonzero-or-zero
    precondition rather than bit 0x2; (3) only as a last resort,
    consider a host-level write to self+10768+0 mirroring what Finding
    55 did for self+10868 -- but given this field is confirmed
    multi-consumer (unlike self+10868, which had exactly one real
    zeroing writer), this needs a full map of every consumer's
    contract first, not a repeat of Finding 58's mistake. ---

--- Finding 65: CORRECTS Finding 64's central claim -- self+10768+0 (0xB9405000)
    IS written by real EVENT_WRITE_SHD packets, every frame, exactly
    like offset+4. The real problem isn't an unreachable code path; the
    game's own packet PRODUCTION genuinely halts at a consistent point,
    and this is the SAME symptom as the long-chased ~131000-byte ring
    plateau, not a separate bug -- Finding 63's sub_820B5BC8 deadlock
    was real and is genuinely fixed by Finding 61's override; what
    remains is one level further upstream.

    A separate live investigation (dispatched to a fork) into whether
    self+10809 bit 0x2 has any non-fatal setter found one: sub_820B6220
    (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:14085-14125, Finding 54's own top-
    level flush entry point, reached every normal frame from
    sub_820CCA68) sets bit 0x2 for real, gated behind a fixed global at
    guest address 0x8265C6A0 -- not exclusively Finding 52's fatal path
    as Finding 64 assumed. This alone doesn't resolve the mystery, but
    directly contradicted Finding 64's premise enough to warrant
    re-checking the underlying data rather than trusting the earlier
    conclusion.

    Re-checked this session's OWN already-captured gpu_trace.log output
    (from Finding 58-61's own test runs, still on disk) and found
    something Finding 64 stated backwards: `grep "wrote.*to 0xB9405000"
    gpu_trace.log` returns real hits (e.g. "wrote 0x00000003 to
    0xB9405000", "wrote 0x00001C8B to 0xB9405000") -- offset+0 clearly
    IS written by real EVENT_WRITE_SHD packets, with small monotonic
    values climbing right alongside offset+4's already-confirmed real
    writes, contradicting Finding 64's "not one ever targets 0xB9405000"
    claim outright. That was a real factual error in Finding 64,
    corrected here.

    Given this, re-ran with a temporary diagnostic (WATCH-65, since
    reverted) added to Finding 63's sub_820B5BC8 override, printing
    target/current/expectation whenever the 50ms bound is hit. Live
    result (40-minute run): current (structPtr+0, i.e. offset+0) froze
    PERMANENTLY at exactly 7307 (0x1C8B) after some point, while target
    and expectation (self+10780-derived) kept climbing unboundedly
    (12,851+ recorded timeouts, target past 58709 and still rising) --
    the override itself works exactly as designed every single time
    (zero false negatives), the underlying wait condition just never
    becomes satisfiable again once current stops. Cross-checked against
    the SAME run's gpu_trace.log: the last-ever EVENT_WRITE_SHD write to
    0xB9405000 is "wrote 0x00001C8B to 0xB9405000" -- 0x1C8B = 7307,
    an EXACT match to the frozen current value. 3639 total real writes
    to that address across the whole run, then none ever again.

    This means Finding 63's deadlock and the long-standing ~131000-byte
    ring-content plateau are the SAME underlying symptom, not two
    separate bugs: real EVENT_WRITE_SHD execution (which requires real
    packets to exist in the ring buffer, which requires the game's own
    CPU thread to keep producing them) genuinely stops at a consistent
    point in every run, for a reason that predates and is independent
    of every wait-loop deadlock fixed this session (Findings 55, 61,
    63). Fixing downstream consumers of a signal that's never sent
    again can't produce content past the point production itself
    halted -- Findings 61/63's overrides are correctly-functioning,
    necessary infrastructure (kept), but they were never going to be
    sufficient alone, and now there's direct evidence why.

    Status: the real open question has shifted, for the first time this
    session, from "why does a downstream wait loop never unblock" (now
    well-understood and fixed in every instance found) to "why does the
    game's own packet-producing thread stop producing new ring/scratch-
    buffer content at all, around a highly consistent real-time/byte-
    count point, across every run regardless of which downstream
    deadlock is or isn't fixed." This is close in spirit to Findings
    43/44's very original open question from early in the investigation,
    now sharpened considerably by everything mapped since. Not yet
    investigated this session (out of session budget) -- concrete next
    step for a future session: live-sample the MAIN thread (not any
    worker thread) at the exact moment content production stops (byte
    ~131068, consistently reproducible) with a full backtrace, the same
    technique that found Finding 63's deadlock -- if it's blocked on
    something, that's the real next link in the chain; if it's still
    running but simply not calling anything that submits new ring
    content, that points to a game-state gate (like Finding 39's dispatch
    table) rather than a stall. ---

--- Finding 66: cross-referenced a real, independent recompilation of a
    different Xbox 360 title (skate3recomp, github.com/mchughalex/
    skate3recomp) whose GPU/kernel layer (its rexglue-sdk submodule,
    github.com/mchughalex/rexglue-skate3) turns out to be Xenia's real
    GPU command processor repackaged for static recompilation --
    src/graphics/command_processor.cpp, graphics_system.cpp,
    src/kernel/xboxkrnl/xboxkrnl_video.cpp. Real, authoritative ground
    truth, not a guess -- and it exposed a genuine gap in this project's
    model, not just a documentation nicety.

    The gap: real hardware/Xenia drives ring-buffer consumption off the
    CPU writing the CP_RB_WPTR MMIO register (graphics_system.cpp:428,
    register index 0x1C5) -- that write is trapped and calls
    CommandProcessor::UpdateWritePointer(value), the authoritative
    "content is valid up to here" signal. This project's entire model
    (GpuCommandTracer::ScanBuffer) has never known about this register at
    all -- it only ever guessed the real/unwritten-content boundary by
    scanning for the first all-zero dword (Finding tracked since the
    project's earliest GPU work). The GPU MMIO register block at
    0x7FC80000 (host/main.cpp) is plain, untrapped memory -- the game's
    writes land there same as any other store, so this project can't
    intercept the write the way Xenia does, but CAN poll the value.

    Implemented: the GPU pump thread (Finding 57's ~1ms-cadence thread)
    now polls CP_RB_WPTR every tick (GpuCommandTracer::
    ObserveWritePointer, host/gpu_trace.cpp/.h) and logs every real
    change. ScanAndTraceFrame now bounds its main-ring scan by the polled
    WPTR value (in bytes) instead of unconditionally trusting the full
    ring size + zero-byte heuristic -- when WPTR is behind the
    heuristic's own already-parsed offset, that's logged as a WARNING
    (never observed live yet, but exactly the shape Finding 62's
    wraparound suspicion would produce if it were ever real).

    Verified live, twice (default 10s watchdog, then a temporary 60s
    extension per this project's own established verify-then-revert
    precedent -- reverted after, host/main.cpp's watchdog is back to
    10s):

      10s run: CP_RB_WPTR climbs in lockstep with the zero-byte
      heuristic's own derived offset for 26 real observed changes, then
      both freeze together, permanently, at 379 dwords / 1516 bytes, for
      the rest of the run (frames 2 through 7925).

      60s run: same lockstep behavior, 164 real observed WPTR changes,
      freezing together at 1231 dwords / 4924 bytes, for the rest of the
      run (frames well past 49687).

    In both runs, at every single one of the 190 combined WPTR
    observations, the heuristic's own "offset currently" value logged
    alongside the new WPTR value was EXACTLY the previous WPTR value --
    zero drift, zero disagreement, the whole time. This directly answers
    part of Finding 65's open question: the zero-byte-padding heuristic
    is not the bug. It has been tracking the real, hardware-authoritative
    content boundary with perfect accuracy this whole investigation.
    When production stops, it's a real stop -- now confirmed by two
    independent signals (this project's own parser AND the real MMIO
    register the game itself writes) agreeing exactly, not just one.

    Honest caveat: neither run's freeze point (1516 bytes, then 4924
    bytes) matches the long-documented ~131000-byte plateau from earlier
    sessions (Findings 43/44 onward) or Finding 65's 7307-dword/
    0xB9405000 numbers -- this run's furthest real progress is
    substantially short of that. Not a contradiction: this investigation
    has landed several real fixes since those numbers were captured
    (Findings 55/57/61/63 among them), and the exact point production
    currently stalls has moved before as a direct result of earlier
    fixes in this same session. Whatever gate the game is waiting on
    before producing more ring content is apparently earlier/different
    right now than the specific 131000-byte-plateau gate previously
    chased -- worth re-establishing which stall is the current one
    before continuing Finding 65's "sample the MAIN thread when
    production stops" plan, now that this fix makes the exact freeze
    point independently confirmable via CP_RB_WPTR without needing the
    old scan-heuristic to be trusted at all.

    Also notable, not yet investigated: the system command buffer
    (Finding 38) had zero real content in either verification run this
    session ("zero padding at offset 0, stopping" every single frame) --
    contrasts with earlier findings describing real content there.
    Possibly run-to-run I/O timing variance, possibly related to
    whatever's now gating the main ring earlier than before. Flagged,
    not chased further this session.

    Status: real, live-verified infrastructure improvement (WPTR
    polling + bound + divergence warning), kept. Confirms Finding 65's
    diagnosis was correct and rules out the scanner itself as an
    alternative explanation. Concrete next step: re-run Finding 65's own
    planned technique (live-sample the MAIN thread with a full backtrace
    at the moment CP_RB_WPTR's polled value stops changing, which is now
    directly observable in gpu_trace.log without guessing) against
    wherever production stalls in the CURRENT build, not the old
    131000-byte number. ---

--- Finding 67: attempted Finding 66's own next step (live-sample the
    MAIN thread once CP_RB_WPTR genuinely stops changing) this session.
    Added a wall-clock timestamp to every [CP_RB_WPTR] log line
    (host/gpu_trace.cpp/.h, kept -- cheap, generally useful) so a frozen
    period's real duration is readable directly from the log. Built a
    monitor script that tails gpu_trace.log and declares "frozen" once

    attach lldb at that moment and capture the game thread's backtrace.

      10s run: froze quickly, matching Finding 66's own 10s run shape.
      60s run: froze at 1231 dwords (matching Finding 66 exactly --
      same build, same conditions, reproducible).
      90s run: did NOT freeze at all -- continuous, if uneven, forward
      progress the entire run, reaching 2137 dwords (8548 bytes) still
      climbing when the watchdog cut it off at t=89926ms. Monitor script

    This is a real, honest negative result, not a failed attempt: the
    "freeze" observed in Finding 66's shorter runs isn't a fixed,
    deterministic wall -- it's run-to-run timing variance in how fast
    the loading screen's real work (disc I/O, asset setup, whatever

    more closely than Finding 65's "permanently froze" language. Worth
    noting: Finding 65's own 7307-dword freeze was captured over a

    are 25-40x too short to have any real chance of reaching that same
    point, freeze or no freeze. The ~131000-byte plateau and the
    7307-count EVENT_WRITE_SHD freeze both likely need comparable real
    time to reproduce, not just a longer watchdog in the 60-90s range.

    Status: Finding 66's WPTR polling infrastructure confirmed
    reproducible and working correctly across all three new runs (kept).
    The MAIN-thread live-sample technique itself was not executed this
    session -- session time didn't stretch to a real 40+-minute watchdog

    Concrete next step: run the monitor script (scratchpad's
    monitor_freeze.sh, or an equivalent) against a genuinely long run
    (approaching or exceeding 40 real minutes, matching Finding 65's own
