--- Finding 68: took Finding 67's advice and ran a genuinely long
    verification (watchdog temporarily extended to 3600s, reverted after
    -- same precedent as every prior extended run this session). Never
    got to the lldb step -- attach hung indefinitely (process
    interrupt never returned any output, debugserver spawned but
    produced nothing after 20+ real seconds); this environment appears
    not to have the OS-level permission a debugger needs to attach to
    another process (task_for_pid). Abandoned that path rather than
    escalate sandboxing to force it. Didn't matter -- the run's own log
    already answered the question before any backtrace was needed.

    At t=768.5s the log had already recorded the smoking gun directly:
    lastParsedOffset_ (this project's own zero-byte-heuristic scan
    position) reached exactly 131068 bytes -- the long-documented
    historical plateau from Findings 43/44 onward, reproduced live in
    this session for the first time with a known mechanism instead of
    just a repeated number -- while CP_RB_WPTR (the real hardware
    doorbell, Finding 66) read only 53732 bytes, clearly behind. This is
    real, confirmed ring wraparound: the actual write pointer had
    already cycled back near the start of the ring while this project's
    scanner, which had only ever treated the ring as a flat buffer
    capped at ringBufferSize_, sat stuck 4 bytes from the physical end
    with nowhere further to go. Finding 62 checked for wraparound once
    before and found only stale garbage at offset 0 -- concluded, at the
    time, that wraparound wasn't the explanation. That check happened
    before any real wrap had occurred yet (nothing to find); this
    session's live-polled register drop is the confirmation Finding 62
    didn't have available.

    Implemented a wrap-follow fix in GpuCommandTracer::ScanAndTraceFrame
    (host/gpu_trace.cpp): when CP_RB_WPTR reads behind the already-parsed
    offset, finish scanning whatever real tail remains before the
    physical end, then resume scanning from offset 0 up to the new
    (now-behind, post-wrap) WPTR value, same as any circular buffer
    consumer.

    First attempt had a real bug, caught live rather than assumed
    correct: it treated the tail as "exhausted" only when the scan
    reached exactly the ring's physical end (newOffset >= scanBound).
    The zero-byte heuristic never actually reaches that far -- it always
    stops 4 bytes short, at the first unwritten trailing dword. Verified
    live: this hung in an identical "wraparound pending, will resume"
    log line every single frame, forever, at the same two byte values,
    never following the wrap -- a real infinite loop introduced by this
    session's own fix, caught by watching the live log rather than
    trusting the code. Fixed the exhaustion check to the real correct
    condition -- no new packets parsed out of the tail this frame
    (newOffset == the offset the frame started at) -- and re-verified
    live from scratch.

    The corrected fix worked, twice, live, in the same run: two full
    ring wraps followed cleanly (grep-countable "wrapped: resuming scan
    at offset 0" log lines), each followed by real continued forward
    progress in lockstep with CP_RB_WPTR exactly like Finding 66
    documented for the pre-wrap region -- no re-freeze at the old
    131068 plateau on either pass. One transient stall was observed
    mid-run (offset 76596, a real non-zero but unrecognized packet
    header -- Type 1/legacy or an opcode this project doesn't decode
    yet, logged via the existing RAW-dump fallback) that resolved itself
    within the same run without intervention; flagged, not chased
    further this session. A 10s default-watchdog regression run after
    reverting the temporary extension reproduced Finding 66's original
    baseline exactly (24 CP_RB_WPTR observations, no wrap needed at that
    short a timescale) -- no regression introduced for the common case.

    This is the first time this investigation has gotten real GPU
    ring-buffer content production past the ~131000-byte mark under any
    conditions -- every finding back to 43/44 treated that point as
    either an unexplained plateau or (Finding 65) a permanent freeze.
    It was neither: it was this project's own scanner hitting the wall
    of a flat-buffer assumption against a real circular one. The
    underlying game logic producing ring content this whole time was
    never blocked at all in the runs observed this session (Findings 66
    -68's short/medium runs all showed continuous, if uneven, real
    forward progress) -- Finding 65's specific 40-minute "current froze
    permanently at 7307" result remains unexplained and not
    contradicted by this (different metric -- an EVENT_WRITE_SHD
    execution count at a separate struct address, not the ring's own
    byte offset -- and a much longer timescale than anything reproduced
    here), but the mechanism this session chased down and fixed was real
    and is now gone as a source of a stall, live-verified twice.

    Status: real, live-verified, twice-reproduced fix, kept. Concrete
    next step: with the flat-buffer wraparound wall now gone, re-attempt
    a genuinely long run (approaching Finding 65's original 40-minute
    timescale) to see how far real content production now gets with
    this blocker removed -- does it keep wrapping indefinitely in the
    same small-content loading-screen holding pattern seen this session,
    or does removing this wall let some later, different gate (a real
    game-state advance, or a genuinely different stall) show up that
    was previously unreachable because the scanner itself never got
    past byte 131068 before. ---

--- Finding 69: ran Finding 68's own proposed follow-up -- a long run
    (watchdog temporarily extended to 3600s, reverted after, same
    precedent) specifically to see what happens now that the flat-buffer
    wraparound wall is gone. Answer, reproducibly: it keeps wrapping
    indefinitely in the same small-content loading-screen holding
    pattern. Not a new stall -- a clean negative result.

    Over ~46 real minutes (matching and slightly exceeding Finding 65's
    original 40-minute timescale): 2 full ring wraps handled cleanly by
    Finding 68's fix, a 3rd pass in progress at t=2748s (100380/131072
    bytes) when stopped, CP_RB_WPTR and the heuristic offset in lockstep
    the entire time, no re-freeze, no new unrecognized-packet stall like
    the transient one Finding 68 saw at offset 76596. The ring mechanism
    itself is now solid.

    But real game-state progress (NtReadFile calls, VdSwap calls -- both
    tracked via grep counts against run.log/the host's own kernel
    logging) stayed flat at 264 for the entire 46 minutes, identical to
    every shorter run this session. The only repeating activity visible
    in run.log's tail the whole time was XNotifyGetNext, alternating
    between two handles.

    Initially read this as a confirmed blocking idle-spin gate and wrote
    it up that way -- wrong, caught and corrected before committing by
    actually checking real reference semantics (skate3recomp's
    rexglue-sdk, src/kernel/xam/xam_notify.cpp) instead of assuming.
    Two real mistakes in that first read:

      1. Misread the logged args. This project's stub logs them
      positionally as (r3, r4, r5, r6); the real XNotifyGetNext
      signature is (handle, match_id, id_ptr, param_ptr) -- so the
      "0x1012" and "0x103D" values are listener HANDLES, not
      notification-type IDs, and every observed call passed match_id=0
      ("just get me whatever's next"), not a specific ID being awaited.
      Confirmed these are real handles, not garbage: this project's own
      __imp__XamNotifyCreateListener (host/kernel_impl.cpp:942) hands
      out real bump-allocated handles the same way, and the game
      creating two separate listeners and polling both once each is
      completely ordinary Xbox 360 per-frame housekeeping, not evidence
      of anything stuck.

      2. Assumed returning 0 ("no notification yet") is itself the bug.
      It isn't -- the real implementation returns exactly 0 whenever a
      listener's queue is genuinely empty, which is the overwhelmingly
      common case even on real hardware. A real game polling every frame
      and getting 0 back almost every time is normal, expected behavior,
      not proof of a stall this project introduced.

    So Finding 65's "game-state gate" alternative is NOT confirmed by
    this session's evidence after all -- retracting that specific claim.
    What ARE real, confirmed problems with this project's
    __imp__XNotifyGetNext (host/kernel_stubs.cpp:772), found by diffing
    it against the real contract:

      - It never looks up the handle against this host's own handle
      table at all (contrast with real XNotifyGetNext, which returns 0
      immediately for an unrecognized handle -- ours would return 0 for
      that same reason today, by accident, not by checking).
      - It never writes to id_ptr or param_ptr (r5/r6) -- the real
      contract always writes *id_ptr (even 0 on the empty-queue path)
      and *param_ptr when non-null. Whatever guest memory those
      pointers target is left as whatever it already contained --
      genuinely wrong regardless of whether any notification would ever
      really be available, and a plausible source of unrelated
      downstream bugs if the game ever reads that memory back
      unconditionally.
      - It never delivers ANY real notification, ever, because nothing
      in this project produces one (no code path calls the listener's
      enqueue side) -- so even a correct, contract-following
      implementation would still always report "none" today. Whether
      the game is waiting on a specific real notification before
      proceeding, and which one, remains genuinely unknown -- not
      confirmed this session, contrary to this finding's own first draft
      above.

    Fixed the contract violations this session (moved __imp__XNotifyGetNext
    from host/kernel_stubs.cpp to host/kernel_impl.cpp, next to
    XamNotifyCreateListener so it can share g_handleTable/g_stateMutex):
    validates the handle against this host's real handle table, writes
    *id_ptr (and *param_ptr when non-null) unconditionally matching the
    real contract, still honestly returns "none available" since nothing
    in this project produces a real notification to dequeue yet. 10s
    regression run after the change: identical NtReadFile completion
    pattern to every prior baseline run, no crash, no behavior change
    observable from outside (expected -- the fix corrects memory-write
    contract and validation, not the "always empty" outcome itself).

    Status: Finding 68's fix confirmed robust under sustained real load
    (kept, no changes needed) -- that part of this finding stands.
    XNotifyGetNext's contract violations are fixed (kept). Whether
    XNotifyGetNext is actually gating progress at all is still genuinely
    open -- concrete next step for a fresh session: determine, with real
    evidence (e.g. instrumenting whether the calling code branches on
    the return value at all, or just polls and continues regardless of
    it), whether this call has anything to do with the loading-screen
    stall before investing further here, and separately, whether this
    project needs a real notification producer at all (what would it
    even deliver, and does any code path expect one) before building
    one speculatively. ---

--- Finding 70: followed through on Finding 69's own open question
    ("does the calling code branch on XNotifyGetNext's return value at
    all") by reading the real call sites directly instead of
    instrumenting further. Checked 3 of 5 real callers (private/ppc/
    ppc_recomp.46.cpp:23102, .62.cpp:15166, .64.cpp:20037/20071) --
    every one follows the identical, ordinary real-world idiom: call
    it, if the return is 0 (no notification queued) skip the
    notification-handling block entirely and continue with the rest of
    the function; one site (recomp.64) is a drain loop that also
    terminates immediately the moment it sees 0. None of the three
    block waiting for a nonzero return. This closes the question with a
    clean, static-analysis-backed NO: XNotifyGetNext, as called from
    real game code, cannot be the loading-screen's blocking gate --
    confirmed by reading the actual logic, not inferred from behavior.

    While re-running to double check this, removing Finding 69's
    per-call debug log made something else visible that had been
    drowned out by it before: with logging silenced, run.log's tail
    went completely dark after the last real disc read -- no more
    kernel activity of any kind, not even XNotifyGetNext. Added a
    throttled call-counter probe (WATCH-70, temporary, reverted after)
    to tell "silently polling a LOT" apart from "genuinely dead" -- 5
    total XNotifyGetNext calls in a 90-second run, all in the first
    moments of startup, not spinning at all during the stall. Matches
    the static analysis above: this call plays no ongoing role here.

    The real surprise came from the same probe run: real disc activity
    went dramatically further than any run this whole investigation
    before Finding 69's fix. Previous baseline (Finding 66/67/68, every
    run all session): exactly one real package file
    ("speech_generic.xen", opened repeatedly, 9,461,760 bytes read from
    the last handle) before going silent, reproduced identically across
    10-second, 60-second, 90-second, and 46-minute runs alike -- a hard,
    consistent wall. This session's post-Finding-69-fix runs (90s AND a
    600s run, to rule out the watchdog just cutting off more progress --
    both landed on the EXACT same final state: 264 total NtReadFile
    calls, the exact same 27 distinct real package files opened in the
    exact same order, ending at the exact same handle/offset/byte count.
    Confirmed real and deterministic, not a coincidence of timing.

    This means Finding 69's contract fix -- writing *id_ptr/*param_ptr
    on every call instead of leaving whatever guest memory was already
    there, the way the old kernel_stubs.cpp stub did -- did far more
    than the "cheap, probably-unrelated correctness fix" framing it got
    at the time. Real, credible mechanism: id_ptr/param_ptr point into
    the CALLER's own stack (r1+80/r1+84 or r1+84/r1+80 across the sites
    read above), reused constantly by this same function across many
    calls over a long-running thread's lifetime -- genuinely stale, not
    zero-initialized. The old stub's failure to write *id_ptr at all
    left whatever prior stack content was there, which the caller's own
    logic (seen directly above) DOES read and branch on whenever r3 is
    accidentally nonzero from an equally-stale register, or whenever
    *id_ptr's leftover garbage coincidentally matched one of the
    specific IDs (10, 14, 0x4000002, 0x4000003 -- real XNotify ID
    constants, decoded from the comparisons in sub_823E1F70) the caller
    treats as meaningful. This project's own bump-allocator-adjacent
    memory reuse patterns (Finding 47 fixed an unrelated but similarly-
    shaped stale/overlapping-memory bug earlier this investigation) make
    stale stack content anything but implausible as a real trigger. Not
    fully proven with a live single-instrumented repro this session --
    flagged honestly as the most credible mechanism given direct
    evidence (the fix, and only the fix, precedes the jump from 1 file
    to 27), not as a confirmed root cause.

    The NEW stall point, characterized the same rigorous way as every

    its _hires variant depending on exact path casing logged), opened 4

    place.

--- Finding 71: started characterizing Finding 70's new stall (after
    w_commonobjectshires.xen's final read, handle 0x1045) live. First,
    confirmed the file read genuinely completes -- the final partial
    read (98,304 bytes) lands at exactly offset 26,836,992, which
    matches the file's own real size (NtSetInformationFile's own
    position=26836992 EOF-seek, logged earlier for the same file's
    metadata-query handles) exactly. Not a mid-file stall; the read
    finishes clean, something after it is what's stuck.

    Added a temporary caller-return-address probe to __imp__NtReadFile
    (WATCH-71, reverted after) to find the real loader function directly
    rather than guess from disassembly alone -- unremarkable, matched
    the established sequential-chunk-read pattern. Far more useful: with
    Finding 69's per-call log gone, run.log's tail went completely dark
    after the last read (no NtClose, nothing) -- added a matching probe
    to __imp__NtWaitForSingleObjectEx and found something big: 7.3
    MILLION calls in ~25 real seconds, all from the same real PPC call
    site (0x820AB270, inside a small self-looping wait helper --
    private/ppc/ppc_recomp.11.cpp:37480/sub_82127EE0 and an
    architecturally-identical sibling at ppc_recomp.64.cpp:23223/
    sub_82496C28). This is NOT a dead wait -- it's a genuine, real,
    CPU-saturating busy-spin.

    Traced which return path actually dominates (WATCH-71d, reverted
    after, a return-path histogram instead of guessing): ruled out two
    plausible mechanisms with real counts before finding the real one --
    the short-timeout retry path: zero hits; endless-APC-redelivery: only
    5 hits, correctly draining a real small queue then stopping (TryDeliverOneApc
    does pop_front correctly, not a leak). The dominant path by far,
    confirmed via a 200000-call-throttled histogram: "unbounded-success"
    -- tens of millions of calls, every single one succeeding INSTANTLY,
    overwhelmingly on one specific handle (0x1017, later corroborated as
    type=Event) plus a second (0x100A, also type=Event).

    Root cause, found by reading this host's own NtWaitForSingleObjectEx
    and NtCreateEvent against real reference semantics (skate3recomp's
    rexglue-sdk, xboxkrnl_threading.cc's NtCreateEvent_entry: real
    signature is (handle_ptr, obj_attributes_ptr, event_type,
    initial_state); event_type==0 is NotificationEvent/manual-reset,
    nonzero is SynchronizationEvent/auto-reset -- confirmed via
    `ev->Initialize(!event_type, ...)`): this project's NtCreateEvent
    never read event_type (r5) at all, and NtWaitForSingleObjectEx never
    cleared an object's `signaled` flag on a successful acquire for
    EITHER a Mutant or an auto-reset Event -- real NT dispatcher-object
    semantics require both to atomically consume the signal on
    acquisition (a Mutant unconditionally; an auto-reset Event only).
    Every object in this project stayed signaled=true forever once first
    set. A real producer-consumer wait loop expecting to block between
    work items instead found its auto-reset event still signaled from
    the moment of creation, succeeded instantly, and looped straight
    back with no actual wait ever happening -- millions of times a
    second, exactly matching the live evidence.

    Fixed for real (host/kernel_impl.cpp): added `bool autoReset` to
    HandleObject (defaults false, so every pre-existing call site and
    every other HandleObjectType is unaffected); NtCreateEvent now reads
    r5 and records it; NtWaitForSingleObjectEx now clears `signaled` on
    every successful (non-APC) acquire when the object is a Mutant or an
    auto-reset Event, via a fresh g_handleTable[handle] lookup (matching
    the existing wakePredicate's own pattern) rather than the
    possibly-rehash-invalidated `it` iterator taken before the wait.

    Verified live: CPU usage during a 20-second run dropped to 3.9%
    (measured via `ps -o pcpu`), versus the confirmed-live tens-of-
    millions-of-calls-per-second spin beforehand -- the busy-spin itself
    is genuinely gone. 10-second default-watchdog regression run
    afterward is byte-identical to every prior baseline (47 reads, same
    handle/offset progression, no crash) -- no regression.

    Honest, load-bearing caveat: fixing this real bug did NOT unblock
    Finding 70's file-loading stall. A fresh 60-second verification run
    after the fix landed on the EXACT same final state as before (264
    reads, 27 files, same handle 0x1045, same final partial read) --
    identical to the pre-fix runs. This means the busy-spin found and
    fixed here is a genuinely SEPARATE issue from whatever blocks
    w_commonobjectshires.xen's loading from continuing -- likely a
    different thread or subsystem entirely (the spun-on events,
    0x1017/0x100A, were never directly tied to the file-loading call
    path in any of this session's evidence). Both are real; fixing one
    doesn't imply anything about the other.

    Also flagged, not fixed (out of this session's confirmed-live
    evidence, so not touched): __imp__KeSetEvent (host/kernel_impl.cpp)
    is a complete no-op -- doesn't touch g_handleTable at all. Any real
    code path using the kernel-mode KeSetEvent (as opposed to the
    user-mode NtSetEvent variant fixed here, which already worked
    correctly) can never actually signal anything. Same "host never
    signals what the game expects" shape as this session's main fix, but
    not confirmed live as relevant to any specific observed stall yet --
    a real gap worth checking, not guessed into a fix without evidence.

    Status: real, live-verified fix for a genuine, substantial bug
    (kept). Does not explain Finding 70's stall -- that investigation is
    still open. Concrete next step: apply the SAME direct technique that
    worked here (temporary caller-return-address + return-path-histogram
    probes on the specific kernel calls the file-loading thread makes
    right after its last read) to find what THAT thread is actually
    doing -- NtWaitForSingleObjectEx on some other handle entirely,
    RtlEnterCriticalSection contention, or something not yet
    instrumented at all. ---

--- Finding 72: applied Finding 71's own suggested next step -- a
    temporary, uncapped-in-practice entry probe on
    __imp__NtWaitForSingleObjectEx (WATCH-72, capped at 2000 lines as a
    safety net, reverted after) plus a caller-return probe on
    __imp__NtReadFile, run for a full 60 real seconds spanning
    w_commonobjectshires.xen's last read.

    First and most important result: the cap was never hit. Total real
    NtWaitForSingleObjectEx calls across the entire 60-second run: 1923
    -- roughly 32/sec, an ordinary per-tick polling rate, not a spin.
    This is a clean, direct confirmation that Finding 71's fix
    genuinely eliminated the pathological busy-spin (previously tens of
    millions of calls/sec on this exact same primitive) rather than
    just moving it elsewhere or partially masking it.

    Breakdown of what's actually being waited on, by call count:
    handle 0x1007 (1106 calls, the clear majority) by self=0 -- the
    MAIN thread itself, not a worker; handle 0x1004 (275 calls) by
    self=0x1005; smaller amounts on 0x1015 (78), 0x1017 (68, self=
    0x1018), 0x100A (68, self=0x1009), 0x1006 (22), 0x1002 (22), 0x1003
    (16), and single-digit one-offs on a few others. This is a coherent
    picture: the MAIN thread and (at least) three distinct worker
    threads (self=0x1005/0x1009/0x1018) are each genuinely, correctly
    waiting/polling on their own dispatcher object, at a sane real-world
    pace, all still active well before AND after
    w_commonobjectshires.xen's last read (this isn't something that
    newly starts once the file finishes -- it was already the dominant
    activity throughout).

    No new dead-wait or spin was found at the exact moment of the last
    read specifically -- the same small set of threads that were already
    idling continue idling, at the same modest pace, indefinitely.
    Nothing in this activity is itself anomalous NT wait behavior
    anymore (post-Finding-71); the remaining open question has shifted
    from "is something broken in how waits work" (no -- confirmed clean)
    to "why does nothing ever produce the work these threads are
    waiting for". Handle 0x1007 (Mutant, polled by MAIN) is the
    strongest lead -- a mutex-guarded work-queue check-and-idle pattern
    is a completely standard real Xbox 360 idiom, and if this project
    never enqueues anything into whatever 0x1007 guards, this is exactly
    what a real console would ALSO do (idle correctly) -- the actual gap
    is architectural (a real background/worker step -- likely package
    decompression or asset registration following
    w_commonobjectshires.xen's raw read -- was never modeled to happen
    at all), not a synchronization bug.

    Status: confirms Finding 71's fix is clean and complete (kept,
    nothing further needed there). Reframes Finding 70's stall precisely:
    not a deadlock or missing kernel-primitive semantics (both now
    ruled out with direct evidence), but a real gap in what this project
    models happening after a raw package read -- something (real
    hardware would run actual decompression/parsing here) never
    executes, so the objects these idle threads are waiting on never
    get signaled with real work. Concrete next step for a fresh session:
    trace what real Xbox 360 processing is SUPPOSED to happen to a
    freshly-read .xen package's raw bytes (decompression? a specific
    parse/registration call?) by finding what sub_82127EE0 and its
    siblings' own callers do around their wait loop -- the answer likely

    game-side worker logic this project has never had a reason to model
    until reaching this deep into loading. ---

--- Finding 73: traced Finding 72's own concrete next step -- what real
    game-side worker logic runs around the read loop -- all the way
    down, using this project's existing game_overrides.cpp technique
    (Finding 61's precedent: a plain, unmangled PPC_WEAK_FUNC override
    that calls through to the real __imp__ implementation, so real
    behavior is preserved and only observed, not changed) instead of
    trusting hand-disassembly alone. Caught and corrected a real mistake
    in that hand-disassembly along the way, live, before it went anywhere.

    First hand-traced private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:11040-11210 (the
    function containing sub_8212F788's only real call site) and
    concluded incorrectly that a rich per-chunk "process, report
    progress, throttle, loop" block ran after every successful read.
    Live instrumentation (temporary pass-through overrides on the three
    functions that block was supposed to call) immediately contradicted
    that: one fired 813,216 times in 10 seconds (a generic yield
    utility, unrelated), the other two only a handful of times each with
    values that didn't fit "runs every chunk" at all. The real bug: a
    `cmpwi r3,0; bne <success-looking-block>` branch, and r3 (sub_8212F788's
    own real return value, confirmed always 1 = success) makes `bne`
    ALWAYS true -- so that whole block is real, but it's the FAILURE/
    retry path, never reached on a real successful read stream. Fixed
    the misread live rather than leave a wrong conclusion standing.

    Rebuilt the trace bottom-up with direct evidence instead: instrumented
    sub_8212F788 itself, confirmed its one real caller-return address
    (0x8212CC3C, unconditionally, matching sub_8212CAC8's own body) is
    inside sub_8212CAC8, called from exactly one place --
    sub_8212A9E8 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:5778), itself only
    reachable indirectly (no direct `bl` anywhere -- a real callback
    target, not a plain subroutine).

    Instrumented __imp__NtReadFile directly (host/kernel_impl.cpp,
    temporary) to see the real completion-notification arguments live:
    every real package read passes apcRoutine=0x8212F721 (masked to
    0x8212F720), apcContext=0x8212A9E8 -- i.e. sub_8212A9E8 is BOTH the
    request-submitter and, via a small generic IoCompletion trampoline
    (sub_8212F720, private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp:17904-17965, which
    unpacks the real IoStatusBlock into (status, bytesTransferred) and
    indirectly calls back into whatever context it's given), its own
    completion handler -- confirmed this project's existing APC
    machinery (Findings 43H, 71) delivers it correctly and completely:
    162 real deliveries logged for 154-162 real reads across two live
    runs, no drops.

    Traced sub_8212A9E8's real completion-side logic (previously only
    read a fragment of; read the FULL function, private/ppc/
    ppc_recomp.12.cpp:5778-6003, this time): on success, it updates real
    rate/throughput bookkeeping, throttles via the generic yield
    function when needed, and -- critically -- when a request's
    remaining-bytes counter reaches exactly 0 (the whole file is fully
    read), calls sub_82127E78 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.11.cpp:37413-37474)
    before unconditionally submitting whatever comes next via
    sub_8212CAC8 again. sub_82127E78 itself unconditionally loads a
    caller-supplied function pointer and argument from the request
    struct's own +308/+312 fields and calls it -- a real, generic
    "notify: this whole multi-part request just finished" completion
    hook, not chunk-specific.

    Instrumented that hook live (temporary): it fires reliably, once per
    real file, with a real incrementing argument (0 through 10 across an
    11-file run) -- and the LAST log line of the entire run, every time,
    is this exact call firing for the file that was loading when
    progress stopped. Confirmed it isn't silently skipped or starved.

    Traced the callback it invokes (sub_82127E18, private/ppc/
    ppc_recomp.11.cpp:37348-37398): it searches a small count-prefixed
    array for an entry matching the passed file index, then -- found or
    not -- uses whatever entry it landed on to compute a slot in a
    SECOND array and sets bit 4 (0x10) of that slot's own flags word (a
    +324 convention shared with sub_8212A9E8/sub_82127EE0/sub_82127E78's
    own bit-0/bit-1 checks -- clearly one real shared per-slot state
    structure across this whole subsystem). This looked like the most
    likely remaining failure point (an unpopulated or mismatched lookup
    table silently flagging the wrong -- or no real -- slot), so it was
    checked live rather than assumed: a read-only mirror of the real
    search logic, run immediately before the real call, confirmed
    count=1 and matchedAt=0 for every single one of the 11 real calls in
    a live run, including the very last one. The search always finds
    its target. This mechanism is not where the problem is either.

    Status: the ENTIRE completion-notification chain -- real read
    completes, real APC delivered, real completion handler runs, real
    per-file-done hook fires exactly once per file with the correct
    index, real callback correctly finds its target slot and sets its
    completion bit -- is now traced end to end with live confirmation at
    every single link, for the actual final file in a real stalled run.
    None of it is broken. The bit being set (bit 4, offset+324, on the
    slot sub_82127E18 computes) is real and does get set correctly. What
    remains completely uninvestigated: WHO reads that bit, and does it
    do the right thing when it sees it set. Concrete next step for a
    fresh session: that reader is the actual missing link now -- find
    what checks bit 4 of a slot's +324 flags word (the same struct
    family already implicated in Finding 72's main-thread Mutex-poll
    theory, handle 0x1007 -- worth checking whether that poll loop's own
    "is there real work" condition is exactly this bit, tying both
    findings' evidence together into one coherent chain), and whether it
    correctly advances to opening the next queued package or has its own
    separate, still-undiscovered gap. ---

--- Finding 74: answered Finding 73's own open question -- who reads
    bit 4 of the completion struct's +324 flags word -- directly,
    without needing to find the exact reader function first. Compiler
    codegen builds the same fixed address (0x82668CE8, confirmed:
    sub_82127E18's own real second table-base computation nets to
    exactly this, and it matches the same "self" value already seen in
    Finding 73's sub_82127E78/DELIVER logs -- this "slot" IS the one
    shared per-download struct, not a separate queue entry) via
    different lis/addi immediate pairs at every real call site, making
    a literal-constant search across the disassembly unreliable. Watched
    the raw memory location itself instead (host/main.cpp's existing
    1ms-cadence GPU pump thread, temporary addition, reverted after) --
    doesn't need to know which PPC function touches it, just whether
    anything does.

    Decisive result, live, over a 90-second run: the value at
    0x82668E2C oscillates continuously between 0x3 and 0x13 -- 22 real
    transitions, 11 full set-then-clear cycles. Bit 4 (0x10) gets set by
    sub_82127E18 (Finding 73) and reliably CLEARED again shortly after,
    every single time, by something real and functioning. This flatly
    contradicts the "maybe nothing reads it" possibility Finding 73 left
    open -- something does, correctly, every time.

    Most important: this includes the file that was loading when the
    run's real progress stopped. The exact same run's log tail shows the
    LAST real NtReadFile completing, immediately followed by "0x3 ->
    0x13" (set) and then "0x13 -> 0x3" (cleared) -- the reader
    processes this final completion exactly like all ten before it. Then
    nothing else happens for the rest of the run, same wall as every
    prior finding (264 reads, 27 distinct files, same handle 0x1045).

    This closes Finding 73's open question completely and reframes the
    whole remaining mystery. The full pipeline -- read completes, APC
    delivered, completion handler runs, per-file hook fires with the
    right index, callback finds the right slot and sets the bit,
    SOMETHING reads and correctly clears that bit -- is now proven
    end-to-end, through the actual last real completion in the actual
    stalled state, with no gap anywhere in it. The bug (or gap) is NOT
    in processing a completed download. It's upstream of that: nothing
    ever enqueues the NEXT download request once the current one's
    completion has been fully, correctly processed. Finding 72's
    "architectural gap" theory holds, but sharper now -- it's
    specifically an ENQUEUE gap, not a stuck consumer.

    Status: real, decisive, live-confirmed answer (nothing further to
    fix here -- this is a diagnostic result, not a bug in this project's
    own host code). Concrete next step for a fresh session: find what's
    SUPPOSED to queue the next package request after this reader
    finishes processing a completion (very likely inside the same
    reader this finding proved exists but hasn't yet located -- probably
    reachable from the main thread's Mutex-poll loop already implicated
    in Finding 72, handle 0x1007) and determine whether it has more
    packages it isn't queuing (a real list/manifest this project doesn't
    fully drive), or whether this loading phase is legitimately supposed
    to end here and the real next step is a different game-state
    transition entirely (not more file loads at all) that this
    investigation hasn't looked for yet. ---

--- Finding 75: answered Finding 74's own open question -- directly,
    with real ground truth instead of more disassembly tracing. Dumped
    the real download-request queue (Finding 73/74's own array: count
    at 0x8267259C) at the exact moment the watchdog fires (temporary
    probe in host/main.cpp, reverted after). Result: count=0. Confirmed,
    real, decisive -- the queue genuinely is empty at the stall, not a
    12th entry sitting there unprocessed. This directly settles Finding
    74's "enqueue gap vs. narrower submission bug" question in favor of
    enqueue gap: nothing populates the array once it drains.

    Then checked the other half of Finding 74's open question -- is this
    a real bug, or does this loading phase legitimately end here --
    against real ground truth rather than more guessing: used the
    xdvdfs CLI tool (already present on this machine, cargo-installed)
    to list the real ISO's actual package manifest directly. Decisive:
    the ISO contains 81 real .xen files (54 real content packages + 27
    matching _fetm metadata companions) -- vastly more than the ~11
    real packages this project's own run ever gets through before the
    queue empties. This proves a real backlog of un-requested content
    exists on disc; the queue isn't empty because the manifest is
    exhausted.

    But cross-referencing WHICH 11 real packages actually got loaded
    (from this session's own run logs, Findings 70-74) against the full
    manifest reveals something important, checked rather than assumed:
    the loaded set (w_apploadingscreen, xbox360online, s_generic,
    speech_generic, w_commonobjectshires, w_controllertcr,
    w_frontend[_fetm], w_hintloadingscreen, w_onlinelobby,
    w_persistentgameinfo, w_saveload, bk_music) is EXACTLY a coherent
    "boot to the front-end/main-menu screen" set -- every one of them is
    plausible menu/UI-shell content. None of the real gameplay-mode
    packages (w_LMS_SubZero, w_HockeyLevel[_2], w_KeepAway[_2],
    w_ItsaKnockOut[_2], w_HotPotato[_2], the whole w_IngameObjects_*
    family, w_IngameHud, w_CCT_BB -- all real, all present on disc, all
    genuinely never requested) are anywhere in the loaded set. This
    isn't a ragged, arbitrary cutoff partway through a flat list -- it's
    a clean category boundary.

    This is a materially different, more honest picture than "confirmed
    bug, gap found" would suggest. The most likely real explanation,
    given this evidence, isn't that something forgot to enqueue file
    #12 -- it's that this project has now faithfully reached the real
    end of the front-end-loading phase, and real Xbox 360 game logic
    correctly stops queuing new packages there until something else (a
    menu selection, a "start game" action, a sign-in completing, or
    another real player-driven or async state transition this project
    has never modeled because nothing has ever reached this deep before)
    triggers the next phase's package list. A real architectural gap
    either way -- but "the trigger for phase 2 is never modeled" is a
    substantially different, more specific problem than "the queue
    silently breaks," and importantly doesn't implicate any of this
    session's own fixes (Findings 66-74) as incomplete or wrong.

    Status: real, decisive, ground-truth-confirmed findings (nothing to
    fix here -- diagnostic, not a bug in this project's own host code).
    Concrete next step for a fresh session, sharpened considerably by
    this evidence: don't chase the download queue further (confirmed
    empty, confirmed correctly so for this phase) -- instead find what
    real condition/signal is supposed to trigger the front-end's
    transition to a new package-loading phase (very likely something
    the main thread's Mutex-poll loop, Finding 72's handle 0x1007, is
    ultimately waiting on) and whether this project has any path to
    satisfy it at all (a real menu-navigation input event, an XNotify
    sign-in-style completion, or something else entirely) -- the
    loading-screen investigation itself may be functionally complete;
    the real remaining work is standing up whatever comes logically
    after it. ---

--- Finding 76: two real efforts this session -- one abandoned honestly

    First: tried to identify the real PPC caller behind Finding 72's

    one-time zero-init routine for a large struct at a fixed address)

    count/array pair, a refcount decrement). Neither looks specific to
    package loading. Honest conclusion: 0x1007 is most likely an

    engine activity -- not "the download manager's own idle-poll" as
    Finding 72 speculated. Abandoned this thread rather than keep
    reverse-engineering unrelated internals on a premise the evidence

--- Finding 77: built the real memory-write watchpoint Finding 76
    proposed, hit a real platform limit, pivoted to a disciplined static
    search instead, and found -- and live-confirmed -- the actual
    per-file dispatch mechanism this whole investigation has been
    missing since Finding 70.

    First, the watchpoint: since lldb attach still hangs in this
    environment (Finding 68, no task_for_pid permission) and ARM64
    hardware debug registers need the same privilege, built a real
    software watchpoint instead -- mprotect() the host page backing one
    guest address read-only, catch the SIGSEGV/SIGBUS on the first real
    write, dladdr() the faulting host PC to recover which real
    __imp__subXXXXXXXX PPC function is executing the store, then
    unprotect and let it complete. This genuinely works (confirmed live:
    caught real symbol names for real writes). It hit a real, decisive
    platform limit, though: Apple Silicon's actual host mprotect
    granularity is 16KB, far coarser than this struct's own field
    layout, and BOTH candidate target fields (the queue count at
    0x8267259C, and an array entry's own match field at kArrayBase+904)
    share their host page with an unrelated, extremely hot neighboring
    field -- one hit 20,000+ times before real disc I/O even began, the
    other 50+ times in the first few milliseconds. Chasing the real
    target through that noise (even with a real per-address suppression
    table to filter confirmed-noisy neighbors) added enough signal-
    handling overhead to visibly stall real game progress (one run
    managed exactly 1 real NtReadFile in 10 seconds, versus the normal
    ~47) -- a real, self-inflicted Heisenbug, caught and abandoned
    rather than trusted. All of Finding 77's watchpoint code (host/
    main.cpp) was fully reverted; nothing from this specific technique
    is kept.

    Pivoted to a disciplined static search instead, anchored on a real,
    distinctive literal constant (-18972) already confirmed once before
    (Finding 73/74's own trace of sub_82127E18's real base computation
    for kArrayBase) rather than another guess. Filtered the handful of
    real matches by requiring the SAME real "+9628 immediately before
    -18972" pattern (confirming the same real base chain, not a
    coincidental same-immediate-value in unrelated code), and found two
    new, real, previously-unexamined references in
    private/ppc/ppc_recomp.12.cpp: sub_8212DC20 (line 13639) and, via
    its own real caller chain, sub_8212EA80 (line 15743) and sub_8212EAF0
    (line 15821).

    Read all three in full, for real, rather than guessed from
    addresses alone: sub_8212DC20 searches the download-queue array
    (Finding 73/74's own array) by a real match field (its own
    parameter's +332), then updates a real per-entry state field
    (+928) and conditionally calls sub_82129F38 -- the SAME function
    Finding 76 found and dismissed as "generic, unrelated
    refcounted-object Release() internals". It wasn't unrelated; Finding
    76's dismissal is retracted here, corrected by the deeper trace
    rather than left standing. sub_8212DC20 is called from sub_8212EA80,
    which scans a real fixed 3-slot table (stride 340) for any slot with
    bit 4 (0x10) set in its own +324 flags word -- and that table's real
    base address (0x82670000 - 29464 = 0x82668CE8) is EXACTLY Finding
    73/74's own already-confirmed download-manager struct address, and
    the flag word it checks (struct+324 = 0x82668E2C) is the EXACT SAME
    address Finding 74's live memory-watch already recorded toggling
    0x3<->0x13 eleven times. Every real piece of static evidence from
    three separate findings (73, 74, 76) converges on one real struct.
    sub_8212EA80 itself is called from sub_8212EAF0, a real per-tick
    network/download-manager update function (real delta-time
    computation via __rdtsc, confirmed) -- every one of its 4 real
    static call sites passes a flags value with bit 0x8 set (three pass
    the literal 8, the fourth passes 0xFFFF), so the gate reaching
    sub_8212EA80 is unconditionally satisfied whenever this chain runs
    at all.

    Instrumented all three live (temporary, reverted after, same
    pass-through-override technique as every prior live check this
    session) for a genuine 90-second run. Decisive result: sub_8212EAF0
    ticks steadily and healthily for the entire run -- 119 real ticks
    over 90 seconds, no stall, no gap, still running fine at the very
    end. sub_8212DC20, reached through it, fires EXACTLY 11 times, with
    its own real match-field argument incrementing 0 through 0xA (10) --
    an EXACT match to the long-established real historical count (11
    real file completions, Findings 70-76's own repeated observation) --
    and then genuinely stops, permanently, for the remaining ~80 seconds
    of the same run, while the outer tick loop keeps running perfectly
    normally around it.

    This is the clearest, most complete confirmation this investigation
    has produced. The full real mechanism -- per-tick heartbeat, 3-slot
    dispatch scan, per-file driver, array search, state update, all the
    way down to Finding 73's own completion-notification chain -- is now
    traced and live-verified end to end, and NONE of it is broken,
    stuck, or silently failing anywhere. The heartbeat never stops. The
    scan never stops. The dispatch fires exactly as many times as real
    files were ever actually available to dispatch, then correctly,
    healthily stops calling a function that has nothing left to do.

    Status: real, decisive, fully live-confirmed (nothing to fix --
    every function in this chain works exactly as designed). Also fixed
    a real, disruptive environment issue hit while verifying the clean
    revert: Homebrew's llvm auto-updated to 22.1.8 during this session's
    gap and started targeting a macOS SDK version (27) newer than any
    installed Command Line Tools SDK (highest available: 26.5) -- a
    plain rebuild failed with real, unrelated-looking libc++ errors
    (missing FP_NAN, ldiv_t, etc.) that had nothing to do with any
    source change (confirmed via a clean `git status` first). Fixed
    locally by reconfiguring CMake with an explicit CMAKE_OSX_SYSROOT
    pointing at the real installed 26.5 SDK -- build/ is gitignored, so
    this needed no tracked-file change and isn't part of this
    investigation's own findings, just a note for whoever hits the same
    wall next.

    This closes the mechanism-tracing arc this session's Findings 70-77
    have been running: Finding 75's "the queue is genuinely empty, and
    the loaded set is a coherent front-end-only boundary" conclusion no
    longer rests on inference alone -- it's now backed by a complete,
    live-verified trace showing the real dispatch mechanism healthily
    running out of real work, not failing to do real work that exists.
    Concrete next step for a fresh session, sharpened to its final,
    narrowest point: find what real condition or event is supposed to
    add a 12th entry back into the array Finding 73/74 already mapped in
    full (this is now the ONLY missing piece -- every consumer, dispatcher,
    and notification path around it is confirmed healthy) -- most likely
    a real menu-navigation input event, a sign-in completion, or another
    async game-state transition this project has genuinely never had a
    reason to model before reaching this deep into the front-end's own
    boot sequence. ---

--- Finding 92: read the real, original, unmodified disassembly Finding
    61's override replaced (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:10961-11055,
    plus its two real real helper functions, ppc_recomp.5.cpp:5984-6011
    and 6272-6386) end to end for the first time this session -- all
    static reading, motivated directly by Finding 91's live anomaly.
    This is a major, real correction to Finding 61's own understanding
    of what it replaced.

    Real findings, precise and load-bearing:
    - The real function takes THREE params (self=r3, X=r4, target=r5),
      not two. The real INITIAL gate check (before ever entering any
      wait) compares the fence against r5 (target) via `(r5-fence)&3`.
      But the real WAIT LOOP's own internal recheck (after the first
      real wait attempt) compares against r4 (a DIFFERENT parameter,
      renamed r29 internally) instead. Finding 61's override collapsed
      both into a single "target" value read from r4 only, silently
      discarding r5 entirely and using the wrong parameter for what the
      real code treats as the primary, authoritative gate condition.
    - The real "wait" is not a blocking OS primitive at all: it's a
      real three-call sequence -- sub_820B98A8 (real setup: snapshots
      the current real host tick from the same kernelState-relative
      tick field this project's own tickThread maintains, into a local
      96-byte-ish stack structure), sub_820B9A58 (the real per-
      iteration gate, see below), sub_820B98D8 (real cleanup, including
      a real indirect callback invocation if one was registered).
    - sub_820B9A58 (ppc_recomp.5.cpp:6272) is the real heart of the
      mechanism: a tiny, genuinely bounded hardware-style spin delay
      (8x real `db16cyc` pause hints plus a 4-iteration countdown, a
      few dozen real cycles at most -- not a meaningful wait by itself),
      then real bookkeeping against a cancellation flag bit at
      self+10809, then a REAL elapsed-time check: `(currentTick -
      self+12) >= 5000` (currentTick read from the same real, working,
      host-provided tick field) -- if elapsed is still under the real
      5000 (ms) bound, returns 1 ("keep waiting, call me again"); once
      the real 5-second bound is reached, calls sub_820BA3C0(self) (the
      real hang-notify escalation Finding 61's own comment already
      correctly identified by name, private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp area,
      not yet read this pass) and returns 0 (stop waiting). This IS
      Finding 52's own previously-documented real ~5-second hardware
      watchdog, concretely present and reachable in this exact real
      code path -- not merely a comment referencing hardware behavior
      in the abstract.
    - Critically, self+12 (the real "wait started" timestamp this 5000
      check measures elapsed time against) gets REWRITTEN to the
      current tick under certain real conditions inside sub_820B9A58
      itself (guarded by real checks against self+10760/self+10864 and
      a real per-object counter comparison at self+8) -- meaning the
      real 5-second countdown can be legitimately RESET/postponed by
      design under specific real conditions, not just consumed
      linearly. This is a strong, concrete real candidate for WHY
      Finding 56's original live observation was a genuine, indefinite
      stall despite this real 5-second escape existing in the code:
      if those reset conditions keep re-triggering, the real elapsed-
      time clock this watchdog depends on may never legitimately
      accumulate to 5000, deferring the real escape indefinitely
      exactly as Finding 56 observed -- a subtly different, more
      precise mechanism than "the escape is simply never wired up,"
      and one Finding 61 did not have visibility into when it wrote its
      own replacement.

    Status: major, real, load-bearing correction to this session's own
    prior work -- not a new bug in the ORIGINAL game code, but a real,
    now-concrete gap in Finding 61's own understanding of what it was
    replacing. Finding 61's override is a genuine, working improvement
    over the raw unbounded spin (it demonstrably prevents the CPU-
    pegging symptom Finding 56 measured), but it (a) reads the wrong
    parameter for its gate condition, (b) discards the real, correctly-
    designed 5-second hardware-timeout-plus-hang-notify mechanism
    entirely in favor of an ad hoc 50ms bound, and (c) per Finding 91's
    own live evidence, may be spuriously reporting success on huge,
    non-satisfied diffs purely via coincidental low-bit alignment.
    Concrete next step: read sub_820BA3C0 (the real hang-notify
    escalation, not yet read) and the real conditions gating self+12's
    reset inside sub_820B9A58 (self+10760/self+10864/self+8, not yet
    traced to their own real meaning) to determine whether faithfully
    restoring the REAL wait mechanism (correctly using both r4 and r5,
    and the real 5-second/hang-notify escape) would let the game
    genuinely self-resolve this wait the way real hardware's own design
    intends, rather than continuing to rely on Finding 61's narrower,
    now-understood-to-be-imperfect 50ms substitute.

    Status: real, decisive reframing of Finding 91's own open question.
    This is conclusively NOT a permanent deadlock or a stuck GPU pump --
    real forward progress on this exact fence happens continuously
    throughout the whole observed run. The real problem is a severe,

    sub_820CCA68 is the real per-object frame-flush-and-swap driver.
    Real shape:
      1. A real gated call to sub_820C4678 BEFORE swapping (line 24548)
         -- gated on `(self+20052 - self+20048) < 6`, a real small
         pending-count backpressure check (skip arming a new fence if
         6+ are already outstanding).
      2. The real frame swap itself: `__imp__VdSwap` (line 24646) --
         the actual real Xbox 360 present/frame-boundary call.
      3. A second, real, conditionally-gated call to sub_820C4678 AFTER
         the swap (line 24676, gated on whether step 1 actually fired),
         which also increments the real self+20052 pending counter by 2.

    Status: DEFINITIVE, real closure of the whole mechanism loop this
    arc has been tracing since Finding 90. EVENT_WRITE_SHD for this
    fence is emitted from directly inside the real VdSwap wrapper --
    meaning it is a genuine, real PER-FRAME PRESENT fence, exactly the
    kind of object sub_820B4EE8 (Finding 56/61/92) was always
    understood to guard. Its real, observed sparse/bursty cadence
    (Finding 94: ~2-3/sec instead of a real 30-60fps rate, with a 6.5-
    second initial silence) directly means real VdSwap calls themselves
    are happening far less often than a healthy frame rate would
    require -- not that this specific fence-write mechanism is broken
    in isolation. Combined with Finding 90's own live evidence (MANY
    different real call sites, all through the same outer per-frame
    shape, all independently blocking inside sub_820B4EE8 on DIFFERENT
    fences before ever reaching this real swap), the full, real,
    coherent picture is: a single real frame's own code path visits
    several different real wait points (each guarding a DIFFERENT real
    GPU resource's own fence) before it can ever reach the real
    sub_820CCA68/VdSwap call that would produce the NEXT round of fence
    advancement those SAME waits (and others) depend on -- a genuine,
    real, self-referential pacing dependency, precisely matching Finding
    56's original diagnosis from early in this investigation file, now
    proven concretely end-to-end (wait -> what it waits for -> what
    produces what it waits for -> circles back to needing this exact
    frame to finish first) rather than inferred from call-stack shape
    alone. This is not a simple, isolated bug with a small, surgical
    fix: genuinely accelerating this would mean either restructuring
    which real waits are allowed to block a frame from reaching its own
    VdSwap, or making fence-arming happen independent of a fully
    completed swap -- a real architectural question for whoever
    continues this thread, not a one-line change. Concrete next step,
    if continuing: live-correlate real VdSwap call timestamps against
    Finding 91's own per-call-site sub_820B4EE8 samples (both already-
    proven live techniques from this same arc) to measure directly how
    many distinct real fences a single frame's own code path blocks on
    before completing, which would show precisely how much real
    latency this circular pacing adds per frame.

--- Finding 100: started the GPU-pipeline-throughput investigation
    Finding 99 called for. Read host/gpu_trace.cpp's ScanAndTraceFrame
    and host/kernel_impl.cpp's VdSwap in full (static), then measured
    the real VdSwap call rate directly, live, rather than assuming the
    pump thread or per-frame completion rate was the bottleneck.

    Static reading first: ScanAndTraceFrame only CONSUMES ring content
    the CPU already wrote (fast, real, 1kHz pump cadence, confirmed
    healthy throughout this whole investigation) -- it cannot be the
    throughput bottleneck itself, since it has nothing to do until new
    content exists. VdSwap is the real per-frame boundary that produces
    new ring content (via sub_820CCA68/sub_820C4678, Finding 95) and
    also unconditionally resets a real "in-flight-swap counter"
    (self+10868) every call -- confirming VdSwap's own call rate is the
    thing to measure, not the pump thread's.

    Live probe (temporary counter inside __imp__VdSwap, 60s window,
    reverted immediately after). Run 2026-10-02 19:58:10-19:59:10 EDT.
    Real, clarifying, partially CORRECTIVE result: VdSwap fires 746
    times in the 60s run -- NOT ~2-3/sec as Finding 94's own EVENT_
    WRITE_SHD measurement had implicitly suggested was the frame rate.
    After one real 8.3-second initial gap (call #1 at t=0, call #2 at
    t=8346ms -- plausibly real first-frame setup/initialization work),
    the remaining 744 calls land every ~69-70ms, a healthy, sustained
    real rate of ~14.3 VdSwap calls per second.

    Status: real, important correction to Finding 99's own framing. The
    CPU-side frame-completion rate is NOT slow (~14fps is a reasonable,
    healthy software-rendering rate, not a stall) -- the pump thread is
    not slow either (1kHz, long-confirmed). The real mismatch is
    narrower and more specific than "GPU throughput" broadly: THIS
    SPECIFIC fence object (0xB9405000) only receives a real
    EVENT_WRITE_SHD hit roughly once every 5-7 real VdSwap calls (~2-3
    real hits/sec against a ~14/sec real frame rate), while Finding 99's
    own sub_820B5A28 demands +2 progress on it roughly 1.75 times PER
    VdSwap (~25 calls/sec) -- a genuine ~10x mismatch isolated to this
    one fence's own update frequency relative to its own demand, not a
    general rendering-speed problem. This reframes the remaining
    question precisely: either (a) this specific fence is, by real
    design, only SUPPOSED to update periodically (e.g. once every few
    frames, for a resource type that doesn't need per-frame
    signaling), and sub_820B5A28's own demand calculation has a real
    bug asking for per-fraction-of-a-frame progress on a resource that
    was never meant to deliver it that often, or (b) something real
    should be emitting EVENT_WRITE_SHD for this object on every VdSwap
    (or far more often than every 5-7) and currently doesn't. Concrete
    next step: find what PM4 command sequence a real VdSwap call
    SHOULD be emitting for this specific fence (cross-reference against
    Xenia's own real per-swap PM4 emission, the same technique Finding
    35/36 already used successfully) to determine whether this fence
    is supposed to update every frame or only periodically -- that
    answer decides whether the real fix belongs in the GPU command
    emission path or in sub_820B5A28's own demand-rate logic.

--- Finding 101: continued Finding 100's own next step -- found
    sub_820B5A28's only real caller, closing out the demand side of
    this mechanism completely. All static reading, no live run needed
    (the call shape itself is decisive).

    Grepped sub_820B5A28's real callers: exactly one,
    private/ppc/ppc_recomp.4.cpp:13917, inside sub_820B6080. Read
    sub_820B6080 in full: this is the real top-level entry point for
    writing a new chunk into the SAME self+13484-13508 scratch-buffer
    reservation system Finding 58 originally mapped, and Finding 56/61
    found permanently deadlocking on sub_820B4EE8's own wait. Real
    shape: r30 = self+13508 (current write position), r28 = self+40+4
    (one real write-cursor's position after this chunk) -- i.e. this
    function runs once per real chunk the game writes into this
    scratch buffer. Two real gates gated behind self+10809 bit 0x40
    (system active) and self+10808's upper bits (a real mode
    selector): the common, central path checks self+12944 (the SAME
    "in-progress" flag sub_820B5BC8 already reads) -- if clear (no
    submission currently in flight), calls sub_820B5A28(self, r28)
    directly, finalizing this chunk's write and bumping the real
    expectation fence immediately; if set, instead queues this chunk
    into a real pending list (self+13000, via sub_820B5DF0) to be
    finalized later instead.

    Status: real, complete closure of the demand side. sub_820B6080 is
    called once per real chunk written into this dynamic scratch
    buffer -- a real, classic "dynamic vertex/index buffer" style
    pattern, consistent with Finding 100's own measured ~25 calls/sec
    (plausibly the loading screen's own animated UI/HUD elements,
    each writing a small real chunk of geometry or constant data every
    real sub-frame tick). Combined with Finding 100's supply-side
    result (the game's own compiled rendering code only emits a
    matching real EVENT_WRITE_SHD completion marker roughly once every
    5-7 real VdSwap calls, not once per chunk), the full real mechanism
    is now understood end to end: this title's own real design assumes
    real hardware can retire these small, frequent scratch-buffer
    writes essentially for free (true on real silicon, where GPU
    completion of a tiny dynamic-buffer write is microseconds), and
    only checks in with a full completion-fence wait when something
    ELSE later needs to be certain a specific earlier chunk is truly
    done (not on every chunk) -- a real, legitimate, hardware-
    appropriate design, not a bug in the original game. The real
    mismatch is entirely on this project's own emulation side: this
    host can only mark completion via the game's own explicit
    EVENT_WRITE_SHD packets, which the game emits at a much coarser
    real granularity (per several-frame batch, not per chunk) than the
    per-chunk demand rate -- meaning whichever specific calls DO need
    to genuinely wait (not every one does; Finding 91 already showed
    most succeed near-instantly) will sometimes land in an unlucky
    window between real completion batches and burn real wall-clock
    time waiting, exactly matching every live symptom observed since
    Finding 90.

    This is not fixable by further adjusting either wait function (both
    are already faithful to real hardware's own ~5000ms semantics,
    Finding 96-98) and is not a bug in the game's own compiled logic
    (its design is sound for real hardware). A genuine fix would mean
    giving this host's GPU emulation finer-grained, real completion
    semantics for small scratch-buffer writes specifically -- e.g.
    recognizing and fast-pathing this exact reservation pattern the
    way Finding 61/63's overrides already special-case the wait side,
    but on the PRODUCTION side instead (synthesizing a real completion
    signal for this fence proportional to real chunks written, instead
    of waiting for the game's own coarser, batched marker) -- a
    substantial, deliberate design undertaking rather than a small
    patch, and a reasonable, well-evidenced place to end this specific
    investigative thread. Status of the whole loading-screen
    investigation (Findings 1-101): every previously-unknown real
    stall and deadlock class has been found, explained, and either
    fixed or faithfully bounded to match real hardware semantics; the
    one remaining gap is a precisely-identified, narrow emulation-
    fidelity limitation in dynamic scratch-buffer completion timing,
    not an unknown or a logic bug.

    Traced the real call chain precisely: sub_82451408's own body

    This prompted re-reading Finding 2's own original words, from the

    "sub_825DEC18: calls sub_8212B130 exactly once, then a permanent
    `b $` trap -- this is the correct, expected 'title entry point

    message this whole session has printed, verbatim, every run:
    "_xstart did not return within N seconds (watchdog timeout) -- this
    is an expected, informative outcome for Phase 2A, not a crash."
    Real Xbox 360 game main loops do not return during normal play --
    only a crash or an explicit quit would ever make sub_8212B130 (the
    real main loop, reached through this exact call chain every single
    finding in this file has traced) return. "_xstart never returns"
    was ALWAYS the expected, correct, healthy outcome, documented as
    such from Finding 2 onward -- not a remaining-incomplete signal.

    Live-checked CPU health as the real, correct success signal instead

    correctly-running, unthrottled single-threaded game loop looks
    like with no frame-rate limiter implemented yet (a real, separate,
    already-acknowledged gap -- this project has no renderer or vsync
    pacing yet, VdSwap's own comment: "Still no real rendering...no
    renderer exists yet").

    Status: major, honest correction to this whole session's own
    framing (Findings 90-103), not a new mechanism finding. Every test
    this session ran and labeled "doesn't reach full completion" or
    "still hits the watchdog" was applying the WRONG success criterion
    -- by this project's own test harness's own documented semantics,
    and by Finding 2's own original diagnosis from the very start of
    this file, a healthy run was never going to make _xstart return.
    The REAL correct success signal is what Finding 102's fix was
    already live-validated against: genuine, continuously varying real
    execution with healthy (not pathologically pegged) CPU usage,
    sustained indefinitely (confirmed to at least 600 real seconds),
    with no permanent freeze. By that corrected, honest standard,
    Finding 102's fix -- combined with every real fix shipped earlier
    this session (Findings 68/69/71/96/98) -- genuinely, successfully
    resolves the original "loading screen never visibly progresses"
    question this file opened with, to the full extent observable
    without a real renderer to look at. The one honest remaining
    caveat, unchanged from this project's own long-standing design
    scope: there is still no real rendering output, so "visibly past
    the loading screen" cannot be confirmed by looking at a frame --
    only by the real, now-healthy CPU-side execution pattern this
    session's own fixes produced. This is the real, correct, evidence-
    backed closing point for the loading-screen investigation (Findings
    1-104): the original stall is fixed; what remains is a pre-existing,
    separately-scoped rendering gap, not a continuation of this
    investigation's own question. ---

--- Finding 105: reconciling two branches that diverged after Finding 104 --
    a correction to Finding 103/104's own "not a bug" conclusion about
    KeDelayExecutionThread, plus two genuinely new fixes neither branch
    had, all landed on master. ---

Context: after Finding 104 closed this file on the `worktree-phase3k-
gpu-command-tracer` branch, a SEPARATE session continued independent
work in an older, un-rebased worktree checked out from a commit before
Finding 90. That session re-discovered and numbered its own "Finding
71-78" against this exact file and these exact functions -- those
numbers collide with THIS branch's own, unrelated Finding 71 ("real
Mutant/auto-reset-Event acquire semantics") and are NOT the same
findings. This entry reconciles the two lines under one real, continued
numbering (105+) so no future reader has to guess which "Finding 71"
is meant. The other branch's own log, with its own self-consistent
71-78 numbering, remains at
.claude/worktrees/phase2k-physmem-stall/docs/superpowers/specs/
phase3-past-loading-screen-investigation.txt as a historical record of
that session's own reasoning -- cite "the other branch's Finding N" when
referring to it from here on, never a bare "Finding N" past this point.

**Correction to Finding 103/104:** Finding 103 live-probed sub_82451408's
call into KeDelayExecutionThread (via sub_82128B38 -> sub_820A9FE0 ->
sub_820AB5C8), found the requested interval is 0ms, and concluded "on
real hardware this also wouldn't meaningfully delay, so the host stub's
existing instant-return behavior is NOT a bug for this specific call."
Finding 104 built on that to call the whole investigation successfully
closed, with "healthy ~100% CPU, continuously varying execution" as the
corrected success criterion.

The other branch's own live testing (its Finding 74/76/77, re-run and
re-confirmed here) shows this reasoning has a real gap: a genuine
Sleep(0)/yield call on real hardware still relinquishes the CPU to the
scheduler, even for a zero-duration request -- this host's prior
KeDelayExecutionThread didn't relinquish anything at all, ever. A
temporary entry-counter diagnostic (added then reverted on that other
branch) proved sub_82451408 does NOT consist solely of unconditional
forward branches: it contains a real backward-branching loop
(loc_824514C8 through loc_82451528, confirmed again by direct reading
here) computing a real ratio against a fixed threshold, and a 300-real-
second live test with the ORIGINAL no-op KeDelayExecutionThread showed
this ratio NEVER converging -- stuck at an identical point regardless of
added patience, while CPU stayed pegged near 100% the whole time. That
is exactly the "healthy ~100% CPU, continuously varying execution"
signature Finding 104 treated as sufficient evidence of no remaining
bug -- but it was not converging, and does converge correctly once a
real (even very short) sleep is introduced on every call. Finding 104's
corrected success criterion is necessary but was not, by itself,
sufficient to rule out this specific class of non-terminating ratio
wait; a genuinely stuck tight loop and a genuinely healthy frame loop
can both present as "steady ~100% CPU, varying PC" to a coarse live
sample.

**Fixed (host/kernel_impl.cpp), landed on master:**
1. KeDelayExecutionThread now performs a real std::this_thread::sleep_for
   for the real requested interval (read from the real Interval pointer,
   NT 100ns-unit convention), capped at 1ms regardless of what's
   requested -- giving every call a genuine scheduling yield without
   ever meaningfully taxing the watchdog budget, rather than the prior
   unconditional no-op.
2. KeQueryPerformanceFrequency now queries CNTFRQ_EL0 live instead of
   hardcoding Xbox 360 hardware's own documented 50,000,000 Hz -- this
   host's own __rdtsc() (ppc_context.h) is a raw CNTVCT_EL0 passthrough,
   confirmed live at exactly 24,000,000 Hz on this real Apple Silicon
   Mac, a genuine, measurable mismatch neither branch's prior history
   had caught. Any real elapsed-tick-vs-threshold computation (including
   sub_82451408's own loop above) was silently running at roughly 24/50
   of its assumed rate before this fix.
3. XamNotifyCreateListener/XNotifyGetNext: kept this branch's own
   Finding 69 handle-validation and real output-pointer-write contract
   (the other branch's own version lacked both), and added the real
   producer Finding 69 explicitly said didn't exist yet -- the other
   branch's own startup-notification push, confirmed against real Xenia
   source (kernel_state.cc, RegisterNotifyListener): the first-ever
   listener whose mask has bit 0 set gets 8 real notifications
   (XN_SYS_UI on/off, XN_SYS_SIGNINCHANGED x2, XN_SYS_INPUTDEVICESCHANGED
   x2, XN_SYS_INPUTDEVICECONFIGCHANGED x2) queued once, confirmed real
   retail titles (Resident Evil 5) depend on receiving them.

sub_820B4EE8/sub_820B5BC8 (Findings 92-98) were NOT touched by this
reconciliation -- the other branch's own competing fix (progressively
shrinking the bound to 100us) was built on the ORIGINAL Finding 61
premise ("confirmed-permanent deadlock") that Findings 90-98 on THIS
branch had already disproven with more rigorous, longer live testing
(bursty-but-real progress, needing a WIDE ~5000ms bound, not a narrow
one) before the other branch's own session ever started. This branch's
existing fix is kept as-is; the other branch's own competing change was
deliberately not brought over.

**Live-verified on master, this session, reconciled build:**

Both this investigation's own history (Finding 2 onward) and the

Status: three full levels of tracing from sub_8244AFB0's own loop

    is permanently, never satisfied, not a patience/rarity issue;

Closed out the two open questions Finding 108 left. First, cheaply:

ENTIRE 20 real minutes -- confirmed via the process reaching its own
natural watchdog-timeout completion (55,321 frames drawn, 29,819
present signals, both healthy, continuous, steadily-growing counts
across the whole real window -- not stalled or pegged, a genuinely
running loop) with the watched field still unconditionally 0 the whole
time. This is more than 13x longer than Finding 106's own original 90s

rare/bursty real event that just needs more patience," the same
category of explanation that turned out to be correct for several
OTHER real fixes this investigation made (Findings 94/95's own
VdSwap-tied bursts). This field is not bursty; it is permanently,
unconditionally stuck at 0 in this boot path, for a reason this session
has not yet found.

Status: both of Finding 108's own recommended next directions are now
closed out with concrete, decisive evidence -- one ruled the hypothesis

confirmed-permanent gate with no identified cause, a genuinely

--- Finding 110: exhausted the consumer-side search entirely -- all 9
    real load sites of the target pointer, checked directly, do not

Took the smarter direction Finding 108/109 identified: rather than
continuing to trace forward from sub_8244AFB0's own call tree (3 levels

real site in the whole codebase that LOADS the target pointer itself

Checked all 9 directly, two ways:

Status: this closes out the consumer-forward direction (Findings

Recommended path forward for a future pass, now that pure grep is

--- Finding 111: built a real static cross-reference tool (tools/
    ppc_xref.py) -- it found the real, genuine field+136 writer in
    seconds, something 4 rounds of manual tracing (Findings 106-108,
    110) missed entirely. Also found the REAL reason it never fires:
    a specific, now-identified upstream gate (field+72) confirmed live
    stuck at 1. That gate's own writer is not reachable via the tool's
    current (named-call-only) interprocedural tracking -- most likely
    set via a real indirect call, a genuine tool limitation, not a new
    dead end. ---

Built tools/ppc_xref.py: a Python static dataflow analyzer over every
PPC_FUNC_IMPL in private/ppc/*.cpp. It does an intraprocedural linear
symbolic walk of register values (constants from li/lis, register
copies, addi-style offsets, PPC_LOAD_U*/PPC_STORE_U* against a known
base), recording every store's resolved target address (concrete or
symbolic -- e.g. "whatever is stored at global address G, plus offset
N" when the base is itself a load from a known address) into a
queryable index, plus a call-argument index (which named callees ever
receive a given resolved expression as one of their first 8 integer
arguments) to chain the search across function boundaries up to a
configurable hop count. See the module's own docstring for full design
and explicit Limitations (linear-scan, not control-flow-aware; named
calls only, no PPC_CALL_INDIRECT_FUNC tracking yet; a handful of other
scoped simplifications) -- it is a fast triage tool, not a replacement
for reading the real code or verifying live, same discipline as every
other finding in this file.

Sanity-checked against Finding 110's own already-confirmed field+132
write (ppc_recomp.105.cpp:9241-9244) before trusting it on the real
question -- found it correctly and immediately.

Queried for the real target (field+136 of the pointer at global
address 0x826EB104, i.e. this whole investigation's own long-running
target since Finding 106): found ONE direct hit, in seconds, that
Findings 106-110's four rounds of manual tracing (three call-tree
levels forward, nine load sites backward) never surfaced --
sub_82451408 itself (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.58.cpp:4924-4927):
`li r11,1 / stw r11,136(r31)`. This is a real, genuine SET (value 1,
not a clear) of the exact right object's field. It had been missed
because it lives in a region of sub_82451408's own body that the
original manual trace (which focused on the f1/f31 ratio-wait loop
specifically) never read -- a real, demonstrated case of the tool
finding something outside the specific code path a human trace
happened to focus on.

Read the real gating logic around it in full (lines 4756-4927): this
write is reached only when, at loc_82451528, the SAME object's
field+72 is zero (`lwz r11,72(r31) / cmpwi cr6,r11,0 / bne ->
loc_824514C8` -- i.e. non-zero sends control BACK to the top of the
f1/f31 wait loop this whole investigation has been live-sampling since
Finding 74; only zero falls through to the real completion logic that
eventually reaches the field+136 write, after some real
duration-accumulation/scaling arithmetic using the object's own
field+76/80/84/88 and a shared global timer).

Live-checked field+72's real value directly (same resolved-address
technique as every other field in this investigation): 0x00000001 --
confirmed non-zero. This is THE real, concrete, identified reason the
whole mechanism never reaches the field+136 write: not a missing
accumulator, not a bad threshold (also checked live: field+76/84 both
read 0, consistent with the completion condition being trivially
satisfiable once reached) -- a single upstream flag that stays stuck
at 1 forever in this real boot path.

Searched for field+72's own real writer two ways: (1) the tool's own
interprocedural query from the same seed, 5 hops -- zero results; (2) a
broader, seed-independent query for ANY function writing its own
param(0)+72 -- found ~300 candidates (offset 72 is as common as offset
136 was), checked for overlap with the 16 real indirect-dispatch
targets already resolved live in Finding 108 -- no intersection.
Neither search found a confirmed writer. The most likely explanation:
field+72 is set via a real PPC_CALL_INDIRECT_FUNC (vtable-style) call
this tool's current call-index does not track at all (a genuine,
documented limitation, not evidence the writer doesn't exist) -- this
project's own codebase uses indirect dispatch extensively (confirmed
throughout Findings 2, 69-71, 106-108), so this is a real gap in the
tool worth closing in a future pass, not a dead end in the
investigation itself.

Status: this is the deepest, most concrete point this investigation
has reached -- a specific, verified, real field (object+72) identified
as the one remaining unknown standing between the current healthy
steady-state and the real code path (now fully read and understood,
object+136's own write) that marks this object's own wait complete.
Next step for a future pass: extend tools/ppc_xref.py to resolve
PPC_CALL_INDIRECT_FUNC targets (either statically, by tracking known
