vtable-pointer values the same way the rest of the tool tracks
pointers, or by feeding it the live-resolved target lists this
session's own earlier manual diagnostics already produced for several
real dispatch sites) and re-run the field+72 query with that
capability added.

tools/ppc_xref.py and its .gitignore entries (tools/.ppc_xref_cache.json,
tools/__pycache__/) are committed as a real, reusable project asset --
not a one-off throwaway script.

--- Finding 112: decisive conclusion -- field+72 is confirmed
    permanently stuck at 1 via a direct hardware watchpoint (two
    independent 20-minute runs), with zero writer found via an
    extended version of the Finding 111 tool (now offset-aware and
    indirect-call-site-tracking) either. This is the honest, final
    state of this specific investigative thread. ---

Extended tools/ppc_xref.py (Finding 111) two ways before this final
live test: (1) fixed an offset-matching gap -- the original
interprocedural search only matched an argument that was EXACTLY the
seed expression, missing the real, common pattern (already seen in
this same investigation, Finding 110's own sub_8244B810 example) of a
caller passing `object+K` as a sub-object pointer; added
match_seed_with_offset to handle this, propagating the adjusted target
offset through the recursion. (2) added tracking for real
PPC_CALL_INDIRECT_FUNC sites (5877 found codebase-wide) as a distinct,
target-unresolved call type, queryable the same way as named calls, so
a short list of "indirect call sites reachable with this object as an
argument" can be generated for live verification instead of guessing.

Re-ran the field+72 query with both fixes: zero direct/interprocedural
writes (same negative as before the fix -- the offset-matching gap
wasn't the reason this search came up empty), and zero reachable
indirect-call sites either, within 5 hops, from the same real object
seed. The sanity check (field+136's own already-confirmed write) still
passes correctly, confirming the tool itself is working -- this is a
genuine negative result, not a bug in the search.

Given static analysis (now meaningfully more capable than Finding 111's
first version) still found nothing, ran the most direct test possible:
a real hardware watchpoint on field+72's own resolved live address
(0x70B9163D88), for two full independent 20-minute (1200s) runs. Zero
genuine write events in either run -- only the initial watchpoint-
creation report, identical to Finding 109's own result for field+136
and to this same field's own first, earlier check. Both runs completed
to their own natural watchdog exit with healthy, continuously-growing
real activity throughout (71,810 frames / 32,472 present signals in
this run's own full window) -- not stalled, not frozen, just never
writing this one specific field.

This is now a triply-confirmed, maximally-verified negative result:
(a) exhaustive forward call-tree tracing (Findings 106-108, 3 levels),
(b) exhaustive backward load-site tracing (Finding 110, all 9 real
references to the containing object), (c) a purpose-built, twice-
extended static cross-reference tool checking both named-call and
indirect-call interprocedural paths (Finding 111 + this finding), and
(d) two independent 20-minute real hardware watchpoints directly on
the field's own live memory. All four, independent techniques agree:
field+72 is permanently 1 in this real boot path, and no code this
session can find -- by any method tried -- ever touches it.

**Conclusion for this investigative thread:** the real, final blocker
keeping this title's own loading sequence from ever completing is now
fully diagnosed down to one specific, named, verified field on one
specific, identified object (self+10768-based structure, field+72),
with the real completion logic that WOULD fire once it clears fully
read and understood (sub_82451408:4756-4927). Why nothing ever clears
it remains unknown after exhausting every static and live technique
this session has available. The most likely remaining explanations,
in order of plausibility: (1) it's set once at real object construction
to a value this project's own asset-loading pipeline never fully
replicates (e.g. a real field normally populated by parsing additional
real data this project's own XDVDFS/package-reading code doesn't yet
decode deeply enough to reach the specific sub-structure that would
flip it); (2) it depends on a real subsystem this project has not yet
implemented at all (network, achievements, or another real XAM service
whose own real completion callback is what the game expects to clear
this flag); (3) it is read by a real DMA-driven or hardware-interrupt
driven path this CPU-side static analysis category cannot see by
construction, not just by tooling limitation.

Recommended for a genuinely different future pass, since this
session's own toolkit (manual tracing, the custom xref tool, live
watchpoints) has been exhausted on this specific question: compare
against a REAL reference implementation if one exists for this exact
title or a close sibling (this project's own established practice of
checking Xenia/skate3recomp-style real reference source for ground
truth, used successfully throughout Findings 69-78), specifically for
whatever real engine subsystem initializes or completes self+10768's
own field+72 -- a real symbol name or comment in a reference
implementation would very likely resolve in minutes what this
session's own blind static/dynamic search could not in many hours.

tools/ppc_xref.py's two extensions (offset-aware matching, indirect-
call tracking) are a real, permanent improvement to the tool and are
committed as part of this finding, alongside the finding itself.

--- Finding 113: lldb process-attach unblocked (user granted Developer
    Tools access to the real controlling terminal app, Warp, after an
    earlier grant to Terminal.app didn't apply since Warp -- not
    Terminal.app -- is this session's actual process-tree root);
    corrects Finding 110-112's own field+72 framing and pinpoints the
    REAL blocking field as field+76 of the same object. ---

With live attach restored, re-examined sub_82451408 (private/ppc/
ppc_recomp.58.cpp:4600-4927) from its real top instead of the middle
slice Findings 110-112 worked from.

First, a live sanity check corrected a misreading: field+72 (host
0x70B9163D88) was read live, pre-breakpoint, within ~1s of process
start, and was ALREADY 0x00000001 (real big-endian value 1) -- not 0,
and not something any of this session's attempts set. Re-reading the
real branch direction at loc_82451528 (line 4780: `bne cr6,
0x824514c8` -- branches to loc_824514C8 when field+72 IS nonzero)
shows field+72==1 selects a real-timebase (mftb/rdtsc-based) elapsed-
time computation path rather than a ratio-division path; both paths
converge back into the same loc_82451534 continuation. field+72 is a
mode selector, not a block -- Findings 111/112's "field+72 is the
permanent blocker" framing was wrong. (A live memory-write attempt
during this correction used the wrong byte order (wrote raw bytes
`01 00 00 00` instead of `00 00 00 01`), briefly setting field+72 to
the real value 0x01000000 instead of 1 in one now-exited diagnostic
process; this had no effect since the mode-selector branch treats any
nonzero value identically, and the process was never kept alive
afterward -- no state persists from this.)

Reading the function from its real start (line 4600) shows r31 (this
object) is reloaded fresh every call via the same canonical global
pointer chain this whole investigation has used throughout (lis
r11,-32145 / lwz r31,-20220(r11) -- guest 0x826EB104 -> 0xB9163D40).
Every call recomputes a fresh elapsed-time value via mftb/sub_820A7248
and funnels it into loc_82451534's real duration-accumulation block
(lines 4782-4927, same block Finding 111 already read). The actual
gate on ever reaching `stw r11,136(r31)` (line 4926-4927, the real
write Finding 111 found) is the pair of `ble` branches at lines 4908
and 4922: the first compares field+76 (loaded at line 4874: `lwz
r10,76(r31)`) against 0 -- when field+76 <= 0, line 4908's `ble`
ALWAYS skips straight to the function epilogue (loc_82451618),
regardless of how much time has accumulated or how many times this
function is called. field+76 is live-confirmed to be 0 every time
this session read it (both via manual breakpoint-stop reads and the
dedicated watchpoint below). field+76, not field+72, is the real
permanent blocker.

Live-confirmed sub_82451408 IS being called repeatedly (breakpoint
hit count climbed to 5 across a handful of manual `continue`s within
about a second of wall time -- this function runs often, not rarely),
and its accumulator-adjacent fields (+76, +84, +88, all read via the
same fixed host addresses as +72) stayed at exactly 0 across every
one of those calls. field+80 reads as the real IEEE-754 float 1.0,
confirming the live memory read/byte-order interpretation itself is
correct (a plausible real value, not noise).

Re-ran tools/ppc_xref.py against the corrected target:
  python3 tools/ppc_xref.py --find-writes 0x826EB104 --deref --offset 76 --hops 4
  python3 tools/ppc_xref.py --find-writes 0x826EB104 --deref --offset 76 --hops 4 --find-indirect
Both return zero hits -- the same static-search dead end Findings
110-112 hit for field+72/136, now reproduced exactly for field+76.

Ran a dedicated real hardware watchpoint on field+76 itself (host
0x70B9163D8C, attached ~1s after process start -- the earliest this
object is reliably mapped) for a full standard 10s run: only the
watchpoint's own initial-creation report fired (the same "new value"
artifact this project's own established methodology (Findings 109,
112) already knows to discount), zero genuine write events before the
process exited normally via the watchdog. field+76 is never written
during this object's entire observed live lifetime, matching the
static tool's own negative result.

Conclusion: the real, corrected permanent blocker is field+76 of the
object at guest 0xB9163D40 (host 0x70B9163D40), staying at 0 for this
object's entire life, which unconditionally prevents field+136 (the
flag sub_8244AFB0's own outer loop -- Finding 106 -- waits on) from
ever being set by sub_82451408. Three independent techniques (manual
read-the-whole-function tracing, the static xref tool, a dedicated
live hardware watchpoint from near process start) now agree on this
specific field, for the same reasons Findings 110-112 already
documented for the superficially similar field+72/136: no statically
or dynamically observable write exists in this session's own toolkit.
The same three candidate explanations Finding 112 already listed
(uninitialized-by-design pending a real subsystem this project hasn't
implemented; a real DMA/interrupt-driven write outside CPU-side static
analysis; a real reference-implementation comparison being the fastest
way to actually resolve it) still apply, now pointed at the correct
field.

Diagnostic-only changes (temporary host/main.cpp watchdog extensions
used during this finding's live tests) all reverted; confirmed via
`git diff --stat host/main.cpp` showing no changes before this commit.

--- Finding 114: constructor hunt was inconclusive (debugger-timing
    artifact); a live full-process thread census rules out a stuck/
    deadlocked worker thread; UnleashedRecomp (hedge-dev, real retail
    Sonic Unleashed PC port via the same XenonRecomp toolchain) checked
    as a real reference project and gives one useful negative result. ---

Attempted to catch the real constructor of the object at guest
0xB9163D40 by breaking at `main` (before any guest code runs) and
watching the pointer slot (host 0x70826EB104) for its first real write,
instead of attaching after launch (which this session's many earlier
tests always found already-set, too late to observe the write).

This technique backfired: stopping at `main` delays process execution
by however long it takes a human to issue the next lldb command, which
is long enough to change the relative scheduling order of the ~10
threads this title's own early boot spawns via ExCreateThread (visible
in its own startup log). The watchpoint did fire with a genuine
old-value-0 write, from a real, previously-untraced call chain
(__imp___xstart -> sub_825DEC18 -> sub_8212B130 -> sub_8244B040,
private/ppc/ppc_recomp.57.cpp:1944), but the captured value
(0x403d16b9, which reinterprets as the plausible IEEE-754 float
~2.955, not a plausible guest heap pointer in this title's normal
0x8xxxxxxx/0xBxxxxxxx range) is consistent with a vector/struct copy
instruction (`stur q0,[x20,#0x28]`, a 16-byte NEON store) writing
unrelated float/vector data through this address because of a
debugger-perturbed, different heap layout, not a real assignment to
this pointer slot. Not trusted as a real finding about the pointer's
actual constructor; reading sub_8244B040 (a generic pooled-allocator-
shaped routine, calling sub_8212DAE8/sub_8212B0A0/sub_8212D2E0) in
isolation did not resolve this ambiguity either.

A separate, cleaner live technique did produce a decisive, trustworthy
result: attached normally (no break-at-main perturbation) ~5s into a
run and took a full `thread list` + `bt all` census of the entire
process (12 threads). Findings:
- Thread #4 is the real guest main thread (the one running __xstart's
  own call chain via std::async) and is ACTIVELY EXECUTING, not
  blocked -- caught deep in a real, live call chain: __imp___xstart ->
  sub_825DEC18 -> sub_8212B130 -> sub_8244B040 -> sub_8244AFB0 ->
  sub_8244AE70 -> sub_82461D28 -> sub_8240A0D0 -> sub_82407EF0 ->
  sub_82446D50 -> sub_82438270 -> sub_82127930 -> sub_823F31A0 ->
  sub_823F52D0 -> sub_823F5238 -> sub_823FA610. This corrects this
  same finding's own constructor-hunt framing above: sub_8244B040 is
  NOT a one-time startup constructor -- it is called FROM WITHIN
  sub_8244AFB0's own stuck loop (Finding 106), every iteration. The
  main thread is doing substantial real work every cycle, not spinning
  on a trivial unmet condition.
- Five threads (#6, #7, #8, #9, #10) are idle in the exact same real
  wait chain: NtWaitForSingleObjectEx -> sub_820AB238 -> sub_8212B210
  -> sub_820ABCE8 (the XApiThreadStartup trampoline). This matches
  host/kernel_impl.cpp's own documented 5-worker completion-queue pool
  (Findings 15/17) exactly -- these threads are correctly idling,
  waiting to be dispatched work that apparently never arrives, not
  deadlocked or buggy themselves.
- One thread (#11) is idle in KeWaitForSingleObject via sub_820C8D28
  directly (not the generic pool dispatcher) -- a specific, distinct
  wait.
- One thread (#12) is idle in NtWaitForSingleObjectEx via a different
  caller, sub_82496C28 -- also a specific, distinct wait.
- Threads #2/#3 are this project's own host-side helper threads
  (`main::$_0`/`main::$_1`), not guest code; thread #1 is the primary
  host thread blocked in the watchdog's own `future::wait_for`, as
  expected.

Conclusion: no deadlocked or stuck worker thread exists. The real
blocker (field+76, Finding 113) is not explained by any thread failing
to run -- the thread that would need to set it (whichever one that is)
is either the busy main thread itself (which never reaches the real
write) or never gets dispatched work by the main thread's own loop
because that loop's real per-iteration work (the sub_8244B040 call
tree) doesn't trigger it.

Checked github.com/hedge-dev/UnleashedRecomp (a real, complete, 5000+-
star retail Xbox 360 Sonic Unleashed PC port using the same
XenonRecomp toolchain) as a reference implementation, per this
project's own established practice of checking real reference source
for ground truth. Its kernel/imports.cpp stubs VdSetGraphicsInterrupt-
Callback, VdSwap, VdInitializeRingBuffer, and VdEnableRingBufferRPtr-
WriteBack completely (just a log line, no real implementation) and the
game still runs and plays correctly -- a real, decisive negative
result against this session's earlier (already-abandoned) hypothesis
that a missing real GPU-interrupt/vblank callback invocation is a
universal cause of loading-screen stalls in XenonRecomp-based
projects. Its own KeDelayExecutionThread sleeps for the real full
requested duration (no 1ms cap, unlike this project's own deliberate
cap -- not directly comparable, since that title has no equivalent
watchdog-timeout constraint). Its ExCreateThread-equivalent and
wait-primitive implementations were not yet compared in similar depth
to this project's own (a reasonable next step if this specific
reference project's own source turns out to implement an equivalent
real subsystem this project hasn't).

No source or binary changes in this finding; no diagnostic main.cpp
edits were left uncommitted (confirmed via `git diff --stat
host/main.cpp` showing no changes).

Finding 115: UnleashedRecomp kernel-surface diff finds real stub-
fidelity gaps, but the known hot call chain makes zero direct kernel
calls -- rules out "missing kernel primitive on this path" as the
field+76 (Finding 113) explanation.

Followed up on Finding 114's own closing note: compared this
project's host/kernel_impl.cpp and host/kernel_stubs.cpp against
UnleashedRecomp's kernel/imports.cpp (fetched via `gh api
repos/hedge-dev/UnleashedRecomp/contents/UnleashedRecomp/kernel/
imports.cpp`) for Ex/Ke/Nt/Rtl/Mm/Ob/Io/Hal/Dbg-prefixed functions.

Cross-referenced against this project's own title's actual 123-entry
import table (host/kernel_imports.txt) rather than UnleashedRecomp's
full import surface -- the two titles import different function sets
and a raw diff against UnleashedRecomp's surface produces false
positives (e.g. NtCreateSemaphore/NtReleaseSemaphore/
NtWaitForMultipleObjectsEx, which UnleashedRecomp imports but this
project's own binary never does).

Found three real stub-fidelity gaps among functions this project's
binary DOES import:
- KeReleaseSemaphore (host/kernel_stubs.cpp): pure no-op, logs and
  returns 0, never touches any semaphore state. However
  KeInitializeSemaphore (host/kernel_impl.cpp:1229) already carries a
  prior-session comment: "No-op -- nothing observed waits on or
  releases this semaphore" -- i.e. this exact gap was already
  investigated and deliberately left as a documented no-op, not an
  oversight. Not re-litigated further this finding.
- NtResumeThread / NtSuspendThread (host/kernel_stubs.cpp): blind
  no-ops, no investigation comment. Checked whether this matters:
  ExCreateThread (host/kernel_impl.cpp:1248) never reads any
  CREATE_SUSPENDED-style creation-flags argument at all and always
  starts the new std::thread immediately. So even if the real guest
  creates a thread suspended and later calls NtResumeThread to start
  it, this project's threads are already running by then -- the
  mismatch only causes threads to start *earlier* than the real
  console would, never *never*. Wrong direction to explain a stall.
- RtlTryEnterCriticalSection (host/kernel_stubs.cpp): blind no-op,
  always returns 0 (BOOLEAN false/failed-to-enter). This IS the right
  shape for a stall if a real spin-loop pattern depends on it
  eventually succeeding. Traced its 4 real call sites (private/ppc/
  ppc_func_mapping.cpp, ppc_recomp.12/38/46.cpp); the one in
  ppc_recomp.12.cpp is inside sub_82128610 (line 114-127), called only
  from sub_82462A30 (ppc_recomp.59.cpp:26997), neither of which
  appears anywhere in the known field+76 hot call chain (sub_8244AFB0,
  sub_8244B040, sub_82451408, and the 7 leaf functions sub_82451408
  calls, confirmed live via breakpoint hit-counts in Finding 113/114).

Then checked the hot chain directly rather than guessing further:
grepped the full body of each of the 10 known hot-chain functions
(sub_8244AFB0, sub_8244B040, sub_82451408, sub_82453B00, sub_820A50F0,
sub_820A9FD0, sub_8212DAE8, sub_8212D2E0, sub_825DEC18, sub_8212B130)
for any `__imp__<Ex|Ke|Nt|Rtl|Mm|Ob|Io|Hal|Dbg|Xam|Vd>` call. Zero
hits in all 10. This call chain is pure in-guest computation -- it
makes no kernel/syscall calls at all, directly. Combined with the
already-zero static-xref and live-watchpoint results for field+76
itself (Finding 113), this means: whatever is supposed to write
field+76 is not reachable from this traced chain by a missing or
broken kernel primitive on this path. The writer, if one exists, is
still in code this investigation has not yet located -- the kernel
diff narrows "where to keep looking" (not in these 10 functions' own
direct syscalls) rather than finding the writer.

No source or binary changes in this finding; no diagnostic main.cpp
edits were left uncommitted (confirmed via `git diff --stat
host/main.cpp` showing no changes).

Finding 116: field+76's real writer found at last -- sub_82451630,
called once early in boot. It genuinely writes field+76, and in this
run writes it to 0 via a config-lookup fallback chain, not because
nothing ever writes it.

Technique: instead of anchoring a search at the known object's
canonical pointer (exhausted six times over, Findings 106-115),
grepped raw XenonRecomp-preserved asm comments across all of
private/ppc/*.cpp for any store with immediate offset 76 (`// st(w|h|
b|fd|fs)...,76(`) -- 616 hits, far too many to read individually
since offset 76 is a generic, widely-reused struct offset across
unrelated objects throughout the 25MB binary. Narrowed by
intersecting against the small, already-established whitelist of
functions that ever dereference THIS object's canonical global
pointer at all (found by grepping for the exact literal pair `lis
r11,-32145` / `lwz rN,-20220(r11)` that every known reader of this
object uses, e.g. sub_82451408's own r31 setup) -- only 10 functions
in the entire codebase ever do this, a tractable set. Cross-checking
each of those 10 functions' bodies against the 616-hit offset-76 list
found exactly one match: sub_82451630 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.58.cpp,
function spans lines 4956-6397), which loads the object pointer into
r23 at entry (line 4981, confirmed never reassigned before the write)
and stores to r23+76 at line 5692.

sub_82451630's only real caller is inside sub_8244B040 (private/ppc/
ppc_recomp.57.cpp:2448), the same per-iteration function already
confirmed live (Finding 114) to run repeatedly on the main guest
thread. Live-confirmed via a breakpoint on `__imp__sub_82451630`
(symbol present, unstripped) that it is called exactly ONCE across a
full watchdog-timeout run -- very early in boot, well before the bulk
of MmAllocatePhysicalMemoryEx allocation traffic. This resolves what
looked like a contradiction: the call site is reachable every
iteration per the static read, but something upstream in
sub_8244B040 only takes that branch on the iteration(s) where some
precondition holds, and that's apparently just the first one. (Which
specific upstream condition gates repeat calls was not traced this
finding -- not needed to answer "who writes field+76".)

Used `thread step-out` at that single breakpoint hit to run the whole
function to completion while still attached, then read field+76 live
via the same fixed-address memory-read technique as Finding 113 (no
register-name dependency, so no debugger-timing-perturbation concern
applies here the way it did for the Finding 114 constructor hunt --
this read happens after a confirmed real return, not during a
perturbed race). Result: field+76 == 0 after the one real call
completes. Confirmed this is NOT "the write never executed" by
reading sub_82451630's actual branch structure around the write
(lines 5592-5692): there are two disjoint ways to reach the write,
with two different sources for the stored value --
  (a) loc_82451B04: r11 = return value of sub_823ED898(...) (a real
      function call result)
  (b) loc_82451B14/B18/B20 (straight fallthrough): r11 =
      PPC_LOAD_U32(0x8244F784) (a hardcoded-looking global fallback,
      confirmed live to hold a real nonzero value, 0xD0030020, this
      run)
Since field+76 ended up 0 while the (b) global is nonzero, path (a)
was the one actually taken -- sub_823ED898's return value was 0 in
this run, and that's what landed in field+76. This is a real,
traced, explained result, not an unresolved negative.

Followed sub_823ED898 to understand what "0" means: sub_823ED898 ->
sub_823EEEF8 -> sub_823EEC00 (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.47.cpp:27031,
ppc_recomp.48.cpp:1503 and 1056). Reading sub_823EEC00's body (radix
bounds-checked against 2 and 36 at lines 1117-1124, byte-at-a-time
digit scan) identifies it as a strtol-shaped string-to-integer parser
with a 0 default -- not a dictionary/config-key lookup as first
guessed from the wrapper names alone. Its string argument traces back
to a global pointer slot read repeatedly throughout sub_82451630 via
the literal pair `lis r11,-32143` / `...,-29792(r11)`, resolving to
absolute guest address 0x82448BA0.

Checked who writes 0x82448BA0: `ppc_xref.py --find-writes 0x82448BA0
--hops 4` found zero writers (the same static-search blind spot this
investigation has hit repeatedly -- see Limitations in the tool's own
docstring). A live read of that address at the sub_82451630 breakpoint
gave 0x396B0040 -- NOT flagged as a confirmed result: this value
doesn't fit any guest address pattern observed elsewhere in this
project (code/data is 0x8xxxxxxx, runtime heap allocations are
0xA0xxxxxx/0xB9xxxxxx), which could mean either genuinely
uninitialized memory at this read's timing, or that this offset
derivation has a mistake not yet caught. Flagged explicitly as
unverified -- a reasonable next step, not a conclusion to build on
yet.

Net result of this finding: field+76 is not "nothing ever writes
it" (the implicit framing since Finding 113). Something does write
it, exactly once, early in boot, and in this run it legitimately
computes to 0 through a real, traced fallback chain ending in a
string-to-int parse of what looks like an empty/null/not-yet-set
config string. The practical next question is no longer "who writes
field+76" (answered) but "what should have populated the config
string at ~0x82448BA0 before sub_82451630 ran, and why didn't it" --
plausibly related to the real file I/O failures already visible in
this project's own boot log (`NtOpenFile: "cache:\" -> status=
0xC000000E`, `NtCreateFile: "d:\_T_E_S_T.___" -> status=0xC0000034`),
though that connection is a hypothesis, not yet verified.

No source or binary changes in this finding; no diagnostic main.cpp
edits were left uncommitted (confirmed via `git diff --stat
host/main.cpp` showing no changes).

Finding 117: Finding 116 correction -- the string-parse path was a
real tracing error (wrong base address, then a wrong call-argument
assumption on top of it). Corrected live tracing shows field+76
simply mirrors a single global, which reads 0 in our loaded image
and may be the same class of bug this project has already fixed once
before (Phase 2S/2T: raw XEX .data section genuinely zero at a slot
real console firmware would have populated).

User asked to verify Finding 116's "live read gave an implausible
value, 0x396B0040" discrepancy before trusting it further -- correct
call. The live read WAS wrong: Finding 116 computed the `lis
r11,-32143` base as 0x82450000 by mentally reusing a different
finding's base (sub_82451408's own `lis r11,-32145` -> 0x826F0000,
for the OBJECT pointer). Running the actual arithmetic
(-32143*65536) gives 0x82710000, not 0x82450000 -- a 0x2C0000
difference. Every address Finding 116 derived from that base was
therefore wrong: the "fallback global" is really at 0x8270F784 (not
0x8244F784), and the "config string pointer slot" is really at
0x82708BA0 (not 0x82448BA0). Re-reading live with the corrected
addresses: the fallback global at 0x8270F784 reads 0 (not the
0xD0030020 Finding 116 reported -- that was a real value, just read
from the wrong address). The string-pointer slot at 0x82708BA0 reads
0xA01BEF50 -- a real, well-formed guest heap address (matches the
0xA0xxxxxx allocation range seen throughout this run's own boot log),
not the implausible garbage Finding 116 flagged. So the specific
"garbage read" that triggered this re-check is fully explained: wrong
address, not corrupted/uninitialized memory.

Fixing the address also exposed a second, independent error in
Finding 116's control-flow trace. Re-reading sub_82451630's source
around the write (ppc_recomp.58.cpp:5660-5667) shows the `lwz
r3,-29792(r27)` line Finding 116 treated as "the string argument fed
into the bl sub_823ED898 call" actually executes AFTER that call
returns (it's set up for a later, unrelated instruction) -- the
call's actual r3 argument is whatever sub_8244E8C0 returned just
before the branch into this block, not that global-string read at
all. Live-confirmed by setting a breakpoint directly on
`__imp__sub_823ED898`: it never fires during a full run. The
"string-parse chain" Finding 116 followed (sub_823ED898 ->
sub_823EEEF8 -> sub_823EEC00, a real strtol-shaped parser --
that part of the reading was correct) is real code that exists, but
it is not the path this run actually takes to reach the field+76
write.

The path actually taken (confirmed by sub_823ED898 never being hit,
and by context around ppc_recomp.58.cpp:5621-5631's `cmplwi
cr6,r3,0` / `beq cr6,0x82451b18`): when r3 is already 0 at that
check, execution jumps straight to loc_82451B18, which loads r11
directly from the SAME global fallback (`lis r11,-32143` /
`lwz r11,-2196(r11)` -- note: -2196, a DIFFERENT nearby offset from
the one Finding 116 misread as -2172; both exist in this function for
different fields, this one is the real source for THIS field) and
falls straight through to the write at loc_82451B20. No string
parse, no sub_823ED898 call, no sub_8244E8C0 lookup on this
particular path -- field+76 is just a direct copy of that global.
Live data is fully consistent with this: the global reads 0, field+76
reads 0 after the one real call completes (Finding 116's own
step-out measurement, which remains valid -- only the explanation of
*why* changes here).

Checked who writes that corrected global (0x8270F784):
`ppc_xref.py --find-writes 0x8270F784 --hops 4` again found zero
writers -- the same recurring blind spot. This time there's a
plausible non-bug explanation sitting right in this project's own
codebase: host/main.cpp:89-96 already documents and fixes an
identical-shaped problem for a different address (0x82670100, the
kernel tick-struct pointer) with the comment "Real Xbox 360
kernel/HAL boot code (outside any title's own executable) populates
this slot... This project never emulates that boot sequence, and the
raw XEX's .data section genuinely contains zero here (confirmed,
Phase 2S) -- so the host writes it directly." If 0x8270F784 is the
same category -- a .data slot the real console's dashboard/firmware
or an earlier boot stage would set before this title's own code runs,
which this project doesn't emulate -- then `ppc_xref.py` finding no
writer is the CORRECT, expected static result (there is no writer in
the recompiled code to find), not another instance of the tool's
known blind spot. Not yet confirmed: what the real value should be.
Phase 2S/2T's fix used Xenia as ground truth for its slot; the same
approach (check Xenia source, or UnleashedRecomp, for whatever this
address or its surrounding structure represents) is the natural next
step before writing any value here, since writing the wrong value
would just trade one wrong default for another.

No source or binary changes in this finding; no diagnostic main.cpp
edits were left uncommitted (confirmed via `git diff --stat
host/main.cpp` showing no changes).

Finding 118: confirmed via a live decisive test that field+76 is a
real, load-bearing permanent blocker, not a benign zero default --
patching it unblocks genuinely new forward progress (real package/
save-data loading never reached before in this entire investigation)
before crashing at a new, later wall.

First, checked whether 0x8270F784 (Finding 117's corrected fallback-
global address) reads 0 because of a bug in this project's own XEX
section-copying logic (host/main.cpp:57-77) or because the retail
binary's own .data section genuinely contains 0 there. Added a
temporary diagnostic read using XenonUtils' own `Image::Find`
(bypasses this project's loader entirely, reads straight from the
parsed XEX) right after section copying in SetupMemoryImage. Result:
0x00000000, straight from the file. Not a loader bug -- this really
is baked into the retail .data section as zero. This matches the
exact shape of a precedent already fixed once in this same file
(host/main.cpp:89-96, the kernel tick-struct pointer): "real Xbox 360
kernel/HAL boot code... populates this slot... the raw XEX's .data
section genuinely contains zero here... so the host writes it
directly."

Given that precedent, ran the decisive test: temporarily patched
0x8270F784 = 1 at the same point in SetupMemoryImage (before
`_xstart` runs), temporarily extended the watchdog to 45s to give
room to observe further, rebuilt, and ran. Result, reproduced twice
identically: no watchdog timeout. Instead, the process now visibly
proceeds through dozens of real `NtCreateFile`/`NtReadFile` calls
against actual package files (`d:\packages\xbox360online_fetm.xen`,
`w_saveload_fetm.xen`, etc.) -- real asset/save-data package loading
activity this investigation has never once observed in ~18 prior
findings, all the way back to Finding 15. Then it crashes
deterministically (same line count, same ~5 second wall time, both
runs) with EXC_BAD_ACCESS / SIGBUS (exit code 138) at a bad guest
pointer dereference.

Backtrace (via lldb `bt` on the crash):
  frame #0: __imp__sub_8256D010 + 412 (`ldr x8, [x8, x9]` -- the
             faulting load, address 0x6f7e600000, well below the
             0x7000000000 guest-memory base, i.e. a garbage/
             underflowed guest pointer, not a valid guest address)
  frame #1: __imp__sub_82453200 + 460
  frame #2: __imp__sub_8244B040 + 5116 (the same per-iteration
             function from Findings 106/114/115/116)
  frame #3: __imp__sub_8212B130 + 204 (already in this
             investigation's known call-chain whitelist, Finding 115)
  frame #4: sub_825DEC18 (same whitelist, Finding 115)
  frame #5: __imp___xstart + 1588
This confirms field+76 is a real gate on genuinely new code, not an
inert default: with it unblocked, execution reaches a function
(sub_8256D010) this investigation has never touched before, and that
function dereferences a bad pointer -- almost certainly because
whatever structure it expects (plausibly populated by the package-
loading machinery just now reached for the first time) isn't fully
set up by this project's current implementation. This is a new,
separate, downstream problem, not evidence against the field+76 fix.

All temporary changes (the `Image::Find` diagnostic, the 0x8270F784
patch, the 45s watchdog extension) were reverted immediately after
the test; `git diff --stat host/main.cpp` confirmed clean before this
finding was written, and the binary was rebuilt from the clean tree
afterward.

Net result: field+76 is THE real blocker this entire phase-3
investigation has been chasing since Finding 106. Fixing it (by
whatever the right permanent mechanism turns out to be -- a host
boot-time patch matching the kernel-tick-struct precedent, or
properly implementing whatever config/profile subsystem should set
it) is very likely to be necessary and is now proven sufficient to
produce real further progress, not just a different kind of stall.
The next wall is sub_8256D010's bad pointer dereference, reached via
sub_82453200 <- sub_8244B040 <- sub_8212B130 <- sub_825DEC18 <-
__imp___xstart, during real package-file loading -- not yet
investigated.

No source or binary changes in this finding beyond the reverted
temporary diagnostics; confirmed via `git diff --stat host/main.cpp`
showing no changes.

Finding 119: sub_8256D010's crash fully explained -- a real,
in-source empty-list fallback branch sets up a null pointer, which
then feeds a null function-pointer call (`bctrl` to guest address 0),
not a codegen or memory-corruption bug.

Reapplied Finding 118's temporary 0x8270F784=1 patch to reproduce,
then worked the crash two ways in parallel: read the PPC source
around the fault (ppc_recomp.81.cpp:4525-4594) and, since this is a
Release build with no debug info (`CMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release`, `-O3
-DNDEBUG`, confirmed via build/CMakeCache.txt -- source-level `expr`
evaluation on `ctx` is not available), cross-checked by disassembling
the actual compiled function (`lldb ... disassemble --name
__imp__sub_8256D010`) and reading raw registers at the fault
(`register read x8 x9 x19 x20`).

First false lead, caught and ruled out: the faulting `ldr x8, [x8,
x9]` used x9 = 0xFFFFFFFF7E600000, which looked like a sign-extension
bug (a 32-bit guest address, zero-extension expected, landing as a
huge negative 64-bit value) given the fault address
(0x6f7e600000) sits below the 0x7000000000 guest-memory base.
Ruled out by computing what that constant actually is:
PPC_IMAGE_BASE + PPC_IMAGE_SIZE - PPC_CODE_BASE*2 (0x82000000 +
0x740000 - 0x820A0000*2) equals exactly 0xFFFFFFFF7E600000 -- this is
the expected, correctly-folded constant half of this project's own
`PPC_LOOKUP_FUNC`/`PPC_CALL_INDIRECT_FUNC` macros (ppc_context.h:110-
113), the mechanism `bctrl`/`PPC_CALL_INDIRECT_FUNC(ctx.ctr.u32)`
always compiles to. Nothing wrong here -- nearly re-reported a
tooling/codegen bug that wasn't real.

Reversing the macro's arithmetic against the actual fault address
(`final = base + PPC_IMAGE_BASE + PPC_IMAGE_SIZE + (y -
PPC_CODE_BASE)*2`, solved for y) gives `ctx.ctr.u32 == 0` at the
crash. The game attempted to call guest function address 0 -- a null
function pointer, not a stray/garbage one. This is consistent with
everything upstream in the source: `ctx.ctr` was loaded two
instructions earlier via `lwz r11,160(r10)` / `mtctr r11`
(ppc_recomp.81.cpp:4588-4590), where r10 itself came from `lwz
r10,0(r3)` (line 4582). Reading backward from there, r3 is set by a
real two-way branch a few lines up (4560-4578): `lwz r11,6784(r31)`
loads a sub-object pointer from the function's own object parameter,
`lwz r10,224(r11)` reads what looks like a count/length field from
that sub-object, and `cmpwi cr6,r10,0` / `ble cr6,->loc_8256D068`
takes a "list is empty" fallback (`li r3,0`) whenever that count is
<= 0; the other branch indexes into a real array at offset
`(count+56)*4` from the sub-object instead. In this run, the
fallback branch is the one taken -- count is <= 0 -- producing r3=0,
then r10=*(0+0) and r11=*(r10+160), both landing in guest address 0's
neighborhood (unmapped/zero-filled in this project's memory image),
giving ctr=0 and the null-pointer call.

Net: this is not a recompiler, sign-extension, or memory-safety bug.
It's a real empty-list/registry condition in the game's own logic --
some sub-object reachable via the object's own field+6784 has a
count field (at that sub-object's own offset+224) that's <= 0 when
the real retail game would apparently have at least one entry
registered there by this point (plausibly something that should get
populated by, or in response to, the package-loading activity this
run was in the middle of when field+76 was unblocked -- e.g. a
content/package registration callback this project's package/
content-loading path doesn't yet implement). What object this is,
what's supposed to populate that count, and whether it's genuinely
missing functionality or a timing/ordering issue (checked before the
real registration happens) are not yet determined -- reasonable next
step, not done this finding.

All temporary changes (the reapplied 0x8270F784=1 patch) were
reverted immediately after this investigation; `git diff --stat
host/main.cpp` confirmed clean and the binary was rebuilt from the
clean tree before this finding was written.

Finding 120: field+76's fix made permanent -- host/main.cpp now
patches 0x8270F784=1 at boot, matching the kernel-tick-struct
precedent. Not a researched-correct value, a placeholder proven
necessary and sufficient to unblock real progress.

Added a permanent patch in SetupMemoryImage (host/main.cpp, right
before the existing GPU-register preseeding block), documented
inline with the same level of detail as the kernel tick struct it
sits next to: what field+76 gates (sub_82451408's loading-complete
path), who sets it from this global (sub_82451630, Findings 116-117),
confirmation the raw XEX .data section is genuinely zero here
(Finding 118, so this is not a loader bug being papered over), and an
explicit caveat that `1` is a placeholder chosen because it's the
minimum value satisfying the observed `<= 0` gate and matches the
sibling field+72 (naturally 1 in this image) -- not because the real
value is known.

Rebuilt and reran to confirm the permanent version behaves
identically to Finding 118's temporary test: exit code 138 (SIGBUS),
same real package-loading activity (`NtCreateFile`/`NtReadFile`
against `w_saveload_fetm.xen` etc.), same crash point
(sub_8256D010, Finding 119's already-understood null function-
pointer call). This was expected and is not a regression -- Finding
119's empty-list issue is a separate, already-documented problem one
step further down the same path, not caused by this fix. The
standard 10-second watchdog was left untouched throughout (confirmed
via `grep -n "wait_for(std::chrono::seconds" host/main.cpp` showing
both call sites still at 10) since the crash now happens at ~5
seconds, well before the watchdog would ever fire -- this patch
changes the failure mode from "silent hang, watchdog timeout" to
"fast, deterministic, debuggable crash," which is strictly better for
continued investigation regardless of what the real value turns out
to be.

This is a real, committed source change (host/main.cpp), not a
reverted diagnostic -- `git diff host/main.cpp` before committing
showed exactly the one intended addition, nothing else.

Finding 121: Finding 119's empty-list root cause narrowed further --
the "sub-object pointer" at the crashing object's field+6784 isn't a
sub-object at all, it's a deliberately null manager/owner reference,
zero-initialized by this exact class's own real constructor
(sub_8256D250) alongside a 16-byte header group. Static search for
who else ever writes it finds no sibling-class candidate; the real
setter, if one exists at runtime, isn't a plain immediate-offset
store.

Rather than continue reasoning about ARM64 register allocation by
hand (the approach that produced a near-miss false lead last finding
-- the PPC_LOOKUP_FUNC constant), used the more reliable technique:
a temporary diagnostic `printf` inserted directly into the generated
source (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.81.cpp, right after the field+6784/
+224 reads in __imp__sub_8256D010), following this project's own
established "temporary edit, verify, revert before commit" discipline
-- previously only ever applied to host/main.cpp, extended here to a
private/ppc/*.cpp file for the first time, with the same discipline
(reverted immediately, `git diff --stat` confirmed clean, rebuilt
from the clean tree before writing this finding).

Live output: `object(r31)=0xB5074FC0 subobj(r31+6784)=0x00000000
count(subobj+224)=0`. The "sub-object pointer" is null -- not a
sub-object with a zero count, an actually-null reference. `lwz
r10,224(r11)` with r11=0 just happens to read near guest address 0
(unmapped/zero-filled in this project's image), coincidentally
producing the same "count<=0" symptom Finding 119 already identified,
but the real fact worth separating out is that field+6784 itself was
never set to point at anything.

Reran the same offset-sweep technique from Finding 116 (grep raw asm
comments for any store to this literal offset): `// stw
[^,]*,6784\(` across all of private/ppc/*.cpp -- only 11 hits
project-wide (a tractable, offset-6784-is-fairly-distinctive result,
unlike offset 76's 616). One of them, sub_8256D250
(ppc_recomp.81.cpp:4955, only 0x240 bytes from the crashing
sub_8256D010 -- almost certainly a sibling virtual method of the
exact same class), is this class's real constructor: it sets the
vtable pointer (`stw r11,0(r31)` with r11 built from `lis
r11,-32248`/`addi r11,r11,-22992`, resolving to guest address
0x8207A630) and, in the same few instructions, zeroes field+6784
alongside 6788/6792/6796 as one deliberate 16-byte group -- field+6784
being null is this class's own intended default state at construction
time, not an uninitialized-memory accident.

Checked the other 10 writers for the same vtable-construction
pattern (`lis r11,-32248` combined with the exact `-22992` addend) to
see if any belongs to this same class and sets a real value later --
none do. Every other offset-6784 writer sits in an unrelated function
using different neighboring field offsets (6768, 6772, 6780 instead
of this class's own 6788/6792/6796/6800), consistent with 6784 being
a coincidentally-reused generic struct offset across several
unrelated large classes in the full retail binary, the same pattern
already seen for offset 76 (Finding 116). So for this specific class,
static search for a plain-immediate-offset store finds no setter at
all besides the constructor's own zero-init.

This doesn't mean no setter exists -- it means if one exists, it's
not a simple `stw rX,6784(rY)` the grep-based sweep can find (e.g. a
computed/indexed store, or a bulk struct copy/memcpy of the whole
object). Determining whether field+6784 is genuinely meant to stay
null in this run's conditions (making the game's own null-deref-on-
empty-manager path a latent bug real hardware would also hit, just
one this project's boot sequence reaches differently) or whether some
missing host-side package/content-loading callback is supposed to
attach a real manager here, would need either a live hardware
watchpoint on this specific object instance's field+6784 across a
longer run (to see if anything EVER writes it, the same technique
Finding 113 used successfully for field+76), or broader investigation
of the package-loading subsystem this run was in the middle of
exercising when the crash occurred. Neither done this finding --
a reasonable next step, not yet started.

All temporary changes (the printf diagnostic and its `#include
<cstdio>` in ppc_recomp.81.cpp) were reverted immediately after this
investigation; `git diff --stat host/main.cpp private/ppc/
ppc_recomp.81.cpp` confirmed both clean, and the binary was rebuilt
from the clean tree before this finding was written.

Finding 122: live hardware watchpoint across the whole construction-
to-crash window confirms, decisively, that nothing ever writes
field+6784 of this object -- the constructor's own zero-init is the
only write that ever happens. This is a genuinely missing setter/
callback, not a timing race or a non-grep-matchable computed store.

Found a clean, zero-source-modification way to read a PPC register at
a function's very first instruction, reusable for future findings:
PPCContext's first member is `r3` (ppc_context.h:310), so at a
breakpoint on a function's entry address (before any instruction
executes), the raw ARM64 calling-convention register `x0` still holds
the unmodified `&ctx` argument, and reading 4 bytes there
(`memory read --size 4 --format x $x0`) gives `ctx.r3.u32` directly --
no DWARF, no disassembly-level register tracing required. Verified
against Finding 121's printf-captured value (0xB5074FC0): exact
match, and confirms heap allocation is deterministic run-to-run in
this harness (no ASLR-equivalent), which is what makes computing a
watch address from one run and using it as a fixed literal in a
second live session valid here.

Broke on sub_8256D250 (the real constructor, Finding 121), read the
object address this way (0xB5074FC0, matching Finding 121 exactly),
computed the host watch address for its field+6784
(0x7000000000 + 0xB5074FC0 + 6784 = 0x70B5076A40), and set a 4-byte
write watchpoint there immediately, before a single further
instruction of the constructor itself executes -- covering every
write to this field from the very first instant the object exists.
Issued five `continue`s, which ran the process all the way through
to Finding 118/119's known crash and then (since lldb's default
crash-stop doesn't auto-resume) re-reported the same stopped
EXC_BAD_ACCESS state four more times harmlessly.

Result: the watchpoint fired exactly once, logged as `new value: 0`
-- the constructor's own zero-init (Finding 121), nothing else.
Hardware watchpoints trigger on every store to the watched address
regardless of the value written or how the store instruction was
generated (immediate offset, computed index, bulk copy -- all of
them), so this is a strictly stronger result than the static grep
sweep from Finding 121: not "no plain-immediate-offset store found,"
but "no store of any kind happens," full stop, for this entire
window.

Net: field+6784 staying null through to the crash is not a timing
race this project's boot ordering lost, and not a store pattern
Finding 121's grep sweep missed. Something that is supposed to call
a setter on this exact object -- attaching whatever manager/owner
reference belongs there -- never runs at all in this project's
current implementation, in the time between this object's
construction and the first attempt to use that reference. The two
live candidates for what that missing call chain might be: (1) some
callback tied to the package-loading activity visible in the boot
log right up until the crash (`s_generic.xen`, `w_saveload_fetm.xen`,
etc. -- plausible, since this is the exact subsystem Finding 118's
field+76 fix newly unblocked access to), or (2) a host-kernel
primitive this project stubs or gets wrong, in the vein of Finding
115's kernel-surface diff, specific to whatever subsystem owns this
manager type. Neither investigated yet.

No source or binary changes in this finding; `git diff --stat
host/main.cpp private/ppc/ppc_recomp.81.cpp` confirmed clean
throughout, and no diagnostic edits were made to any source file this
time -- the entire investigation used only live lldb commands against
the unmodified, already-clean binary.

Finding 123: identified the real missing method -- sub_8256B7D0, this
class's own vtable slot+84, is the "attach manager" setter Finding
121/122 inferred must exist. Confirmed live it never executes at all
during a full run -- hit count 0, not even reaching its own internal
guard check. Also corrects a wrong dismissal in Finding 121.

Rather than keep reasoning about what SHOULD set field+6784, read the
class's actual vtable data directly: broke at `__imp___xstart` (after
SetupMemoryImage has mapped the image, unlike an earlier attempt at
`main` which failed with "memory read failed" -- the guest image
isn't loaded yet that early) and dumped 48 words (192 bytes) at the
vtable address Finding 121 already computed (0x8207A630, guest,
`lis r11,-32248`/`addi r11,r11,-22992`), byte-swapping each (host is
little-endian, guest data is big-endian) to recover the real guest
function address at each slot.

This turned up a correction to Finding 121's own analysis: slot+48
(0x30) resolves to 0x8256D010 -- the crashing function itself,
confirming this is genuinely this object's own vtable -- and slot+84
(0x54) resolves to 0x8256B7D0, the exact function Finding 121 found
independently via the field+6784 offset-sweep but DISMISSED as an
unrelated class, because its surrounding source text didn't show the
same `lis r11,-32248`/`addi r11,r11,-22992` vtable-construction
sequence nearby (that check only catches a CONSTRUCTOR setting its
OWN vtable pointer; a virtual method doesn't re-set the vtable
pointer at all, so the absence of that pattern proves nothing about
class membership -- a real gap in that check, not caught at the
time).

Re-read sub_8256B7D0 (ppc_recomp.81.cpp:756-825+) with this
confirmed: it's genuinely a two-parameter virtual method (`this`,
flag). Guarded by `cmpwi cr6,r30,1` / `bne cr6,->loc_8256B90C`
(r30 = the second parameter) -- only when called with flag==1 does it
walk `*(this+264)` -> deref -> `+208` -> `+32` to resolve a real
manager/owner reference, store it into field+6784 (line 805-806,
confirming this is the exact field+6784 write Finding 121 already
found and attributed to "an unrelated function"), set field+6788 from
a related field, call sub_825789F8 (unexamined -- some setup/
registration step), and then re-check the newly-attached manager's
own field+224 (the SAME count check sub_8256D010 performs) -- strong
structural evidence this genuinely is the method responsible for
making sub_8256D010's later access safe.

Live-confirmed via a breakpoint on `__imp__sub_8256B7D0` across a
full run to the crash (and past it, via repeated `continue`): hit
count 0. Not "called with flag=0 so it skips the write" -- never
entered at all, at any point, for any object. Combined with Finding
122's watchpoint result (field+6784 never written past construction),
this is now airtight: the method that's supposed to run never runs,
full stop.

Attempted to find who's supposed to call vtable slot+84 (virtual
dispatch, so no direct `bl sub_8256B7D0` exists anywhere --
confirmed, grep for direct callers found only the function-mapping
table entry). Tried the obvious static approach: scan every
`// lwz rX,0(rY)` (vtable-pointer fetch) immediately followed within
a few lines by `// lwz rX,84(rX)` (slot+84 fetch from that same
register) across all of private/ppc/*.cpp. Found 53 candidate sites,
but offset 84 is itself a common, small struct offset reused by many
unrelated classes' own vtables (the same lesson as offsets 76 and
6784 in earlier findings, just worse here since 84 is a much more
common offset than either) -- distinguishing which of the 53, if any,
actually dispatch on an instance of THIS specific class would require
tracing each site's object provenance individually. Not done this
finding; stopped here rather than grinding through 53 low-confidence
candidates by hand.

Next step, not started: either trace a subset of the 53 candidates
(prioritizing ones in files already linked to this investigation's
known call chain, e.g. ppc_recomp.57.cpp:6698/9306 and
ppc_recomp.72.cpp, which also appeared in Finding 115/121's own
whitelist-adjacent searches), or pivot to this report's second
hypothesis (a missing/wrong host-kernel primitive specific to
whatever subsystem is supposed to trigger this attachment, in the
vein of Finding 115's kernel-surface diff).

No source or binary changes in this finding; confirmed via `git diff
--stat host/main.cpp private/ppc/ppc_recomp.81.cpp` showing no
changes -- this investigation used only live lldb commands and static
analysis against the unmodified, already-clean binary.

Finding 124: traced the real construction call chain live (rather
than the 53 static slot+84 candidates, which stayed too ambiguous to
prioritize) -- it connects straight back to Finding 106's original
stuck loop. Did not find vtable slot+84's caller yet; did find and
correct one of this session's own dead ends, and found a new,
reusable binary-search dispatch pattern worth keeping.

Live-confirmed construction chain (via breakpoint + `bt` on
`__imp__sub_8256D250`, cross-checked against each frame's actual
disassembly rather than trusting symbol names alone -- see the
dead-end below for why that caution paid off):
`sub_8244AFB0` (Finding 106's own stuck loop) -> `sub_8244AE70` ->
`sub_82461D28` -> `sub_825E8D28` -> `sub_825E8A60` ->
`sub_824532F0` -> `sub_8244A458` -> `sub_825DDB48` (the factory,
confirmed via Finding 121) -> `sub_8256D250` (the constructor). This
object's entire construction happens inside the exact loop this whole
phase-3 investigation started from -- a real, concrete link between
the very first finding of this session's larger effort and this
thread's own crash chain, not previously established.

Caught and corrected a dead end from earlier this finding: set a
breakpoint by name on `__imp__sub_825DDB48` to confirm the factory
runs, and it showed hit count 0 even while the constructor it's
supposed to call (per source) clearly did run. Rather than conclude
something impossible (a function's body executing without its entry
running), disassembled the actual calling frame directly
(`frame select 1` / `disassemble --frame`) and found real, matching
logic (size=7056, call sub_8212D2E0, conditionally call
sub_8256D250) at a DIFFERENT address than the one
`__imp__sub_825DDB48` resolved to -- a symbol-table quirk (this build
has no debug info, Finding 119, and apparently an inexact or
colliding entry for this one internal symbol), not a real behavioral
contradiction. The source-level logic match is what actually settles
it; the breakpoint-by-name result for this one symbol was simply
unreliable. Worth remembering: when a breakpoint-by-name result
contradicts other live evidence, check the disassembly at the
suspect address before trusting either signal.

This also retroactively confirms Finding 123's own live memory-search
finding (the `{factory_ptr, type_tag}` table at guest 0x8209D1D8)
was a real but UNRELATED table -- a genuine dead end, not a tooling
error. A read watchpoint on that table's first entry never fired
across the whole run (already noted in Finding 123), and this
finding's own disassembly of the real construction chain shows the
actual dispatch mechanism is completely different: `sub_8244A458`
(private/ppc/ppc_recomp.57.cpp:22-110) is a binary search, not a
linear table scan -- sorted keys at `table_base+4` (stride 8), parallel
function-pointer values at `table_base+8` (stride 8), entry count at
`table_base+29816`, table_base = `lis r11,-32143` / `addi
r7,r11,-29776`. On a key match it does a genuine tail call
(`bctr`, not `bctrl` -- no return address pushed) straight into the
resolved handler. This is a distinct, reusable pattern from the
linear table Finding 123 found; worth keeping both in mind for future
tracing in this codebase, now that it's confirmed more than one
dispatch convention is in use.

Followed the chain one step past construction to see if the attach
call might sit immediately next to it: `sub_824532F0` (the chain's
frame #2, immediately above the factory/constructor), after getting
the constructed object back in r31, checks a specific bit
(`0x20000000`) of field+104 and -- depending on that bit -- calls
EITHER vtable slot+0 (not slot+84) with flag=1, OR a different
function (`sub_8244E458`) entirely. Neither is the slot+84 attach
call Finding 123 identified. So the attach trigger is not immediately
adjacent to construction in this specific call chain; it's either
inside `sub_8244E458` (not yet examined) or triggered by something
unrelated to this construction event altogether (e.g. a later,
separate callback tied to package-load completion, which this run's
fatal crash may prevent from ever being reached).

Ran that exact widened test before closing out this finding: a read
watchpoint on the vtable slot itself (guest 0x8207A630+84 =
0x8207A684 -- the shared slot, not any one instance's own field+6784)
across a full run to the crash. It never fired either. This is
strictly stronger than Finding 123's direct breakpoint on
`sub_8256B7D0`: it proves no object of this class, not just this one
instance, ever has its slot+84 method dispatched anywhere in this
entire run. Combined with Finding 123, this is now airtight at the
class level, not just the instance level.

Net: slot+84's real caller is still not found, but its absence is
now confirmed as thoroughly as this investigation's techniques allow.
This finding's value is the corrected, live-confirmed call chain
(tying this whole crash investigation back to Finding 106), the
caught dead end (saves a future session from re-trusting the
0x8209D1D8 table), the new binary-search-dispatch pattern, and the
class-wide (not just instance-wide) confirmation that slot+84 never
fires. The one reasonable next step left, not started: read
`sub_8244E458` (the other branch of `sub_824532F0`'s post-
construction dispatch) -- or pivot to this investigation's second
standing hypothesis (a missing/wrong host-kernel primitive specific
to whatever subsystem should be triggering this attachment, Finding
115's style).

No source or binary changes in this finding; confirmed via `git diff
--stat host/main.cpp private/ppc/ppc_recomp.81.cpp` showing no
changes -- this investigation used only live lldb commands and static
analysis against the unmodified, already-clean binary.

Finding 125: the field+6784/vtable-slot+84 mystery (Findings 121-124)
is resolved -- not a missing feature, a pure timing consequence of
Finding 119's crash. There is no broken attachment subsystem. The
game's own normal queue-and-drain mechanism would attach the manager
completely correctly on the NEXT tick; the process never survives
to a next tick because Finding 119's crash happens first, later in
the exact same tick.

Started from `sub_8244E458`, the function Finding 124 identified as
the real second half of `sub_824532F0`'s post-construction dispatch
(the one that didn't call slot+84 directly). Reading it fully (past
where Finding 124 stopped) revealed a deferred-work QUEUE, not a
dead end: a fixed 16-slot array (capacity check `cmpwi cr6,r11,16` /
`beqlr cr6` -- silently drops the item if full, a separate, real
latent bug not pursued further here), 28-byte stride, a shared
counter at offset+468. Confirmed live this is a SINGLE GLOBAL
SINGLETON, not a per-caller queue: broke on `__imp__sub_8244E458`,
read its actual `r3` (queue owner) parameter via the `$x0`-offset-0
trick (Finding 122), and compared against the global pointer slot at
`lis r11,-32143` / `lwz r10,-29804(r11)` (guest 0x82708B94) --
exact match (0xB91621F0), live-confirmed, not assumed.

Found a sibling enqueue function at the same address neighborhood,
`sub_8244E410` (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.57.cpp:10565-10603, literally
the function immediately before `sub_8244E458` in the same file),
which enqueues into the identical global queue with its own type tag
(2) -- confirming this is a shared, multi-producer event queue used
by more than one subsystem, not something built just for our class.

Set a READ watchpoint on the queue's shared counter field (guest
0xB91621F0+468, host 0x70B91623C4) spanning the whole run from
`__imp___xstart` through the crash, rather than continuing to guess
at which function drains it. Three distinct call sites hit it across
several `continue`s: two were `sub_8244E458` re-reading its own
just-written counter (expected, matches its own source), and one was
genuinely new -- `sub_8245BAC0`, called directly from `sub_8244B040`
itself (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.57.cpp), not nested inside the
construction chain at all.

Read `sub_8245BAC0` (private/ppc/ppc_recomp.59.cpp:9439 onward): a
real drain loop. Checks the counter, early-returns if zero
(`ble cr6,->loc_8245BCEC`), otherwise walks live entries and
dispatches each by a small type tag (0-6) through a jump table
(`lis r12,-32186`/`addi r12,r12,-17564`, indexed `*4`), each case a
distinct handler. Case 0 (`loc_8245BBAC`) does call a virtual method
on the stored object -- vtable slot+4, not slot+84 directly -- so
this doesn't resolve to a byte-exact match with `sub_8256B7D0`
without reconciling an apparent offset discrepancy between what
`sub_8244E458` writes (fields at +20/+28/+40 within its own locally-
computed entry base) and what `sub_8245BAC0` reads (fields at +0/+8/
+20 within ITS OWN differently-based local pointer) -- not resolved
byte-for-byte this finding, and not needed to be: the exact case
number stops mattering once the real explanation (below) is in hand.

The decisive check: where does `sub_8244B040` call these two
functions relative to each other? `grep -n "sub_8245BAC0\|
sub_8244AFB0" private/ppc/ppc_recomp.57.cpp` (excluding definitions)
shows `sub_8245BAC0` called three times (lines 2730, 2738, 2746),
ALL before the single call to `sub_8244AFB0` (line 2758) -- the exact
function whose deep call tree does both our object's construction
(Finding 124's chain) and, later in the same tree, Finding 119's
crash. So within any one tick: drain runs first (operating on
whatever was queued by the END of the PREVIOUS tick), then
construction runs and enqueues our object for the queue to pick up
NEXT tick -- a perfectly ordinary, correct design. The object
simply hasn't had its turn yet when the process dies, later in this
exact same tick, from an entirely unrelated cause (Finding 119).

Net: Findings 121-124's hunt for "who's supposed to call vtable
slot+84" was looking for something that doesn't need fixing. The
real mechanism (a global deferred-work queue, drained once per tick
before new work is queued for the next one) is intact and would
handle this correctly on its own, given a second tick to run in.
Fixing Finding 119's null function-pointer crash (the real, sole
remaining blocker in this chain) should resolve field+6784 as a
side effect, for free, rather than needing any separate intervention
-- a strong, actionable, well-evidenced conclusion to pursue next
over continuing to chase this thread any further.

No source or binary changes in this finding; confirmed via `git diff
--stat host/main.cpp private/ppc/ppc_recomp.81.cpp` showing no
changes -- this investigation used only live lldb commands and static
analysis against the unmodified, already-clean binary.

Finding 126: fixed Finding 119's crash with two real, permanent,
broadly-scoped host/toolchain changes -- a guard on indirect guest
calls, and a compile flag preventing clang from trapping on genuine
guest idle loops. The game now runs a full real boot (package
loading, object construction, graceful internal shutdown decision)
and reaches the exact same watchdog-timeout exit this whole
investigation started from, instead of crashing.

Fix 1 -- guard `PPC_CALL_INDIRECT_FUNC` against invalid targets
(private/ppc/ppc_context.h): Finding 119's crash was a guest call
through a null function pointer (`ctx.ctr.u32 == 0`), which makes
`PPC_LOOKUP_FUNC`'s own address arithmetic
(`uint32_t(y) - PPC_CODE_BASE`) underflow and read far outside the
real function table -- faulting on the read itself, before any
null check on the result could run. This shape (fall back to a
not-yet-ready object, call through it anyway) is architecturally
guaranteed to recur throughout the rest of this title's code now
that real gameplay logic is reachable, not something to special-
case per crash site. Added `PPC_GuardedCallIndirect` -- checks the
target falls within `[PPC_CODE_BASE, PPC_CODE_BASE + PPC_IMAGE_SIZE)`
before doing the lookup at all, logs once (capped at 20 total
messages, to avoid log spam if this recurs often) and no-ops
otherwise, instead of letting the macro fault. Live-confirmed: same
build, same run, the guard fires exactly once at the exact Finding
119 site (`[guard] ... invalid guest address 0x0`), and execution
continues past it into real further package-loading activity never
observed in this investigation before.

Fix 2 -- `-fno-finite-loops` for the whole BigBumpinPPC target
(CMakeLists.txt): with Fix 1 alone, the process survived the
original crash but then hit a NEW stop (EXC_BREAKPOINT / SIGTRAP,
exit 133) at `sub_825DEC18 + 96` -- disassembly showed a bare
`brk #0x1`. Read the real PPC source (ppc_recomp.105.cpp:10616-10639):
`sub_825DEC18` calls `sub_8212B130` once and then falls into
`loc_825DEC34: // b 0x825dec34` -- an unconditional branch to itself,
a deliberate real-hardware idle-forever state (this function sits
directly under `__imp___xstart`; every single backtrace in Findings
118-125 shows `sub_8212B130` still on the stack, meaning under normal
operation it never returns here at all). A side-effect-free loop that
provably never terminates is UB per the C++ standard's forward-
progress rules, and clang's optimizer is permitted to -- and at -O3,
does -- replace it with a trap instead of actually looping. Correct
per the standard, wrong for recompiled guest code: this is a genuine
intentional idle state, not unreachable code. `-fno-finite-loops`
(confirmed supported by this clang) disables that specific
optimization for the whole PPC target, not just this one function,
since the same shape almost certainly recurs elsewhere in a title
this size. Live-confirmed: rebuilt, reran -- the process now stays
alive past the point that used to trap, doing real work (more
package loads, `MmFreePhysicalMemory`/`ExTerminateThread`/
`ObDeleteSymbolicLink` -- a genuine, orderly internal shutdown
sequence, not corruption), and exits with the SAME watchdog-timeout
message and exit code (2) that this entire Phase 3 investigation
started from back at Finding 106 -- except now preceded by a full,
real, previously-never-observed boot sequence instead of a silent,
unexplained hang.

Both changes are real, permanent, committed source changes, not
reverted diagnostics -- matching Finding 120's precedent (the
field+76 boot patch), not the usual "revert before commit" discipline
used throughout this investigation's many temporary probes.

Housekeeping note worth recording: `private/ppc/ppc_context.h` lives
inside this repo's git-ignored `private/` directory (the whole tree
is excluded, since it's ~110 files of XenonRecomp-decompiled game
code, regenerated from the retail XEX rather than authored here).
There is no tracked setup/generation script that would reproduce a
hand-edit to this one file if `private/` were ever wiped and
regenerated -- confirmed by searching for one. Since
`ppc_context.h` is not decompiled game logic but a small, hand-
adapted toolchain support header (it already differs from its
upstream submodule counterpart,
`thirdparty/XenonRecomp/XenonUtils/ppc_context.h`, e.g. the project's
own earlier `#include <cstdio>` addition), force-added it as a
tracked exception to the `private/` ignore rule (`git add -f`) so
this fix survives a regeneration. No other file under `private/` was
touched or force-added.

Remaining open question from this finding, not investigated: WHY the
game's own logic decides to shut down (ExTerminateThread et al.)
rather than continue running -- plausibly because, under today's two
placeholder/guard fixes (field+76's value, Finding 120; this guarded
null-call), there still isn't enough real, correctly-configured
content for it to find something to do, and it's reaching a genuine
"nothing to display, exit gracefully" decision in its own code. Not
yet traced to a specific check or confirmed as the real cause.

Finding 127: Finding 126's "graceful shutdown" read was wrong --
corrected live. The main thread isn't idling at all; it's in a NEW
genuine busy-wait (same shape as Finding 106's original stall),
spinning on a display/video-mode status bit that's set once and
never cleared. Strong, concrete lead: this looks tied to the
project's own hardcoded 1280x720 viewport vs. the game's own
1920x1080 check.

Re-examined Finding 126's claim with a longer live run. Temporarily
extended the watchdog to 90s (reverted after, standard discipline)
and timed a real run: `time ./build/BigBumpinHost` showed 88.98s
user time out of 90.20s wall time -- essentially 100% CPU the entire
run, not idling. The log output stops at the exact same
`ExTerminateThread`/`MmFreePhysicalMemory` sequence Finding 126 saw,
then produces ZERO further lines for the remaining ~85 seconds
despite the process staying fully busy -- a silent loop making no
further host-visible (logged) calls, not a clean exit.

Attached live and sampled thread #4's PC six times across 30 seconds
(5s apart): pinned at the exact same instruction every single time
(`__imp__sub_824A0B68 + 396`). A genuine tight busy-loop, confirmed,
not a transient or idling state -- correcting Finding 126's "reaches
idle, exits via watchdog" framing. (An earlier two-sample check during
the same investigation had caught it mid-transient and looked like it
was still moving; six samples 5s apart settled the question.)

Read the real source (ppc_recomp.65.cpp:23745-23821): straightforward
code, no mystery in the shape -- `if (field+92 of a global object at
guest 0x826E2110 != 0) { loc_824A0BC4: goto loc_824A0BC4; }` -- an
explicit, deliberate spin-wait for a status field to clear, written
by the original game as a real wait loop (this is exactly why
Finding 126's `-fno-finite-loops` fix was still correct and necessary
-- this loop, too, is a genuine guest wait state that clang would
otherwise trap on).

Static search for the writer (`ppc_xref.py --find-writes 0x826E2110
--offset 92 --hops 4`) found nothing, the same recurring blind spot.
Set a live WRITE watchpoint on the field instead (host
0x70826E216C) spanning the whole 90-second run. It fired exactly
twice: once at 0 (construction-time zero-init) and once at
`16777216` (0x01000000, a single high bit, not a plain boolean) --
call chain `sub_824A78E0 <- sub_824A84E0 <- sub_825F8BD0 <-
sub_8244B040 <- sub_8212B130 <- ...` (sub_825F8BD0 is already in
this investigation's own canonical-object whitelist from Finding
116). No third write ever happens in the full 90-second run --
nothing clears this bit, same unresolved-completion-signal shape as
Findings 113 and 121-125, just a new instance of it.

Read the setter (`sub_824A78E0`, ppc_recomp.66.cpp:5040 onward) to
understand what the bit represents, and it's a concrete, named thing,
not another generic flag: it reads this same global object's field+0
and field+4 and compares them against the literals 1920 and 1080
(lines 5086-5095, `cmpwi cr6,r11,1920` / `cmpwi cr6,r11,1080`) --
this object is a display/video-mode configuration structure, and this
function clamps/validates the requested resolution against (at
least) 1920x1080 before setting the pending-bit this investigation is
chasing. This project's own host/main.cpp hardcodes the Metal
renderer's viewport to 1280x720 ("Fixed at 1280x720 for this
milestone") and pre-seeds the GPU's AVIVO_D1MODE_VIEWPORT_SIZE
register to match. A game requesting/validating a 1920x1080-class
mode while the host's own GPU register state and renderer are fixed
at 1280x720 is a strong, concrete candidate for why nothing ever
signals "mode-set complete" back to this field -- plausible, not yet
proven: the exact real VD*-family kernel call or GPU register this
field is supposed to be cleared by has not been identified.

Reasonable next step, not started: trace what real display-mode-
change acknowledgment mechanism (likely a VdSetDisplayMode-adjacent
kernel call, or a specific GPU register this project's MMIO block
doesn't yet back) is supposed to clear this bit, and whether seeding
it correctly (matching this project's own existing 1280x720 GPU-
register-preseeding precedent in host/main.cpp) resolves this the
same low-risk way Finding 120 resolved field+76.

No source or binary changes in this finding; the temporary 90s
watchdog extension was reverted (`sed` back to `seconds(10)` both
call sites) and confirmed clean via `git diff --stat host/main.cpp`
before this finding was written.

Finding 128: Finding 127's "pending mode-change ack" framing for
field+92 doesn't hold up under a direct test -- disproved the quick
fix, and found the real check is a one-time trap, not a retry loop,
which is itself the reason the quick fix couldn't have worked. Real
semantics of the bit still not nailed down; this needs a careful
full read of sub_824A78E0, not another quick patch attempt.

Added temporary diagnostic logging to `VdSetDisplayMode` and
`VdQueryVideoMode` (both already real, implemented kernel functions,
not stubs -- VdSetDisplayMode no-ops to success, VdQueryVideoMode
returns success without writing any data, "no confirmed struct
layout"). Confirmed live: `VdSetDisplayMode` is called exactly once,
with real arguments; `VdQueryVideoMode` is called twice, and its
second call's r5 argument is `0x050002D0` -- the EXACT value
host/main.cpp pre-seeds into `AVIVO_D1MODE_VIEWPORT_SIZE` for this
project's own fixed 1280x720 viewport. This is evidence the game is
correctly seeing this project's real 1280x720 configuration flow
back through at least one real path, which weakens (does not
disprove, but weakens) the specific "1920x1080 mismatch" framing
Finding 127 proposed.

Tested the direct hypothesis two ways, both negative:
1. Cleared the field+92 bit inside `VdSetDisplayMode` itself (the
   natural point a real "mode change complete" signal would fire).
   No effect -- still hangs identically. Explanation found after:
   `VdSetDisplayMode` runs once, early, before the main tick loop
   even starts; the bit doesn't get SET until later (confirmed
   Finding 127's own call chain, `sub_824A78E0` inside the tick
   loop), so clearing it here is already stale by the time it matters.
2. Cleared the field+92 bit continuously, every 1ms, from the
   existing host-side tick thread (a blunt test of "does clearing it
   at all, by any mechanism, unblock progress" -- temporarily
   extended the watchdog to 30s to give room to observe). Also no
   effect -- confirmed live via repeated thread-PC sampling (same
   technique as Finding 127): still pinned at the exact same
   instruction. This is the more informative negative result: it
   rules out "the real fix is just clearing this bit, by whatever
   means" entirely.

Read the real PPC source for why #2 couldn't have worked
(ppc_recomp.65.cpp:23796-23805, already quoted in Finding 127): the
check is `lwz r11,92(r31); cmplwi cr6,r11,0; beq cr6,->proceed` --
executed EXACTLY ONCE -- followed, on the nonzero path, by
`loc_824A0BC4: goto loc_824A0BC4;`, an unconditional branch with no
re-read of memory at all. This is a one-shot decision into a true
dead-end trap, not a polling/retry loop. Once the guest branches into
it, no amount of host-side memory patching afterward can un-make
that decision -- explains both negative results at once, and is
itself a useful correction to how this bit's check works (Finding
127 implicitly assumed a retry-style wait).

Went back to pin down exactly what sets the bit, since Finding 127's
own source reading (ppc_recomp.66.cpp:5150-5157, `li r11,1; stw
r11,92(r31)`) turned out not to match the live value observed
(`16777216` / `0x01000000`, not `1`) -- a discrepancy not caught at
the time. Resolved it by disassembling the real compiled function
directly and locating the exact instruction at the watchpoint's own
reported offset (ppc_recomp.66's `__imp__sub_824A78E0`, host offset
+624/+628): `mov w9, #0x1000000; str w9, [x19, w11, uxtw]` -- a
literal, unconditional store of exactly the observed value, reached
via a different branch than the one Finding 127 happened to read.
This means Finding 127's identification of the SETTER function was
right (confirmed again, same `sub_824A78E0`), but the specific
branch/line within it, and so the real triggering condition, was
not. The earlier source read (lines 5150-5157) is a different,
later write to the same field within the same large function, not
the one that actually fired live.

Net: still don't know the real trigger or correct fix. What's ruled
out: a simple "clear this bit" host patch, by any timing. What's
confirmed: the check is a one-shot trap not a poll, `sub_824A78E0` is
still the right function to understand, and the project's own real
1280x720 config IS visible to the game through at least the
`VdQueryVideoMode` path tested here. Reasonable next step, not
started: read `sub_824A78E0` end-to-end against its live
disassembly (not source alone -- Finding 127's source-only read
already produced one wrong attribution) to find the actual branch
condition controlling the `mov w9,#0x1000000` store, since that
condition is what needs to evaluate differently for the bit to
never get set in the first place.

All temporary changes (diagnostic logging in both `VdSetDisplayMode`
and `VdQueryVideoMode`, the periodic-clear test in the host tick
