thread, the 30s watchdog extension) were reverted; `git diff --stat
host/main.cpp host/kernel_impl.cpp` confirmed both clean, and the
binary was rebuilt from the clean tree before this finding was
written.

Finding 129: did the full disasm/source reconciliation Finding 128
asked for, found a real, well-justified, concrete root cause
(`XGetVideoMode`'s empty output), implemented and tested a proper
fix for it -- and it still didn't unblock the hang. Live-confirmed
negative result, not abandoned mid-attempt; root cause for THIS
exact field+92 write is solid, but something else, not yet found,
also needs fixing, or this write isn't the real blocker after all.

Source-only reading kept producing wrong attributions (twice now,
Findings 127 and 128). This time, read the live disassembly
instruction-by-instruction from the function's real entry point
(`lldb disassemble --name __imp__sub_824A78E0 --count 170`, not a
small window around the known offset) and matched each ARM64
instruction forward against the PPC source by data flow, not by
line-number guessing. This resolved the earlier confusion
conclusively: the write firing the watchpoint is a `stwx`-shaped
indexed store (`PPC_STORE_U32(ctx.r31.u32 + 92, ...)` computed via a
separate address-add instruction), which a literal-offset grep for
`92(r31)` can never match -- a real, generalizable gap in that
search technique worth remembering for future findings.

Traced the controlling branch precisely: this instruction block is
reached whenever EITHER `XGetVideoMode`'s output `dwValid`-shaped
field (offset+16 relative to its output struct) reads false, OR
that struct's `dwDisplayHeight` (offset+4) reads below 720; a
separate `b.hs` branch skips the entire block (and so the write)
when height is >=720 and the query succeeded. `XGetVideoMode`
(host/kernel_impl.cpp) was already a real, host-implemented kernel
function -- not a stub -- but a no-op leaving its output buffer
zero-initialized, which is exactly "invalid, height=0": guaranteed
to take the problem branch every time, matching the real Xbox 360
`XVIDEO_MODE` struct layout (dwDisplayWidth@0, dwDisplayHeight@4,
fIsInterlaced@8, fIsWideScreen@12, fIsHiDef@16, RefreshRate@20 as a
float, VideoStandard@24 -- recognized from the real XDK API, not
guessed, and the field positions line up exactly with what the live
disassembly reads).

Implemented a real fix: wrote actual values into all six fields
matching this project's own fixed 1280x720 renderer configuration
(width=1280, height=720, not interlaced, widescreen=true,
fIsHiDef=true since 720p is HD, refresh=60.0f, NTSC). Rebuilt,
temporarily extended the watchdog to 30s, ran a full timed test:
still hangs identically (89s~ CPU-busy of 90s pattern repeated at
30s scale, same `MmFreePhysicalMemory`/`ExTerminateThread` tail, same
watchdog exit). Live-sampled thread #4's PC directly: still pinned
at the exact same instruction (`sub_824A0B68 + 396`) as every prior
finding in this sub-investigation. The fix had zero observable
effect on the actual hang, despite being correct and well-justified
on its own terms.

This means one of: (a) this specific write isn't actually what
causes `sub_824A0B68`'s busy-wait to trip (field+92 might get set to
a problem value via a completely different path even with this fix
in place -- not re-verified live after the fix, a gap in this
finding's own testing worth flagging), (b) there's a second,
independent cause also setting the bit or an equivalent blocking
condition, or (c) `sub_824A0B68`'s check isn't reading THIS field at
all and Finding 127's original identification of the blocking
condition itself needs re-verification from scratch, live, against
the post-fix binary, rather than assumed still valid.

Reverted the `XGetVideoMode` implementation along with the watchdog
extension, since it's a real improvement in isolation but doesn't
solve the problem it was written for, and committing an unproven fix
would misrepresent its status -- matches Finding 128's own
discipline of not keeping a change that didn't demonstrate the
effect it was built to test. `git diff --stat host/main.cpp
host/kernel_impl.cpp` confirmed both clean before this finding was
written, and the binary was rebuilt from the clean tree.

Reasonable next step, not started: before trying another fix, re-
verify live (fresh watchpoint + thread sample, same techniques used
throughout) which field `sub_824A0B68`'s actual check reads and
what value it holds at the moment of the stall, on the CURRENT
clean binary -- confirming the blocking condition directly, rather
than continuing to extend a chain of inferences (Finding 127's
original attribution, now three findings and one real fix attempt
deep) that has already needed two corrections.

Finding 130: the XGetVideoMode fix's values DO reach the caller
correctly, but the hang persists -- so the gating isn't what
Finding 129 assumed.

Re-verified the stall live on the clean binary: thread #4 pinned at
`sub_824A0B68+396` (self-branch), `x10 = 0x01000000` at the check,
i.e. field+92 of the object at guest 0x826E2110 holds exactly the
value Finding 127's watchpoint captured. Finding 127's attribution
of the blocking condition is correct. Attach-by-raw-address
breakpoints failed (ASLR load bias); reliable method is external
attach to the already-stuck process and read registers after
`thread select 4`.

Test: re-applied an XGetVideoMode that writes width=1280, height=720,
hiDef=1 into the caller's struct, and added a temporary guest-side
printf right after the call. Output: `hiDef=0x1 height=720` -- the
caller reads back exactly what was written. Yet the stall is
unchanged. So the branch that stores 0x01000000 is not controlled by
the XVIDEO_MODE fields alone. Next step: map which guest instruction
stores the 0x01000000 value and walk backward from it on the live
disassembly, rather than inferring the gate from the source.

Reverted both temporary edits (kernel_impl.cpp via git checkout;
the guest-side printf and its cstdio include in the git-ignored
private/ppc/ppc_recomp.66.cpp by hand). Rebuilt clean; `git status`
shows no tracked changes.

Finding 131: the 0x01000000 store to field+92 is NOT gated by the
XVIDEO_MODE values. The real gate is field+28 of the same object,
checked on entry to sub_824A78E0. Finding 130's hypothesis (height /
hiDef control the store) was wrong.

Walked the live disassembly of __imp__sub_824A78E0 (current clean
binary, `disassemble --name ... --count 400`) backward from the store
at +624 (`mov w9,#0x1000000; str w9,[x19,w11,uxtw]`):
- +224 `cbz w9` on field+28 (loaded at +172 from the object). If
  field+28 != 0 the function takes the +228..+312 path and returns
  early via __restgprlr_20 -- no store.
- field+28 == 0 falls to +316..+500, which calls __imp__XGetVideoMode.
- Every path after XGetVideoMode (hiDef==0 at +544 -> +592; height
  < 720 at +588 -> +592; height >= 720 -> +752 -> +768 height-vs-1080
  -> +612; height >= 1080 -> +1244 -> +612) converges on +612 and
  then falls straight into the +620/+624 store. Nothing between +612
  and the store branches, so the store is unconditional past the
  field+28 gate.

Consistent with the source: `lwz r11,28(r31); cmpwi r11,0; beq
->loc_824A7920` at ppc_recomp.66.cpp:5066-5071 (proceed only when
field+28 is zero). sub_824A0B68 zeroes field+28 only on the path
where field+92 == 0 (its own cbz at +392/+400), so when field+92 is
nonzero (the stall) field+28 is never cleared either.

Live watchpoint evidence from Finding 127 (90 s run, two writes only)
means nothing else writes field+92, so no clearing path exists in
this build. Result: sub_824A78E0 sets the bit whenever it runs with
field+28 == 0, and sub_824A0B68 then spins forever on it.

Open question for the next step: what should make field+28 nonzero
(or otherwise stop sub_824A78E0 from running its display setup) on
the normal path? That's the real missing piece, not the mode values.

Finding 132: field+28 is itself set by sub_824A78E0 -- so it's a
one-time latch, not a value the stall path ever clears.

Watchpoint on field+28 (guest 0x826E212C, host 0x70826E212C) from
`__imp___xstart` for a short run: one change, 0 -> 0x1000000, at
`sub_824A78E0+9364`, called from `sub_824A84E0+252` <-
`sub_825F8BD0+268` <- `sub_8244B040+3384` <- `sub_8212B130+232`.
Live disasm of +9352..+9364: `ldr w8,[x20,#0xf8]` (object) ;
`add w8,w8,#0x1c` ; `mov w10,#0x1000000` ; `str w10,[x19,w8,uxtw]`
-- a real `stw 0x1000000, 28(r31)` on the function's tail. It runs
after an optional call (`sub_820CD028`, +9316) and both paths
converge on +9328, so the write always happens once the function
gets past its entry gate.

So one call to sub_824A78E0 does two things: sets field+28 nonzero
(Finding 131's entry gate, now latched -- the next call returns
early) and sets field+92 nonzero (Finding 131's store at +624). The
only path that zeroes field+28 is sub_824A0B68's unblocked branch
(cbz on field+92 == 0). In the stall that branch never runs, so
field+28 stays latched and field+92 stays set.

Remaining question: which event should clear field+92 (or unlatch
field+28) after the first display setup. Nothing in this build
writes field+92 after the first call (Finding 127), and the
watchpoint shows nothing rewrites field+28 either.

Finding 133: the display-setup caller chain, and why it doesn't
self-correct.

Read the callers of the latching function (live chain from Finding
132):
- `sub_825F8BD0` (ppc_recomp.109.cpp:5534): reads the canonical
  object (slot 0x826EB104, the same object as Findings 113-125) and
  the display object at 0x826E2110 (r29). It reads display field+16
  and calls the display-mode setup path (via sub_824A84E0).
- `sub_824A84E0` (ppc_recomp.66.cpp:6748): stores its three
  arguments into display fields +0 (width), +4 (height), +16, sets
  +20/+24/+64 to fixed values, calls `sub_824A78E0` (the latch), and
  then clamps width/height against a small table (1280x720,
  640x480). The caller's arguments are the source of the resolution.

So the display setup is driven each tick from `sub_8244B040` through
`sub_825F8BD0`. Nothing in this chain clears display field+92 or
field+28. The only place that zeroes field+28 is the unblocked branch
of `sub_824A0B68`, which never runs because field+92 stays set
(Findings 127, 131, 132). That's a deadlock inside the build's own
logic: the setup latches its flags, then the teardown waits for a
completion signal the build never produces.

Hypotheses for the missing completion signal (none verified):
1. A vblank / graphics-interrupt callback that the game expects to
   clear field+92. Mostly ruled out: Finding 114 showed the
   UnleashedRecomp reference stubs these callbacks too and still
   runs.
2. A kernel call on the normal path (XGetVideoMode returning real
   data) that we're still missing. Finding 130 showed the XVIDEO_MODE
   values reach the caller and don't change the outcome.
3. The first setup is supposed to run only once, behind a guard that
   this build satisfies incorrectly on entry. Would explain why the
   latch sits at the wrong value at the first call.

Hypothesis 3 is the cheapest to test next: trace the guard that
decides whether `sub_825F8BD0` calls `sub_824A84E0` at all, in the
per-tick loop, to see whether this single call should be running
at all on the normal path.

Finding 134: the field+28 / display-setup guard test. Hypothesis 3
from Finding 133 is falsified -- the setup is supposed to run once,
and it does. The stall is downstream, in sub_824A0B68's wait on
field+92. Also corrects the byte-order error behind several earlier
"0x01000000" values.

Guard, read from live source and the disassembly:
- The per-tick caller `sub_8244B040` calls `sub_825F8BD0`
  unconditionally (ppc_recomp.57.cpp:2539-2541); its return value
  only gates the code after it.
- The real guard is inside `sub_825F8BD0` (ppc_recomp.109.cpp:
  5546-5573): `clrlwi r11,r11,31` on display field+16, then `bne` to
  `loc_825F8C20` (return 0). So the setup runs only when display
  field+16 bit 0 is CLEAR.
- `sub_824A78E0`'s own early return on field+28 (Finding 131) is a
  second, independent guard inside the setup.

Live values, same build:
- At `__imp___xstart` (boot): display field+16 = 0, field+28 = 0,
  field+92 = 0. The bit-0 guard passes, so the setup runs exactly
  once -- matching the single watchpoint hit in Finding 132.
- During the stall (attach, 6 s in): field+16 = 85983747 (0x05200203,
  bit 0 SET), field+28 = 1, field+92 = 1. So after the first setup,
  the guard blocks any second call. Nothing re-runs the setup, and
  the stall is not caused by repeated setup.

Correction to Findings 127-133: the guest value written to field+28
and field+92 is 1, not 0x01000000. Those earlier values came from
watchpoint "new value" output and from raw `str w9` host bytes
(little-endian). A big-endian guest reads 0x00000001. Read through
`__builtin_bswap32`, both fields are 1. The "0x01000000 bit" framing
(which Finding 127 used to infer a bit-24 status flag) is wrong; the
latch is a plain boolean. Findings 127's stall attribution (a busy
wait on field+92, as an equality test against zero) still holds.

Net: the setup-guard path behaves correctly. The remaining question
is still the same, now sharper: what should clear field+92 back to 0
so that sub_824A0B68 unblocks and clears field+28? Nothing in this
build writes field+92 after the first setup.

Finding 135: the stall is the idle-tick teardown, not a stray call.
Also corrects Finding 128's negative result.

Teardown flow, live-confirmed from source:
- `sub_8244B040` (ppc_recomp.57.cpp:2788-2818): when the work queue
  item's count (+8) is zero (the normal idle tick), it falls through
  to `loc_8244B610` and calls `sub_824A3CC8`.
- `sub_824A3CC8` (ppc_recomp.65.cpp:31506-31560): only tears down if
  display field+16 bit 0 is SET (the state the setup leaves behind)
  and field+28 is nonzero. It then calls `sub_824A0B68` (the spin)
  and clears field+16 bit 0, which lets the setup run again later.
- `sub_824A0B68`: calls four guest functions (`sub_8249A228`,
  `sub_8251C158`, `sub_820B4B88`, `sub_820B9B30`) and then reads
  field+92 once. Nonzero means self-branch forever.

So the design expects field+92 to be cleared by an asynchronous
completion between the setup's store and that read. Nothing in this
build does that, and the stall is the first idle tick after setup.

Correction to Finding 128: its periodic 1 ms clear could not have
landed between the setup's store and the read, which happen within
microseconds on the same thread. That negative result was not a
test of the completion-signal hypothesis.

Hook limits: all four callees before the check are guest code (no
host implementation in kernel_impl.cpp or kernel_stubs.cpp), so a
host-side hook cannot clear field+92 at the right moment without
patching guest instructions, which this build cannot do at runtime
(the guest code is compiled host text).

Decision needed: the two available experiments are (a) a
diagnostic build that replaces the field+92 check in sub_824A0B68
with an unconditional fall-through, to see whether the game gets
past the first idle tick, or (b) stopping. (a) is a test only and
would not be committed as a fix.

Finding 136: removing the field+92 wait in sub_824A0B68 ends the
stall. Diagnostic only, not committed.

Test: temporarily replaced the `beq cr6,0x824a0bc8` on the field+92
compare with an unconditional `goto loc_824A0BC8` in the generated
ppc_recomp.65.cpp (git-ignored, reverted by hand afterward), extended
the watchdog to 60 s, rebuilt, and ran.

Result: no busy-spin. Attached 8 s in: thread #4 (the guest main
thread) is parked at `sub_825DEC18+96`, which is the designed idle
self-loop after `sub_8212B130` returns. Worker threads are on normal
waits (`__semwait_signal` / `__psynch_cvwait`). The process still
exits with the watchdog message at the end of the run, consistent
with the designed idle state rather than a hang. Log tail: the same
ExTerminateThread / MmFreePhysicalMemory sequence as before, then
`NtWaitForSingleObjectEx: unknown handle 0x1007`.

Meaning: the field+92 completion wait is the only thing holding the
display setup open. Once the wait is out of the way, the main tick
chain returns instead of spinning, and the guest reaches its idle
state. That confirms the stall is this one wait, not something
downstream.

Not established: whether the game is playable after this (no renderer
or content check was done), or whether the real completion signal is
a kernel call or a GPU event. The next question is what the real
build clears field+92 with, so the wait can be satisfied correctly
rather than bypassed.

Reverted both temporary edits; `git diff --stat host/main.cpp` is
clean and the binary was rebuilt from the clean tree.

Finding 137: searched for the real completion signal for field+92.
Not found. Recorded what was ruled out.

Method: scanned every generated ppc_recomp file for functions that
(a) build the display object (`lis -32146` + `addi ...,8464`) and
(b) store to offset 92 through a non-stack base. 34 functions had
offset-92 stores; 8 had a non-stack base. Live watchpoint evidence
(Findings 127, 132, 136) already showed that in the reachable run,
field+92 is written only by `sub_824A78E0`, so any real clearer must
live on a path this build never reaches.

Ruled out as the clearer:
- `sub_824A2B90` (ppc_recomp.65.cpp:29361): allocator/constructor that
  stores a computed nonzero value.
- `sub_82515D58` (ppc_recomp.71.cpp:3088): initialization routine
  storing r30 at offsets 76/88/92 of a different object.
- `sub_8244B040` / `sub_82451630`: stores at +92 are on other
  objects (the canonical object, not the display object).

Not checked in full: `sub_8249D1C0` (`stb r11,92(r31)`), `sub_824A2A48`
(`stb r10,92(r3)`), `sub_82405DA0` (`stw ...,92(r20)`), and the
offset-92 stores in `sub_824A78E0` at +6380 (`stw r10,92(r11)`).

Conclusion so far: no clearing store to the display object's field+92
exists on any path reachable in this build's run. That fits a
completion signal that is delivered asynchronously by host or device
code this build does not produce. Next step, not done: check whether
any kernel or GPU call in the display path (VdSetDisplayMode,
VdSwap, VdCallGraphicsNotificationRoutines, the vblank interrupt
callback) is expected to deliver that signal.

Finding 138: the vblank / swap callback path is not the field+92
completion signal. Ruled out.

Checked the registered graphics callback that `VdSwap` already invokes
(host/kernel_impl.cpp:1692-1696, Finding 32): the game's callback
`sub_820B5160` (ppc_recomp.4.cpp:11402). Its body manipulates kernel
event bits on a different object (offsets 10772/10776 from its own
argument, `r4`), uses a `bctrl` through a registered handler on that
object, and never touches the display object at guest 0x826E2110.
So a vblank event cannot clear display field+92 directly.

Remaining candidates for the completion signal, not yet checked:
- A write from a worker thread that the stall path waits on
  (`__semwait_signal` / `__psynch_cvwait` threads in Finding 136's
  attach). Those threads sit in normal waits; none was observed
  storing to the display object.
- The `stb` stores at `sub_8249D1C0` (ppc_recomp.65.cpp:14600) and
  `sub_824A2A48` (ppc_recomp.65.cpp:28882), which Finding 137 did not
  finish. Neither is confirmed as writing the display object.

Finding 139: the last two static candidates for writing the display
object's field+92 are ruled out. Static search over the generated
code finds no clearing store to that field on any path.

- `sub_8249D1C0` (ppc_recomp.65.cpp:14600, `stb r11,92(r31)`): r31 is
  the function's own argument (`mr r31,r4`), not the display object.
  The display address is only loaded into r11 as a value stored into
  that argument's +92 byte. Not a write to the display object.
- `sub_824A2A48` (ppc_recomp.65.cpp:28882, `stb r10,92(r3)` with
  r10 = 1): r3 is the function's argument object. It writes 1 to that
  object's +92, and the display object is not involved.

Static conclusion: no store in the generated code clears the display
object's field+92 on any reachable path. Together with the live
watchpoints (Findings 127, 132, 136), the only writer is the setup
(sets it to 1), and nothing in this build's code ever clears it.

So the completion signal is not produced by any guest code in the
reachable path. It must come from host or device behavior the build
does not model yet, or the clear sits on a branch this build's
inputs never reach. Not resolved; recommend deciding the next step
with the user rather than guessing further.

Finding 140: a host-side clear of display field+92 in `VdSwap` unblocks
the stall. Diagnostic only, reverted, not committed.

Test: added a temporary block at the top of `VdSwap` (host/kernel_impl.cpp)
that stores 0 to guest 0x826E216C if it is nonzero, extended the watchdog
to 60 s, rebuilt, and ran.

Result: the clear fired once, on the first `VdSwap` after the display
setup (log: `VdSwap cleared display field+92 (was 1)`). The main guest
thread then sat at `sub_825DEC18+96` (the designed idle loop) at 8 s,
not in the `sub_824A0B68` spin. Same outcome as Finding 136's direct
bypass of the wait.

Meaning: `VdSwap` is a usable host-observable point between the display
setup's latch and the teardown's read, and clearing the field there is
sufficient for the game to reach its idle state. This is a placeholder
for the real completion signal, not the signal itself. Timing and
ordering were not verified against real hardware.

Reverted: `git checkout -- host/kernel_impl.cpp` restored the file, the
watchdog is back to 10 s, and the binary was rebuilt from the clean tree.

Finding 141: the VdSwap placeholder for display field+92 is committed.
This is a stand-in for the real completion signal, not the signal
itself (see Finding 140 for the test).

Change: host/kernel_impl.cpp `__imp__VdSwap` clears guest 0x826E216C
(display object field+92) to 0 when it is nonzero, with a comment
stating it is a placeholder for the real completion signal, not
verified against real hardware timing, and to be replaced when the
signal is identified.

Verified: rebuilt and ran the placeholder build headless; exit code 2
(the standard watchdog exit), with the same idle-state outcome as the
Finding 140 test. Not a crash.

Still open: the real completion signal for display field+92.

Finding 142: after the placeholder (Finding 141), the windowed build
presents frames but shows no game draw activity. Not playable yet.

Ran `--window` for 12 s. Renderer report: 589 drawn frames, 33 present
signals. Log: no `VdSwap` lines in the kernel trace and no PM4 draw
lines; the only draw-related output is the renderer's own summary.
Stubbed kernel calls are the expected boot set (MmFreePhysicalMemory
x66, NetDll_XNetGetEthernetLinkStatus x3, a few profile and launch
calls, one KeReleaseSemaphore). Guest main thread is parked in the
designed idle loop.

Read: the window is presenting, but the presents are not carrying game
content. I did not look at the window itself, so a blank or clear
screen is an inference from the trace, not a visual check.

Next step, not done: find where the game is supposed to issue its
first frame's draw commands after the idle loop, and whether they are
gated by the same display state or by content that is not loaded.

Finding 143: the game does issue frames and draws, and the renderer
drops the real geometry. Frames are not empty.

Evidence:
- `breakpoint set --name __imp__VdSwap` fired repeatedly during a run,
  called from `sub_820CCA68` <- `sub_824A76D0` (the display frame
  path). The game presents every frame.
- `gpu_trace.log` from a 12 s windowed run: 118 IM_LOAD_IMMEDIATE, 52
  DRAW_INDX_2 lines split as:
  - 28 with `primType=8`, logged as `unsupported primType=8, skipped`.
  - 24 with `primType=1 vertexCount=1` (the sub-project 2 point
    placeholder).

Primitive type 8 is `kRectangleList` (0x08), confirmed against Xenia's
`src/xenia/gpu/xenos.h` (PrimitiveType enum). A rectangle list is three
vertices per rectangle, which Xenia's own comment (xenos.h:934) notes
D3D9 resolves by drawing as a triangle.

So the first real gap is concrete: the renderer skips the game's
rectangle-list draws. Implementing that is a renderer change (expand
each rectangle to two triangles, or emit the third corner), not a
kernel fix. Not implemented in this finding; needs a decision.

Finding 144: rectangle-list draws (primType 8) are expanded to
triangles in the draw-command builder. Trace-verified, not visually.

Change (host/gpu_trace.cpp): primType 8 maps to the existing Triangle
draw type with an `isRectList` flag. For non-indexed rectangle lists,
each group of three source vertices A, B, C becomes two triangles,
(A, B, D) and (A, D, C), where D = B + C - A on the position. Each
vertex keeps its full source record stride so the translated vertex
layout still applies. D's other attributes copy B's record, which is
an approximation. Indexed rectangle lists are logged and skipped for
now. No renderer change was needed.

Verified: rebuilt clean; a 12 s windowed run logs 28 primType=8 draws
each with vertexCount=6 (two triangles), and the renderer logs no
errors. The window was not looked at, so the image itself is not
confirmed.

Still open: indexed rectangle lists, the approximated attribute
interpolation on the implied corner, and whether the rectangles land
where the game intends (needs a visual check).

Finding 145: visual check of the rectangle expansion (Finding 144).
Rectangles reach the screen as geometry; the placeholder shader colours
them black, and no game art is drawn yet.

macOS blocked a full-screen capture (`screencapture`: "could not create
image from display"; the terminal lacks screen-recording permission and
that cannot be granted from here). Instead, a temporary readback in the
Metal renderer rendered one frame into an offscreen texture of the same
size and format, wrote its BGRA pixels to a file, and converted to PNG.

First attempt: at frame 120 the snapshot held only the 24 point
placeholders at the origin and showed only the clear colour. Those
frames carry no rectangles.

Second attempt: the dump triggered on the first frame containing a
rectangle-expanded command (six-vertex `Triangle`). That frame shows a
single black triangle in the lower-right, with a diagonal edge clipped
at the frame boundary, about 111k non-clear sampled pixels of 2560x1440
at 1/7 sampling. It is consistent with one of a rectangle's two
triangles, the other probably off-screen; not confirmed against the
game's intended layout.

Reverted the renderer readback (`git checkout -- host/renderer_metal.mm`),
rebuilt clean, and confirmed no stray processes.

Not established: whether the rectangles' positions are what the game
intends, the real colours and textures (blocked on the placeholder
shader and on content), and the indexed rectangle case (still skipped).

Finding 146: the black triangle is the game's own vertex colour, not a
shader fault. The "placeholder shader" concern from Finding 145 does
not hold: the renderer is already using the translated shader from
sub-project 3b (`now using real translated shader`), and the vertex
colour data reaches it.

Checked the expanded rectangle vertices directly (temporary print in
host/gpu_trace.cpp, reverted): the float4 attribute at byte offset 12
of each 28-byte record is real data.
- first rectangle: attr = (0.000, 1.000, 1.000, 0.498) (cyan, half alpha)
- second rectangle: attr = (0.000, 0.000, 0.000, 0.996) (black)

The frame dumped in Finding 145 showed a black triangle, which matches the
second rectangle's colour. So the rendering path is carrying the game's
colours correctly for at least those two rectangles.

Still open: the cyan rectangle did not appear in that dumped frame (it
may be off-screen or in a different frame); textures (no texture binding
is in this path); whether more rectangles across frames cover the
intended layout; indexed rectangle lists (still skipped).

Reverted the print; `git status` shows no tracked changes in host/.

Finding 147: why the cyan rectangle is missing from the captured frame.
Not fully resolved. The cause is a coordinate-space mismatch between
the game's vertex positions and what the renderer does with them.

Positions (temporary print, reverted): the cyan rectangle's six expanded
vertices span x -0.5..2559.5 and y -0.5..191.5. The black rectangle
spans x -0.5..1279.5 and y -0.5..479.5. The renderer passes these
straight through as normalized device coordinates (the translated vertex
shader applies no transform), so most of the cyan rectangle lies far
outside the [-1, 1] view and never rasterizes.

Viewport (temporary print of writes to PA_CL_VPORT_* registers 0x210F..
0x2114, reverted): the game writes x scale 640, x offset 640, y scale
-240, y offset 240, z scale 1, z offset 0. That describes a 1280x480
window, and the renderer ignores it. Applying it as window coordinates
does not bring the cyan rectangle on-screen either: its x extent reaches
2559, beyond 1280.

So the positions are not in the 1280x480 window space, and they are not
normalized device coordinates either. Reading the Xenos register that
enables or disables viewport transform (PA_CL_VTE_CNTL) and checking how
the game builds its rectangle vertices is the next step. Not done.

Reverted the gpu_state print; `git status` shows no tracked changes in
host/. The renderer is unchanged by this finding.

Finding 148: the cyan rectangle appears once rectangle positions are
mapped to normalized coordinates when the viewport is off. Verified
visually on one frame.

Register check: PA_CL_VTE_CNTL (0x2206) takes two values in this run:
0x00000300 (viewport scale/offset enables bits 0-5 all clear) and
0x0000043F (enables set). Xenos treats a position as a window pixel
coordinate when those enables are clear; the renderer had been
treating every position as NDC.

Change (host/gpu_trace.cpp, rectangle-list expansion only): when
PA_CL_VTE_CNTL bits 0-5 are all clear, each rectangle position (and the
implied fourth corner) maps from window pixels to NDC for a 1280x720
target: x' = (x + 0.5) * 2 / 1280 - 1, y' = 1 - (y + 0.5) * 2 / 720.
Positions with viewport enabled are unchanged.

Visual check (temporary offscreen readback in the renderer, reverted):
the cyan rectangle now fills the top band of the frame, down to about
191 px, matching its position extent. The black rectangle is also
mapped and was not separately checked.

Caveat: the 1280x720 target size is assumed from the game's own
AVIVO viewport value and the project's fixed window, not read from the
game's render target. The black rectangle's placement and the rest of
the frame are not checked. Indexed rectangle lists are still skipped.

Finding 149: target size confirmed from the game's own scissor
registers; the cyan band is a 1280x192 window region.

Temporary print of writes (reverted):
- PA_SC_SCREEN_SCISSOR_BR (0x200F) = 0x20002000: the maximum, so the
  screen scissor does not constrain the frame.
- PA_SC_WINDOW_SCISSOR_BR (0x2082) = 0x00C00500: x 1280, y 192. That is
  a 1280x192 window region, and its bottom edge matches the cyan band's
  bottom at about 191.5 px seen in Finding 148.
- Smaller window regions (0x00100010, 16x16) also appear.

So the 1280x720 logical target assumed in Finding 148 is consistent
with the game's own scissor values, and the cyan rectangle is a
full-width 1280x192 band that the window scissor would clip anyway.
The black rectangle, which spans 1280x480, is not separately checked
in a visual frame. Reverted the register print; host/ has no tracked
changes.

Finding 150: the black rectangle is drawn, but its shape is a slanted
parallelogram, which suggests the fourth-corner rule is wrong for this
game's vertex order.

Earlier frames did not contain both rectangles: the dump captured only
the cyan one, and the render loop logged `1 cmds`. Triggering the dump on
a frame with two rectangle commands captured both (`render loop: 2 cmds`).

Frame (1280x720 logical, dumped at half resolution): cyan band across the
top right, black drawn over it. Black fills from the top edge down to a
bottom edge at about 480 px, between x 0 and 1280, with a diagonal left
edge running from (0, 0) to (640, 480) and a diagonal right edge from
(640, 0) to (1280, 480). The bottom edge matches black's position extent.

The shape is a parallelogram because the mapped inputs are
A(-0.5,-0.5), B(639.5,-0.5), C(1279.5,479.5), and the fourth corner
D = B + C - A puts the implied corner at (1919.5, 479.5). With three
top-left, top-right, bottom-left inputs, that rule gives an axis-aligned
rectangle. These inputs are not that arrangement, so the rule does not
hold for this game's vertices. Xenia's D3D9 resolve comment (xenos.h:
928-937) describes three vertices for a rectangle list without giving the
corner order, so the rule is not confirmed from that source either.

Next step, not done: test the candidate corner rules (for example D =
A + C - B, or treating the three inputs as top-left, bottom-left,
bottom-right) against the expected axis-aligned rectangles, and check the
game's own vertex order. Reverted the renderer readback and the vertex
print; host/ has no tracked changes.

Finding 151: the corrected corner rule gives axis-aligned rectangles.
Both were visually checked on their own frames.

Derived from the mapped positions. The game's three vertices for a
rectangle have the right angle at the second vertex (v1). For the cyan
rectangle: (-1,1), (1,1), (1,0.467). For the black one: (-1,1), (0,1),
(0,-0.333). So the fourth corner is D = v0 + v2 - v1, not v1 + v2 - v0.
Triangles split along the diagonal v0-v2: (v0, v1, v2) and (v0, v2, D).

Change (host/gpu_trace.cpp): D = A + C - B, and the triangles become
(A,B,C) and (A,C,D). Attributes of D still copy B's record.

Visual checks (temporary offscreen readback, reverted):
- Cyan: a full-width band, top 192 px, exactly matching the game's
  window scissor (1280x192, Finding 149).
- Black: an axis-aligned rectangle covering x 0-640, y 0-480 (the
  expected extent of its three vertices).

The two rectangles were checked in separate frames, because the game
does not always submit both in the same frame. Renderer readback and
the diagnostic print were reverted; only gpu_trace.cpp is changed.

Finding 152: indexed rectangle lists are now expanded through the index
buffer, but the path is untested: no indexed rectangle draw occurred in
the 12 s captures.

Change (host/gpu_trace.cpp): the rectangle branch is now the first
branch of the vertex-count chain. For indexed draws it reads the resolved
index buffer (16- or 32-bit, host little-endian after resolution) and
gathers each triangle's three vertices by index, skipping any index beyond
the vertex buffer's capacity. Non-indexed draws use vertices in order, as
before. The fourth-corner rule and the window-to-NDC mapping are unchanged.
The old indexed-rectangle skip is removed.

Verified: rebuilt clean; a 12 s windowed run logs 28 non-indexed
primType=8 draws with vertexCount=6, unchanged from Finding 151, and no
skipped-draw lines. No indexed rectangle draw appeared, so the new indexed
path has not run on real data.

Open: confirm that indexed rectangle draws appear in a longer run, and
check their output visually once one does.

Finding 153: the indexed rectangle path still has no real input. A
40 s windowed run submitted the same fixed draw set every frame: 28
non-indexed `primType=8` draws (vertexCount 6) and 24 `primType=1`
point placeholders. No indexed draw of any type appeared, so the indexed
path from Finding 152 cannot be checked visually until the game submits
an indexed rectangle list. Left untested, not reverted.

Finding 154: a connected idle controller does not change the static draw
set. Input is ruled out as the reason the frame is fixed. Diagnostic only,
reverted.

Test: temporarily made XamInputGetCapabilities and XamInputGetState report
user 0 as a connected gamepad with no buttons and neutral sticks (instead
of "device not connected"). Ran 40 s windowed. The draw set was unchanged:
28 non-indexed primType=8 draws and 24 point placeholders, 597 drawn
frames. XamInputGetState is not logged by a stub, so the log does not show
whether the game called it; the unchanged output is the evidence that the
game's frame content does not depend on this input in this run.

Read: the main guest thread is in its idle loop (Finding 136), and
VdSwap re-presents the last draw list. The static content is most likely
because the frame logic is not advancing, not because it waits for a pad.
Next step, not done: find what the idle main thread would normally be
driven by to build new frames.

Finding 155: the frame driver is inside the original outer loop. Frame
building may be gated by the same loading-complete flag as Finding 106.

Traced the frame path (static, from the generated source):
- `sub_820CCA68` is the helper that calls `VdSwap` (three call sites).
- `sub_824A76D0` is the frame function that calls it
  (ppc_recomp.66.cpp:13468), reached via `sub_824AAFF0`
  (ppc_recomp.66.cpp:13468 caller chain).
- `sub_824AAFF0` is called from `sub_8244AFB0`, the outer loop of
  Finding 106 (ppc_recomp.57.cpp:1918, inside that function's range), and
  from several other places.
- The time base is real (`__rdtsc` reads `cntvct_el0`, ppc_context.h:731),
  so frame pacing does not explain the static frame.

Read: frames are issued from `sub_8244AFB0`'s loop, and that loop was the
stall Finding 106 found in the first place, gated on the canonical object's
`field+136`. Finding 113 traced `field+136` back to `field+76`, which the
boot patch in Finding 120 sets. So the static frame may be the same
loading-complete gate still holding, rather than a separate frame-logic
problem. Not verified: a live check of `field+136` during the frame loop.

Finding 156: the loading-complete gate is open, so Finding 155's
hypothesis is ruled out. The static frame is not caused by that flag.

Live read (external attach to the running windowed build, about 8 s in),
canonical object at guest 0x826EB104 (the Finding 113 object):
- field+136 = 1 (the completion flag sub_8244AFB0 waits on, Finding 106)
- field+76 = 1 (the boot-patched value, Finding 120)
- field+72 = 1 (the mode selector, Finding 113)

So the outer loop's completion condition is satisfied. The frame driver
(`sub_824AAFF0`) runs inside that loop and presents frames, which matches
the repeated VdSwap calls. The frame content does not change, so the
scene the game builds is static for some other reason. Not identified.

Finding 157: the frame function is a present and timing wrapper. It does
not build scene content.

Read `sub_824A76D0` (ppc_recomp.66.cpp, body from the function start):
- It runs a registered callback through a function pointer loaded from a
  table at lis -32143 offset -540 (`bctrl`), then reads display field+16
  bit 10.
- When that bit is set and field+300 is nonzero, it calls `sub_820CC8E0`,
  then `sub_820CCA68` (the VdSwap helper, with an argument from a table at
  field+228, indexed by field+300), then returns.
- It also records frame timing (`mftb` deltas into a global, field+220 and
  field+224 from the frame-rate values in field+248..+260).

So the swap is conditional on display field+16 bit 10 and the frame index
field+300, and the scene's draw commands are not written here. The static
content must come from the command builder that fills the ring buffer
before this wrapper runs. Not located. Next step, not done: find which
function writes the PM4 command stream each frame, and check whether it
runs on each frame or once.

Finding 158: the static frame is the game's own repeating command set,
not a tracer or ring artifact.

Counted the traced frame blocks from a 12 s windowed run (gpu_trace.log,
"starting offset"): the ring start offsets vary (0, 2332, 124, 988, 700 and
others), so the read pointer does advance. The offsets cycle through the
same small set, so the game writes the same command content each frame.
The tracer is not re-reading one stuck region.

Read: the screen is what the game draws in its current state. Its frame
content is static because its own state machine is not advancing (the
earlier controller test, Finding 154, and the loading-complete flags,
Finding 156, are not the cause). Renderer, tracer and ring handling are
behaving as the game's commands direct.

Not identified: the game-state condition that holds the scene constant
(likely a menu or loading state waiting on something the build does not
yet supply). Not located in this finding.

Finding 159: the per-frame callback that the frame wrapper calls is never
registered. A possible explanation for the static scene, not confirmed.

Live (external attach, windowed build, 8 s in): the callback pointer
slot at guest 0x8270FE20 and its argument slot at 0x8270FE24 both read 0.
`sub_824A76D0` (ppc_recomp.66.cpp, the frame wrapper) loads the pointer
from that slot and calls it through `bctrl` only when it is nonzero, so
the per-frame callback is skipped every frame.

Static search: the only generated function that addresses that slot is
`sub_824A76D0` itself (lis -32143, addi -540). No generated code writes it.
So the registration is not in the generated code; it is either a
mechanism the build does not reach (a boot path that never runs) or a
host-side call that should set it and does not.

Read: this is a second, independent missing registration, alongside the
display-latch and frame-state chains from earlier findings. Whether it is
what holds the scene static is not yet tested. Next step, not done: find
the function that should register this per-frame callback (a boot-time
initializer, or a kernel call that takes a function pointer), then check
whether it runs in this build.

Finding 160: no writer found for the frame-callback slot (guest
0x8270FE20). Two more paths ruled out.

- Direct-offset search: no generated code loads or stores at offset -540
  or -544 from a non-stack base, apart from one unrelated load
  (ppc_recomp.61.cpp:12083, `lwz r18,-544(r6)`). The slot is not reached
  by that addressing form.
- Host-side registration: `VdSetGraphicsInterruptCallback` stores its
  callback in the host tracer (`g_gpuTracer.SetGraphicsInterruptCallback`),
  not in guest memory at that address. It is a separate callback
  (interrupt/vblank), not this frame-wrapper pointer.

So the slot's writer is not a plain store in the generated code, and not
a host-side kernel call either. It may be written through a pointer
computed at runtime, or by a boot path this build does not reach. Not
resolved. Recommend pausing this chase: it has taken many rounds without
converging on a single missing step.

Finding 161: write-watchpoint on the frame-callback slot (guest
0x8270FE20, host 0x708270FE20), set at `__imp___xstart`, run to the
watchdog exit: one hit only, a store of 0. No store of a nonzero value
occurred in the run.

Read: the slot is never registered in this build. Hardware watchpoints
catch every store regardless of addressing form, so this closes the
question that Finding 160 left open. It does not show what the game
expects that callback to do.

Note: my first attempt used a mistyped host address (0x70827FE20) and
set no valid watchpoint; it was rerun with the correct address. The
backtrace of the single zero store was not captured (the script's
backtrace came after the second continue), so the writer of that zero
is unknown. It is not the registration.

Finding 162: the frame-callback registration is still not found in the
generated code, and the search has stopped converging.

Checked: `addi` of -544 and -548 from any base (only stack-relative hits and
one unrelated `addi r7,r11,-548` at ppc_recomp.33.cpp:18055, whose base is
not confirmed to be the display region); direct offsets -540 and -544 from
any non-stack base (one unrelated load). The runtime watchpoint (Finding
161) showed no nonzero store to the slot across the run.

Read: the callback is not registered by any store this build reaches. The
frame wrapper's per-frame callback may be optional, meaning the scene the
build draws is produced without it. That would make the callback a red
herring for the static frame. Not tested.

Recommendation: stop chasing the callback. The remaining unknown is what
the game's scene logic is waiting on, and that is better approached by a
runtime trace of the game's own state variables across frames than by
searching for this one slot.

Finding 163: first runtime state trace (headless, per-present diff of the
display object at 0x826E2110 and the canonical object, reverted after).

Changes seen per frame (frames 1-32):
- display +300 alternates 1 <-> 2 every frame (double-buffer index): the
  frame logic runs.
- canonical +12 advances by roughly 0.8 million ticks per frame (a
  timestamp, read from the real timebase): the game's clock runs.
- display +92 goes 1 -> 0 at frame 1 (the Finding 141 VdSwap placeholder
  clears it, as intended).
- canonical +136 goes 0 -> 1 at frame 30 (the loading-complete flag from
  Finding 156).
- display +308..+380 are filled in at frame 1 (per-frame state table).

After frame 32 no other word changed in this run, and VdSwap stopped
printing: about 33 presents total in 10 s headless. Earlier windowed runs
presented about 590 frames, so this may be the headless run ending, not the
game freezing.

Read so far: the game's per-frame logic and clock are running, and the
loading-complete flag is set at frame 30. The state that is still
unexplained is why the scene content stays constant in the windowed run.
Next: repeat the trace in the windowed run for a longer span, to see
whether any state word changes after frame 32.

Finding 164: correction to Finding 158. The guest stops presenting after
frame 32. The static screen is the last frame, held by the renderer.

Windowed state trace (probe from Finding 163, reverted after), 40 s run:
- The state trace shows changes only through frame 32 (last change:
  disp+300 2->1 at f32). No word changes after that.
- `present signals received: 33` in the run summary: the guest called
  VdSwap 33 times. The renderer's `drawn frames: 596` counts the renderer
  redrawing the held frame, not new guest frames.
- The run ends with the watchdog message "_xstart did not return within
  10 seconds".

Corrected read: Finding 158 said the game kept presenting the same
command set. That was wrong. The guest's frame loop stops at frame 32,
and the repeated draws are the renderer repeating the last frame. The

Next step, not done: find where the guest's main thread blocks after its

Finding 165: the stall is the main thread exiting into shutdown after the
loading loop completes, not a blocked call. The one failing wait is a side
effect, not the stall.

Evidence:
- Invalid-handle wait (handle 0x1007, never created) returns
  STATUS_INVALID_HANDLE at once; it is logged once. Caller
  `sub_820AB238` (wrapper), called from `sub_82127EE0` and `sub_82496C28`
  (retry loops). The single log line means the retry does not repeat; not
  the stall.
- Attach at about 6 s, all threads: the guest main thread (thread #9,
  `_xstart`) is at `sub_825DEC18+96`, the `b .` tail after
  `sub_8212B130` returned. `sub_8212B130` is a thread trampoline that
  `bctrl`s the per-tick function `sub_8244B040`. So `sub_8244B040`
  returned.
- Inside `sub_8244B040` (ppc_recomp.57.cpp ~2740-2810): after
  `sub_8244AFB0` (the Finding 106 loop, gated on canonical `field+136`,
  set to 1 at frame 30, Finding 156), the code reads a global (lis -32143,
  offset -916) and `r31+8`. If `r31+8 == 0` it goes to loc_8244B610 and
  calls the teardown `sub_824A3CC8` (plus `sub_8244D188`, `sub_8244E658`).
  That matches the teardown messages in the log (ExTerminateThread,
  MmFreePhysicalMemory, ObDeleteSymbolicLink) right before the watchdog.

Read: loading completes at frame 30, the loop returns, and the game takes
the shutdown branch instead of the in-game branch. The stall is a wrong
exit decision after loading, not a blocked wait.

Not yet confirmed: which condition selects shutdown (the -916 global and
`r31+8` value at that point). Next step: read those two values at the
moment `sub_8244AFB0` returns.

Finding 166: the shutdown branch is taken because `sub_8244AFB0` returned
and the post-loop code runs a normal drain-then-teardown sequence. The
open question is now what `sub_8244AFB0` returning means: loading done, or
quit.

Probe (temporary, reverted; ppc_recomp.57.cpp restored from backup, grep
confirms no TEMP-PROBE left):
- Right after `sub_8244AFB0` returns: global at guest 0x8270FC6C
  (lis -32143, offset -916) = 0; r27 object = 0xB91621F0; object+8 = 10.
- Global 0 means the `sub_824A9DD8` branch is skipped. object+8 = 10 is
  nonzero, so the code enters the drain loop (loc_8244B5F8) and calls
  `sub_82453200` on the object.
- Drain counts object+8 down 10 -> 9 -> ... -> 0, one per call. At 0 the
  code runs teardown (`sub_824A3CC8` and the following calls).

Read: the drain is a normal queue flush and the teardown is the game's own
post-loop path. So the shutdown is not a fault. It is what the code does
once `sub_8244AFB0` returns.

Open: field+136 = 1 at frame 30 (Finding 156) was taken as the
loading-complete signal. Since the return leads to shutdown, that flag may
mean "exit requested" instead, or the loop returned for a different reason
than the one it waits on. Next: read Finding 106's loop body to see which
state `field+136` gates, and what sets it. Not yet read.

Finding 167: the early exit is caused by our boot placeholder. The
loading-progress threshold field+76 is 1, so the game ends loading at
frame 30 and takes the shutdown path. Raising the placeholder keeps frames
running past 32.

Chain, now read from code and live:
- `sub_8244AFB0` (the frame loop) runs frames while canonical field+136 == 0,
  and exits when it is nonzero (ppc_recomp.57.cpp, loop tail).
- Hardware watchpoint on canonical field+136 (set at the loop's entry):
  the only write of 1 is inside `sub_82451408` (ppc_recomp.58.cpp, store
  at `stw r11,136(r31)`, ~line 4927 of the file).
- That store is conditional: a float progress accumulator (global at
  -29812) must exceed a threshold, and the threshold is `lwz r10,76(r31)`,
  i.e. field+76. The gate `cmpwi r10,0` also requires field+76 > 0.
- Our boot placeholder (host/main.cpp, kLoadingProgressConfigSlot = 0x8270F784,
  Finding 118) sets that source to 1. A threshold of 1 is passed almost
  at once, so field+136 becomes 1 at frame 30.

Experiment (temporary, reverted, not committed): placeholder 100000000
instead of 1. Result, 40 s windowed run: `present signals received: 178`
(was 33), no teardown messages, the frame loop keeps running. The draw
list did not visibly change: 177 rectangle draws and 24 point draws, one
rectangle per present. So frames continue, but this run does not show that
the picture changes. Not yet verified visually.

Read: the stall at frame 32 was our placeholder ending loading early, not a
blocked call. The real progress threshold is unknown. The number 1 was a
minimum gate guess (Finding 118), not a researched value. 100000000 is a
test value, not a fix.

Recommendation: find the real meaning and unit of field+76 (what sets the
loading-progress threshold in the console's configuration), and find what
the accumulator at -29812 measures, before choosing a value. Until then,
keep the committed placeholder unchanged.

Finding 168: field+76 is a loading-duration threshold in seconds, and the
accumulator is elapsed time. Our placeholder of 1 is one second.

Live read (temporary probe in VdSwap, reverted), per present:
- accumulator (global 0x82708B8C, the float at offset -29812) rises about
  0.033-0.035 per present: 0.0 (presents 1-2), 0.083, 0.117, 0.151, ...,
  0.966 (present 29), 1.000351 (present 30).
- field+76 = 1 and field+80 = 1.0 throughout.
- field+136 goes 0 -> 1 at present 30, the first present where the
  accumulator is >= field+76.

Read from code (sub_82451408, ppc_recomp.58.cpp): each call adds a float
built from the timebase delta (mftb / __rdtsc, the same clock as
Finding 75) to the accumulator. The threshold is compared against that
accumulator. The step size, about 0.034 per present, matches elapsed
seconds at the harness's present rate, so the unit is seconds.

Caveat: the unit comes from the step size and the code path, not from the
constant in the float formula, which is not fully decoded. Treat it as
seconds with high but not full confidence.

Consequence: a threshold of 1 means loading ends after about one second, and
the game then tears down. The real value is a minimum loading time the game
expects, which this build does not set. Choosing one would be a guess. The
value should come from the game's own configuration or be measured from the
real loading time.

Finding 169: the config chain has no loading-time key. `field+76` is a direct
copy of the placeholder global, and the chain's keys are display settings.

Read `sub_82451630` (ppc_recomp.58.cpp, lines 4956-6395), the boot-time
initializer:
- The config lookups `sub_8244E8C0` use these names, read live from guest
  memory (lldb, at the initializer's entry):
    "fps" (fallback "f") -> writes object +36, +40, +44, +48 as 60.0 / fps
    (60.0 at `-11516(r26)`, default 30 when the lookup is empty)
    "xres" (fallback "x") and "yres" (fallback "y") -> display size
  Other keys (offsets 4060-4076) were not read in this pass; none of the
  keys read so far is a loading time.
- `field+76` is written by one store, `stw r11,76(r23)` (line ~5681), where
  r11 is the global at `lis -32143` offset -2172 (guest 0x8270F784). That is
  our boot placeholder slot (Finding 118). No config lookup feeds field+76.

Corrections to Finding 168:
- Its unit ("seconds") was inferred from the accumulator's step size and
  is still the best reading. But the threshold has no config source in this
  initializer. Finding 168's chain was right that the placeholder is the
  only source here; it was wrong to imply the config chain would supply a
  loading time.

Read: this build never sets `field+76` to a real value. The value the game
expects is not in the config keys this initializer reads (fps, xres, yres).
It may come from another source (a save, a profile, or a computed value
written elsewhere). Not found yet.

Next (not done): read the other config keys at offsets 4060-4076 (the
remaining lookups in this function) to rule out a loading-time key, then
search for other writers to field+76 (the store list is in the
ppc_recomp.51.cpp range, many objects, so use a watchpoint on the
canonical object's +76 at boot).

Finding 170: the game never writes `field+76` itself. Its only writer in
the run is the boot copy from the placeholder global. The real value has to
come from the console, and this build has no copy of it.

Watchpoint on canonical field+76 (lldb, set at `sub_82451630` entry; one
write of the run after that entry):
- `sub_82451630+5128` (ppc_recomp.58.cpp, `stw r11,76(r23)`), copying the
  global at -2172 (guest 0x8270F784, our placeholder slot). No other write
  to field+76 occurred before the watchdog exit.
- The first "hit" was the watchpoint being set, not a write (the expression
  value is printed as an 8-byte value, so it is not a 4-byte store).

Checked for a game-side source of the value:
- `config/BigBumpin.toml` is the recompiler's build settings, not game
  config.
- The keys read by `sub_82451630` (fps, xres, yres) are display settings.
- Writes before `sub_82451630`'s entry are not covered by this watchpoint.
  Not checked: a boot-time write to 0x8270F784 earlier than that entry.

Read: the game expects the console to have set the global at 0x8270F784 to
the loading-threshold value before boot. Our build sets it to 1 (Finding
118). The correct value is not in the game files or the XEX, so no value
is derivable from this build. Any number is a choice, not a recovered
value.

Finding 171: threshold sweep on the placeholder (kLoadingProgressConfigSlot,
host/main.cpp). Only the value changed each run. Windowed, about 12 s each.

| value | presents | teardown messages | watchdog line |
|-------|----------|-------------------|---------------|
| 1     | 32       | 6 (teardown ran)  | yes           |
| 3     | 91       | 6 (teardown ran)  | yes           |
| 10    | 178      | 0                 | yes           |
| 30    | 179      | 0                 | yes           |

Read: teardown happens at about the value set (value 1 at about 32 presents,
value 3 at about 91). Values 10 and 30 were still running when the 10 s
watchdog ended the run. 178 presents is the same cap seen with 100000000
(Finding 167), so the run length, not the threshold, limits these runs.
Whether 10 or 30 reaches teardown or the next state is not known from this
sweep.

main.cpp is restored to the committed value (1); rebuilt.

happens and what state the game reaches.

Finding 172: with the watchdog raised to 120 s (test-only, reverted), thresholds
10 and 30 both reach teardown within about 100 s. Teardown is the same
path seen at thresholds 1 and 3 (6 `ExTerminateThread` messages each run,
then the `MmFreePhysicalMemory` cleanup). Neither run printed `_xstart`
returning. The threshold only changes how long the game runs before this
teardown.

Caveat: the present count did not print, because the runs were stopped with
pkill before the exit summary. Time to teardown was not timed. The result
for 10 and 30 is "teardown reached", not the present count.

Consequence: the teardown is not caused by one bad threshold. Every threshold
value tested ends in the same shutdown, just later. So the post-loop path
(`sub_8244B040`: `sub_8244AFB0` returns, then drain and teardown, then the
function returns, Finding 165) is the game's exit route after this loop, not
a timing issue.

Open: what the game should do after this loop completes. In the real game
the main thread does not return here, so either a next phase should start in
`sub_8244B040`, or the loop's completion should not reach teardown in this
build. Not resolved; the value of field+76 is still not the cause.

main.cpp restored (watchdog 10 s, placeholder 1); rebuilt.

Finding 173: `sub_8244B040` is a one-shot, not a state machine. It has no
loop back to its top and no call that starts a next phase.

Control-flow map (ppc_recomp.57.cpp, function range 1944-3020):
- Early return at line 57 (1944+57).
- Main body: a chain of flag-gated calls (loc_8244B098 .. loc_8244B5E8),
  then `sub_8244AFB0` (the progress/frame loop, Finding 106).
- After the loop: the drain (loc_8244B5F8, one back-branch, Finding 166),
  then teardown (loc_8244B610: `sub_82413610`, `sub_82461BE8`,
  `sub_824A3CC8`, `sub_8244D188`, `sub_8244E658`), then cleanup calls
  (`sub_82129F38`, `sub_8212BF70`, `sub_82456578`, `sub_82457D30`,
  `sub_8245DBC0`), then `sub_8212DBD0`, then return (line 1076).
- Nothing after the teardown starts a new phase or re-enters the loop.

Callers: the only caller found is the thread trampoline `sub_8212B130`
(bctrl on the function pointer, Finding 165). It is reached from
`sub_825DEC18`, which after the trampoline returns runs its own `b .`
idle loop. So the top-level thread is one call deep and does not loop.

Read: as built, this function treats "loading complete" (field+136 = 1) as
the end of the whole session. A game that continued after loading would need
either (a) field+136 cleared when the next phase starts (no clear found in
this function), or (b) the teardown to be followed by a caller loop that
runs the next phase (no such caller found in the code, since the trampoline
does not loop). Neither is present in this build.

Open: find the caller that should loop over phases, or the clear of
field+136 that should start the next phase. Next step would be a watchpoint
on canonical field+136 at the first write after loading, or the code that
runs after the trampoline's `bl 0x823f3668` (the global at offset 9956 is
checked there).

Finding 174: field+136 is written once in the run (0 -> 1, inside
`sub_82451408`, the same write as Finding 167) and never cleared. Nothing
resets it after loading, so there is no next phase to start from it.

Watchpoint on canonical field+136, set at `sub_8244AFB0` entry, continued
through the teardown to the watchdog exit:
- One real write, `sub_82451408+1912` (the 0 -> 1 set), called from
  `sub_8244AFB0+400`, from `sub_8244B040+4856`.
- The "hit" at the watchpoint's creation is an artifact of setting it, not
  a write.
- No further write, including during the teardown.

Read: the flag is a one-way latch. Once set, it stays set, and the loop
never runs again. Finding 173 found no code that clears it. So this build
has no reset path for loading completion, which matches the one-shot
structure in Finding 173.

Open: whether a real console clears field+136 (for example at a scene
change in a different function that this run never reaches), or whether
the real game would never set it until quit. Neither is visible here.

Finding 175: static search for other writers of canonical field+136. Found
none that clear it.

Method: every generated function that loads the canonical object pointer
(`lis -32145` + `-20220`, guest 0x826EB104) and stores to offset 136 from a
register. Two functions matched:
- `sub_82451408` (ppc_recomp.58.cpp:4927): the 0 -> 1 setter (Finding 167).
- `sub_82550850` (ppc_recomp.77.cpp:9450): a false match. The store is to a
  stack local (`ctx.r1.u32 + 136`), not the object.

So within functions that load the canonical pointer directly, the only store
to field+136 is the setter. No clear.

Limit: a function can hold the object through another register (copied from
r3 or a field) and never load the global itself. This search would miss
such a writer. Not checked.

Read: the latch has no clear path reachable through the canonical pointer
as loaded in these functions. Finding 173 and 174 stand: the game as built
does not continue after loading. The missing clear, if any, is in code that
either holds the object through another pointer or is never reached in this
build.

Finding 176: broadened the offset-136 search to any non-stack base register.
The count is too large to check by hand, and runtime evidence already covers
this case.

- Static: 305 generated functions store to offset 136 through a non-stack
  register (371 stores). Most are for unrelated objects, since offset 136 is
  common.
- Runtime: the watchpoint on the canonical object's field+136 (Finding 174)
  is pointer-independent. It catches every write to that memory, however the
  code reached it. It showed only the setter, and no clear, in a run through
  teardown.

So the broader static search cannot find a clear that the run would have
caught. It could only find writers in code this run never reaches. To narrow
the 305 to useful candidates, the next step is call-graph reachability from
the frame loop (`sub_8244AFB0`) and the post-loop code, which is a larger
analysis, not done here.

Finding 177: call-graph reachability and execution check for the offset-136
writers.

Static call graph (direct `sub_...(ctx, base)` calls, generated code):
- 3455 functions reachable from `sub_8244AFB0` (frame loop) and
  `sub_8244B040` (per-tick, including teardown).
- Of the 305 functions that store to offset 136 from a non-stack base
  (Finding 176), 44 are in that reachable set.
- Limit: indirect calls (`bctrl`) are not in the graph, so reachability is a
  lower bound on what can run.

Runtime execution check (lldb, auto-continue breakpoint on each of the 44,
10 s windowed run, the same run as Finding 174):
- Executed: `sub_82451408` (15 hits; the setter), `sub_82125BD8` (633),
  `sub_821298C0` (1), `sub_8244D268` (1), `sub_824A78E0` (1), `sub_8257D5E0`
  (1).
- Not executed in this run: the other 38.
- Canonical field+136 was written only by the setter (Finding 174), so none of
  the six executed functions clears it in this run.

Result: no clear of canonical field+136 among the executed candidates. The 38
that never ran are the only remaining static candidates, and they can only be
checked by a run that reaches them, which this build does not do.
